ABSTRACT

Curcumin is a naturally occurring polyphenolic compound with anti‐inflammatory, antioxidant, antiapoptotic, and anticancer properties. Numerous studies have demonstrated that curcumin can regulate multiple molecular targets, including signaling pathways such as Toll‐like receptor 4 (TLR4)/myeloid differentiation primary response 88 (MyD88)/nuclear factor kappa B (NF‐κB), Janus kinase (JAK)/signal transducer and activator of transcription (STAT), PTEN‐induced kinase 1 (PINK1)/Parkin, Parkin, phosphoinositide 3‐kinase (PI3K)/protein kinase B (Akt), mitogen‐activated protein kinase (MAPK), and nuclear factor erythroid 2‐related factor 2 (Nrf2)/antioxidant response element (ARE). Notably, curcumin exhibits therapeutic potential in a variety of diseases, including neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory diseases, and autoimmune diseases. Its mechanisms of action are associated with the suppression of inflammation, regulation of mitochondrial dysfunction, inhibition of apoptosis, attenuation of oxidative stress, and enhancement of neuroplasticity. However, the extremely low oral bioavailability of curcumin limits its application in disease therapy. This review systematically summarizes recent advances in curcumin research, including its pharmacological properties, molecular mechanisms of action, and nanodelivery systems, such as nanoparticles, liposomes, nanoemulsions, polymeric micelles, and exosomes, which have been developed to enhance curcumin bioavailability. Furthermore, this review provides an in‐depth overview of the therapeutic benefits of curcumin in neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory diseases, and autoimmune diseases. It focuses on evidence from in vitro studies, in vivo experiments, and potential clinical applications, while also discusses the therapeutic potential, current challenges, and future perspectives of curcumin in disease management.

Curcumin, a naturally occurring polyphenol, exhibits significant anti‐inflammatory and antioxidant capabilities. This review elucidates the specific molecular mechanisms by which curcumin exerts therapeutic effects on various diseases, including neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancers, inflammatory disorders, and autoimmune diseases, and outlines multiple strategies to improve its bioavailability. By compiling the therapeutic advantages of curcumin across these diverse diseases, we highlight its broad pharmacological potential, substantiated by in vitro, in vivo, and clinical studies, with the goal of offering evidence for curcumin's potential as a novel therapy for these diseases.

1 Introduction

Turmeric, a key member of the ginger family, originated in India and is widely used in culinary applications, traditional medicine, and dye production [1]. Historical records indicate that the use of turmeric in Indian Vedic culture dates to approximately 2500 BC. In Ayurvedic medicine, turmeric is valued for its diverse health benefits, including wound healing and the treatment of inflammatory conditions, skin diseases, and digestive disorders [2, 3]. Curcumin, a natural polyphenolic compound extracted from the turmeric rhizome, is composed of curcumin, demethoxycurcumin, and bisdemethoxycurcumin (BDMC) [4]. Owing to its diverse pharmacological properties—including anti‐inflammatory [5], antioxidant, and neuroprotective effects—curcumin has garnered significant attention in medical research [6, 7]. However, despite this therapeutic potential, factors such as low water solubility, poor stability, and rapid metabolic degradation result in low oral bioavailability, which hinders its development and clinical application [8]. Consequently, current research efforts are directed toward the development of novel drug delivery systems, including lipid nanoparticle formulations, lipid carriers, nanoemulsions (NEs), polymeric micelles, and exosomes, among others [9, 10, 11], to enhance the pharmacokinetic profiles and efficacy of curcumin.

Curcumin, the primary bioactive component of turmeric, modulates multiple signaling pathways—such as NF‐κB (nuclear factor kappaB), mitogen‐activated protein kinase (MAPK), Wnt/β‐catenin, and the phosphatidylinositol 3‐kinase/protein kinase B (PI3K/AKT) cascade—which play essential roles in inflammatory responses, apoptosis, and oxidative stress [12, 13, 14, 15, 16]. These signaling pathways significantly contribute to the pathogenesis of neurological disorders [17], cancer [18], cardiovascular diseases [19], inflammatory conditions [20], and autoimmune diseases [21]. In addition, sustained microglial activation mediated by pattern recognition receptors, including Toll‐like receptors (TLRs) and the NLRP3 inflammasome, can induce neuronal injury through the release of inflammatory mediators and oxidative stress [22]. At present, research on curcumin has primarily focused on elucidating its therapeutic targets across a variety of diseases [23]. Although the interactions of curcumin with various molecular targets have been extensively reported, a comprehensive synthesis of the specific mechanisms underlying its therapeutic effects in diverse diseases is still lacking. A deeper understanding of these mechanisms is crucial for informing the development of curcumin‐based therapeutic strategies and their eventual clinical application.

In summary, curcumin has attracted significant research interest owing to its long history in traditional medicine and its substantial therapeutic potential in biomedical research. Its structural versatility, broad pharmacological activities, and low bioavailability constitute key avenues for future investigation. With ongoing elucidation of its molecular mechanisms and continued advancements in drug delivery systems, curcumin is expected to become a core therapeutic agent for the treatment of various diseases, including neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. This review provides a comprehensive synthesis of the specific mechanisms underlying the modulatory effects of curcumin on these conditions. Furthermore, strategies for enhancing curcumin bioavailability—focusing on novel nanoparticle formulations—are evaluated, while curcumin treatment modalities and clinical efficacy are also assessed. Ultimately, this review seeks to establish a solid theoretical foundation for curcumin research as well as to facilitate the development of novel treatments for diseases, as illustrated in graphic abstract.

2 Pharmacological Properties of Curcumin

Curcumin is a hydrophobic polyphenolic compound abundantly present in the rhizome of turmeric, comprising between 5 and 6% of its content. Turmeric is primarily distributed across Southeast Asia, particularly in India, where its rhizome is utilized as a food additive and spice due to its yellow pigment and aromatic volatile oils [24]. Curcumin exhibits a wide range of pharmacological properties, including anti‐inflammatory, antioxidant, antirheumatic, antitumor, antifibrotic, antidiabetic, antiobesity, cardioprotective, antithrombotic, lipid‐modulating, and antiviral effects [24, 25, 26]. Research has shown that curcumin exerts ameliorative effects on neurological diseases via the targeting of multiple signaling pathways [27]. Additionally, clinical studies have demonstrated that curcumin is nontoxic to humans at doses of up to 8 g per day over 3 months, indicating that it is safe even at high dosages [28].

Despite the significant safety and therapeutic potential of curcumin in various diseases, its application is limited by low bioavailability [29]. Pharmacokinetic studies have reported that the oral bioavailability of curcumin is only approximately 1% [30], primarily attributable to its low aqueous solubility, poor absorption, and rapid metabolic transformation [24, 31]. Notably, curcumin is prone to degradation under alkaline conditions, decomposing within 30 min at pH > 7. Its degradation occurs more slowly under acidic conditions, with approximately 20% being decomposed within 1 h [32]. Furthermore, most orally administered curcumin is rapidly metabolized in the liver and plasma, with a large proportion being excreted in its unchanged form [33, 34].

Over recent years, novel approaches have been explored to enhance the bioavailability and biocompatibility of curcumin in vivo, including the use of liposomes, micelles, and advanced nanoparticle formulations. These strategies have aimed at overcoming challenges associated with poor solubility, inadequate absorption, and rapid metabolism. For instance, Yang et al. [35] reported that nanoparticles derived from curcumin–bovine serum albumin (BSA) exhibit sustained release characteristics, thereby prolonging drug half‐life and reducing dosing frequency, while simultaneously increasing blood–brain barrier permeating ability. These nanoparticles exert anti‐inflammatory and neuroprotective effects by inhibiting macrophage M1 polarization and blocking inflammatory signaling pathways [35].

In another study, the curcumin NE exerted cardioprotective effects through inhibition of angiotensin‐converting enzyme (ACE) activity and pronounced antioxidant activity [36]. To prevent curcumin degradation in the gastric environment, a colloidal delivery system loaded with curcumin was developed, which reduced its release in the stomach, enabling it to reach the intestine intact and exert therapeutic effects at inflammatory sites [37]. Lin et al. [38] prepared curcumin‐loaded cationic liposomes and observed that these liposomal formulations inhibited tumor angiogenesis. Yeh et al. [39] investigated the therapeutic potential of curcumin in osteoarthritis by encapsulating curcumin within liposomes with stable particle characteristics. The results demonstrated that curcumin effectively reduced macrophage differentiation, removed mineral deposits in chondrocytes, and decreased the production of proinflammatory cytokines.

Studies indicate that curcumin possesses the potential to prevent and treat neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases through its anti‐inflammatory, antioxidant, and antiapoptotic properties, as well as by enhancing neuroplasticity [7, 40, 41, 42, 43]. Curcumin exerts neuroprotective effects by downregulating the expression of proinflammatory cytokines (e.g., IL‐1β, IL‐6, TNF‐α) and the transcription factor NF‐κB [43]. One study reported the protective effects of curcumin in apolipoprotein E‐deficient mice, showing that curcumin attenuated the progression of aortic aneurysms in atherosclerotic mice, reduced macrophage infiltration and the production of proinflammatory mediators, and inhibited the activation of the extracellular signal‐regulated kinase (ERK) signaling pathway, while simultaneously increasing superoxide dismutase (SOD) levels [44]. Recognized as a potent natural antioxidant, curcumin significantly increases glutathione (GSH) levels and reduces the production of reactive oxygen species (ROS), which effectively alleviates neuronal cytotoxicity [45]. Neural cell apoptosis is a primary mechanism underlying degeneration following brain injury. Notably, studies have indicated that curcumin exerts strong antiapoptotic effects in the brain [46] by increasing the expression of BCL‐2 and reducing that of BAX and Caspase‐3, thereby providing neuroprotection against ischemic damage. Importantly, this mechanism has been replicated in the N2a cell model of ischemic stroke [47]. Furthermore, curcumin treatment was found to induce a concentration‐dependent downregulation of antiapoptotic proteins and upregulation of proapoptotic proteins. These findings suggest that curcumin represents a novel therapeutic approach for inhibiting synovial fibroblast proliferation in rheumatoid arthritis (RA) [48]. Studies have also reported that enhanced neuroplasticity can promote recovery from neurological disorders [17]. Increased levels of brain‐derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) contribute to the enhancement of neuroplasticity, modulating the expression of plasticity‐related genes, repairing neuronal damage, and alleviating depressive symptoms [42]. In the following section, we summarize the potential mechanisms by which curcumin exerts its therapeutic effects on neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases.

3 The Mechanism Underlying the Effects of Curcumin

Curcumin is a polyphenolic compound extracted from the rhizome of curcuma longa (turmeric) and exhibits a wide range of pharmacological activities, including anti‐inflammatory, antioxidant, antiapoptotic, and neuroprotective effects. In recent years, curcumin has been extensively investigated, and numerous studies have demonstrated its therapeutic potential in various pathological conditions, including neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory diseases, and autoimmune diseases. Current evidence indicates that curcumin exerts its therapeutic effects through multiple molecular mechanisms, including the suppression of inflammatory responses, amelioration of mitochondrial dysfunction, modulation of signaling pathways, such as PI3K/Akt, MAPK, and Nrf2/ARE, and promotion of neuroplasticity. Therefore, as a naturally derived bioactive compound with multitarget properties, curcumin holds considerable potential as a promising candidate for the development of therapeutic agents against various diseases. Based on recent advances in this field, this section provides an overview of the major molecular mechanisms underlying the biological effects of curcumin.

3.1 The Mechanism by Which Curcumin Exerts its Anti‐Inflammatory Effects

Inflammation is a complex physiological and pathological process, typically serving as an adaptive response triggered by harmful stimuli such as infection and tissue injury to maintain organismal homeostasis. Its severity is largely dependent on the nature of the stimulus or injury, disease stage, and duration, and it may lead to immune cell recruitment, tissue damage, and even cell death. Acute inflammation promotes tissue repair and pathogen clearance, whereas chronic inflammation drives disease progression through a feed‐forward loop involving damage‐associated molecular patterns and sustained immune activation [49]. These inflammatory states are characterized by the excessive production of proinflammatory cytokines (such as IL‐1β, IL‐6, and TNF‐α), chemokines, and ROS [50, 51, 52]. Notably, the NLRP3 inflammasome is a pivotal molecular complex that amplifies cascade responses by promoting the maturation of IL‐1β and IL‐18 and inducing pyroptosis, serving as a primary and synergistic driver in a variety of chronic inflammation‐associated diseases. Dysregulated activation of NLRP3 contributes to the progression of conditions such as RA, neurological disorders, and atherosclerosis [53].

Due to its multitarget actions and favorable safety profile, curcumin is considered a promising agent for the modulation of inflammation [54]. Curcumin has been shown to exert immunomodulatory effects on macrophages, inhibiting M1 polarization while promoting the anti‐inflammatory M2 phenotype. These effects occur primarily through the inhibition of the NF‐κB/NLRP3 and JAK/STAT signaling pathways, alongside the downregulation of proinflammatory cytokines (such as IL‐6 and TNF‐α) and the upregulation of anti‐inflammatory mediators, such as TGF‐β and IL‐10 [54]. Collectively, these findings underscore the therapeutic potential of curcumin in alleviating inflammation through immunomodulatory mechanisms (Figure 1).

FIGURE 1 Anti‐inflammatory mechanisms of curcumin. Lipopolysaccharide (LPS) is recognized by Toll‐like receptor 4 (TLR4), leading to the recruitment of myeloid differentiation primary response 88 (MyD88) and TIRAP and the subsequent activation of the MyD88‐dependent signaling pathway. MyD88 activation promotes the phosphorylation of IRAK, which, in turn, activates IKKα and triggers NF‐κB signaling. Curcumin inhibits the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB signaling pathway, thereby modulating inflammatory responses, suppressing immune cell activation and the production of proinflammatory factors under various diseases conditions, and consequently exerting anti‐inflammatory and tissue‐protective effects. Under LPS stimulation, the JAK2/STAT3 signaling pathway is upregulated, resulting in the increased secretion of proinflammatory mediators. Curcumin modulating inflammatory signaling by upregulating the protein expression of suppressor of cytokine signaling 1 (SOCS‐1), which inhibits the phosphorylation of JAK2/STAT3.

3.1.1 TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB Signaling Pathway

The TLR family consists of transmembrane receptors that detect the presence of pathogens by recognizing PAMPs or DAMPs, subsequently activating innate immune responses to defend the host [55]. Upon activation, TLRs induce the release of cytokines and initiate intracellular stress responses [56]. In mammals, TLRs also induce the expression of effector molecules, including inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS) and antimicrobial peptides, that kill microbial pathogens [57]. NF‐κB acts as an effector transcription factor for TLR4. Notably, NF‐κB is a key modulator of inflammatory responses in neurons and microglia, an effect mediated via the upregulation of proinflammatory cytokine expression. Upon binding to lipopolysaccharide (LPS), TLR4 recruits adaptor proteins, such as myeloid differentiation factor 88 (MyD88) and TIR domain‐containing adaptor protein (TIRAP), thereby activating the MyD88‐dependent pathway [58]. This activation leads to the phosphorylation of interleukin‐1 receptor‐associated kinase (IRAK), which subsequently activates the IκB kinase (IKK) complex. NF‐κB, maintained in an inactive state through its interaction with IκB, is released following the IKK‐mediated phosphorylation of IκB. This process, which can be stimulated by macrophages and inflammatory cytokines, enables NF‐κB to drive the production of proinflammatory factors [59].

Neurological injury is often accompanied by microglia‐mediated inflammatory responses, with the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB signaling pathway playing a vital role in this process. The activation of TLRs can promote the production of cytokines, adhesion molecules, and chemokines [60]. The absence of TLR4, which is predominantly expressed in microglia, can induce a shift in the polarization of these cells from the M1 to the M2 phenotype, facilitating their migration and subsequently alleviating the progression of neuroinflammation while simultaneously improving neurological function [61]. Zhang et al. treated microglia with curcumin to evaluate its anti‐inflammatory effects and found that it inhibits the TLR4/NF‐κB signaling pathway. This inhibition reduced the levels of proinflammatory factors (e.g., iNOS, IL‐6, IL‐1β), while upregulating those of anti‐inflammatory mediators (e.g., arginase 1 [Arg‐1], IL‐4, IL‐10, CD206), thus modulating microglial polarization and exerting neuroprotective effects [14]. Moreover, administering curcumin via intraperitoneal injection inhibits the neuroinflammatory cascade, thereby alleviating neuronal damage and behavioral deficits, an effect mediated through the attenuation of TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB signaling pathway activation [62]. In models of cerebral ischemia, the activation of the NLRP3 inflammasome triggers the cleavage of Caspase‐1, which, in turn, cleaves gasdermin D (GSDMD), pro‐IL‐1β, and pro‐IL‐18, generating the respective active forms (GSDMD‐N, IL‐1β, and IL‐18). In a mouse model of middle cerebral artery occlusion (MCAO), curcumin treatment alleviated postischemic white matter damage and improved sensorimotor dysfunction through the inhibition of the NLRP3/NF‐κB signaling pathway [63]. While neuronal integrity and neurological function are compromised in neurological disorders, neuroinflammation further exacerbates their progression. Studies involving TLR4 knockout mice indicated that curcumin administration following neural injury promotes a microglial phenotypic shift toward the M2 state, thus reducing neuroinflammation, likely via the inhibition of the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB signaling pathway. These findings highlight the potential of curcumin as a therapeutic agent for alleviating neuroinflammatory responses in neurological disorders [52].

Coronary microembolization (CME)‐induced local myocardial inflammation is one of the major contributors to myocardial injury and subsequent progressive cardiac dysfunction. Following CME, TLR4 activates the NF‐κB signaling pathway via MyD88, promoting the release of inflammatory mediators such as TNF‐α and IL‐1β, ultimately resulting in cardiomyocyte injury and apoptosis [19]. In this context, previous studies have reported that inhibitors of the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB signaling pathway exert protective effects against CME‐induced myocardial injury in vivo [64]. Liu et al. [65] found in a rat CME model that CME significantly elevated serum cardiac Troponin I levels, increased inflammatory cytokine expression, and enhanced the myocardial cell apoptosis index, leading to marked deterioration of cardiac function. Curcumin attenuated myocardial inflammatory responses and exerted cardioprotective effects by inhibiting the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB signaling pathway. It has also been reported that TLR4/MyD88 expression is markedly upregulated in cancer and contributes to tumor metastasis; and curcumin treatment significantly suppresses cancer cell proliferation and migration, potentially through inhibition of the TLR4/MyD88 signaling pathway [66]. In a mouse model of ulcerative colitis (UC), curcumin alleviated disease symptoms, reduced inflammation, and restored mucosal barrier function by upregulating ZO‐1 and Claudin expression. Its anti‐inflammatory effects were mediated through the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB pathway, accompanied by suppression of proinflammatory cytokines and macrophage activation [67]. Another study investigated the protective effects of curcumin in sodium monoiodoacetate‐induced osteoarthritis, and the results demonstrated that curcumin reduced the expression of proinflammatory factors and alleviated joint swelling and cartilage damage by inhibiting the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB signaling pathway [68].

Collectively, these data establish the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB signaling pathway as a key regulator of inflammation, playing an essential role in ameliorating neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases.

3.1.2 JAK/STAT Signaling Pathway

The JAK/STAT pathway is a major mediator of cytokine signaling, playing a critical role in initiating innate immunity, coordinating adaptive immune processes, and ultimately mediating inflammatory and immune responses [69]. This pathway is frequently activated in neurological conditions, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases [70]. Diverse cytokines associated with autoimmune inflammation, such as IL‐6, IL‐10, and IFN‐γ, transduce intracellular signals through the JAK/STAT pathway [71]. Notably, the levels of IFN‐γ and IL‐6, the most potent activators of this pathway, are elevated in inflammatory diseases [70].

In a rat model of PD induced by alpha‐synuclein (α‐Syn), inhibition of the JAK/STAT pathway suppresses microglial activation and the production of proinflammatory cytokines and chemokines, thereby reducing neuroinflammation [72]. Research has shown that enhancing the expression of triggering receptor expressed on myeloid cells 2 effectively suppresses the phosphorylation of JAK2 and STAT3. This promotes the polarization of microglia from the M1 to the M2 phenotype and decreases the synthesis and release of inflammatory cytokines [73], thereby alleviating cognitive deficits in mice [74]. Additionally, under LPS stimulation, the downregulation of the JAK/STAT signaling pathway significantly reduces the secretion of proinflammatory mediators from microglia [75]. Suppressor of cytokine signaling 1 (SOCS‐1) acts as a negative regulator of STAT3, inhibiting IL‐6 signaling and interfering with the migration of immune cells in the CNS [76]. Curcumin exerts anti‐inflammatory effects via the modulation of the JAK/STAT signaling pathway. Under LPS stimulation, JAK2/STAT3 signaling in microglia rapidly increases, whereas curcumin treatment inhibits this pathway by upregulating SOCS‐1 protein levels, which enhances IL‐10 and IL‐4 expression in microglia. These changes indicate that curcumin mediates anti‐inflammatory activity through SOCS‐1, which inhibits JAK2/STAT3 phosphorylation, thereby alleviating neural damage [77]. A different study demonstrated that curcumin inhibits the JAK2/STAT3 signaling pathway, reducing inflammation following cerebral ischemia and providing neuroprotective effects. Ham et al. [78] found that cancer‐associated fibroblasts (CAFs) can secrete cytokines such as IL‐6 and CCL2 to activate the JAK2/STAT3 signaling pathway in gastric cancer cells, thereby enhancing resistance to 5‐fluorouracil (FU) chemotherapy. Following curcumin treatment, phosphorylation of JAK2/STAT3 and the downstream expression of the antiapoptotic protein BCL‐2 were significantly inhibited, which in turn increased the chemosensitivity of gastric cancer cells and attenuated CAF‐induced chemoresistance. Studies have shown that in an ovarian cancer rat model, curcumin treatment significantly reduced IL‐6 levels and inhibited JAK2 expression as well as STAT3 phosphorylation, thereby suppressing tumor cell proliferation and promoting apoptosis [79]. Zhao et al. [80] investigated the mechanism of curcumin in a mouse model of colitis by examining JAK2/STAT3/SOCS signaling‐mediated dendritic cell (DC) activation. The results demonstrated that curcumin restored immune homeostasis and effectively ameliorated colitis by modulating the JAK2/STAT3/SOCS pathway and inhibiting DC activation. In addition, curcumin significantly inhibited the proliferation, migration, and invasion of human osteosarcoma cells in a dose‐dependent manner. It induced G0/G1 cell cycle arrest and apoptosis through suppression of the JAK2/STAT3 pathway, thereby inhibiting tumor growth [81]. It has also been reported that oncostatin M induces the expression of MMP‐1 and MMP‐3 in chondrocytes via activation of the JAK/STAT signaling pathway. Curcumin treatment markedly inhibited STAT phosphorylation and blocked JAK/STAT signal transduction, leading to the downregulation of MMP gene expression [82].

These findings suggest that curcumin exerts anti‐inflammatory and chondroprotective effects by inhibiting the JAK/STAT signaling pathway [83].

3.2 Curcumin Modulates Mitochondrial Dysfunction

Mitochondria, double‐membrane organelles present in all eukaryotic cells, use electrochemical energy from reducing equivalents for the production of adenosine triphosphate (ATP) [84]. These organelles are responsible for providing energy in the form of ATP for diverse cellular processes. Disruption or damage to mitochondria can lead to alterations in cellular bioenergetics, redox balance, and mitochondrial dynamics. Furthermore, mitochondria participate in ROS metabolism, calcium signaling, and apoptosis [85], playing a critical role in regulating synaptic plasticity, cancer cell proliferation, cardiomyocyte homeostasis, and inflammatory responses. Consequently, mitochondrial dysfunction is recognized as a major contributor to the pathogenesis of multiple diseases [86]. Specifically, changes in mitochondrial function activate several neuropathological processes, including the dysregulation of Ca2+ homeostasis, oxidative stress, mitophagy, mitochondrial DNA mutations, and apoptosis [87]. Despite the varying initial causes of these signals, the resulting tissue injury and disease initiation and progression generally involve similar underlying mechanisms. This mechanistic overlap emphasizes the importance of these mechanisms as common targets for intervention strategies [88]. A comprehensive understanding of the processes underlying mitochondrial dysfunction allows the development of interventions capable of slowing the progression of neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases [89]. Curcumin acts on multiple targets, resulting in the mitigation of mitochondrial dysfunction and enhance mitochondrial function. It has been shown that curcumin reduces mitochondrial dysfunction and fission while enhancing fusion‐related processes, biogenesis, and the expression of synaptic proteins. Curcumin also protects mitochondrial structure and helps maintain mitochondrial function [90]. Jiang et al. demonstrated that curcumin significantly lowers intracellular ROS levels, thereby alleviating oxidative damage [91]. Below, we review the mechanisms underlying the dysregulation of Ca2+ homeostasis, mitophagy, and apoptosis, while assessing recent research advances regarding the impact of curcumin on these pathways (Figure 2).

FIGURE 2 Mechanisms by which curcumin modulates mitochondrial dysfunction. The accumulation of Aβ is a critical initiating factor in Alzheimer's disease (AD), leading to Ca2+ overload in mitochondria and the disruption of mitochondrial homeostasis, endoplasmic reticulum dysfunction, the inhibition of the electron transport chain complexes, synaptic dysfunction, the depletion of mitochondrial DNA, and the loss of mitochondrial membrane potential (∆Ψm). This cascade results in the release of cytochrome c and subsequent apoptosis. These events ultimately result in cytochrome c release and apoptosis. A similar mechanism also occurs in cancer, where it induces tumor cell apoptosis and inhibits tumor growth. Curcumin has been shown to attenuate apoptosis, alleviate mitochondrial dysfunction, and protect against neuronal injury, while also exerting cardioprotective effects. Increased oxidative stress contributes to mitochondrial dysfunction, further elevating reactive oxygen species (ROS) levels within mitochondria. Curcumin exerts its protective effects by modifying Keap1, facilitating the binding of nuclear factor erythroid 2‐related factor 2 (Nrf2) to antioxidant response elements (ARE), thereby inhibiting Nrf2 activation and upregulating the expression of heme oxygenase‐1 (HO‐1), glutathione peroxidase (GPx), NAD(P)H: quinone oxidoreductase 1 (NQO1), and glutathione (GSH), which mitigates oxidative damage. Additionally, Parkin, in conjunction with PINK1, is involved in the clearance of damaged mitochondria through mitophagy, leading to mitochondrial dysfunction. Curcumin promotes Parkin/PINK1‐mediated mitophagy. In autoimmune diseases, curcumin promotes PINK1/Parkin‐mediated mitophagy, upregulates Beclin‐1, p62, and LC3B expression, enhances the removal of damaged mitochondria, inhibits Caspase‐3 activation, restores mitochondrial membrane potential, and maintains cell survival, thereby protecting chondrocytes and alleviating inflammatory injury.

3.2.1 Dysregulation of Ca2+ Homeostasis

Ca2+ plays a critical role in numerous neuronal activities, such as the activation of signal transduction, the regulation of transcription, and the initiation of synaptic transmission. Ca2+ homeostasis at the plasma membrane is primarily attributed to the selective permeability properties of the plasma membrane, ATP‐dependent dynamic functions, and the sodium‐calcium exchanger system that actively pumps Ca2+ ions out of the cell [92, 93]. Additionally, the endoplasmic reticulum and mitochondria function as intracellular Ca2+ reservoirs, buffering cytosolic Ca2+ concentrations and maintaining them at low levels. However, when the integrity of the cell membrane or the function of ATP‐dependent Ca2+‐ATPase pumps/uniport systems is compromised, intracellular Ca2+ levels may rise. Persistent elevation of intracellular Ca2+ can disrupt endoplasmic reticulum function and induce neurotoxicity, manifested as mitotic arrest, the cessation of ATP synthesis, and excessive activation of calcium‐dependent hydrolases [94, 95]. Such mechanisms ultimately impair neuronal function and lead to neuronal cell death. This underscores the importance of maintaining intracellular Ca2+ homeostasis for neuronal survival. Neurons heavily rely on mitochondria for energy production and Ca2+ buffering, making them particularly susceptible to mitochondrial dysfunction. The accumulation of β‐amyloid (Aβ) constitutes a primary etiological event in Alzheimer's disease (AD), inducing mitochondrial Ca2+ overload and disrupting mitochondrial homeostasis [96]. One study indicated that the pathological activation of Ca2+‐dependent proteases and protein phosphatases may lead to neuronal damage in MS [97]. Notably, curcumin has been shown to modulate intracellular calcium release in SH‐SY5Y neuronal cells, which was associated with improved neuronal survival rates [98]. In addition, studies have shown that mitochondrial dysfunction is a critical event in the development of cardiotoxicity. Curcumin can effectively preserve mitochondrial membrane integrity and restore mitochondrial function, thereby alleviating cardiomyocyte injury and exerting cardioprotective effects [99]. Zheng et al. [100] found that curcumin promoted calcium release from the endoplasmic reticulum into the cytoplasm while inhibited calcium efflux, leading to a decrease in mitochondrial membrane potential (MMP), depletion of ATP, release of cytochrome c, and activation of Caspase‐3. Ultimately, these events promote tumor cell apoptosis and suppress tumor growth.

3.2.2 PINK1/Parkin Signaling Pathway

PTEN‐induced kinase 1 (PINK1) and the E3 ubiquitin–protein ligase Parkin are critical factors in the regulation of mitophagy and mitochondrial biogenesis [101]. Under physiological conditions, PINK1 is imported into mitochondria and is subsequently degraded through proteolysis. Following mitochondrial depolarization and the loss of membrane potential, PINK1 rapidly accumulates on the outer mitochondrial membrane (OMM), where it recruits Parkin, leading to the initiation of mitophagy [102, 103]. Research has shown that PINK1/Parkin interact with mitochondrial genes, a process associated with the regulation of mitochondrial biogenesis and the maintenance of homeostasis [104, 105]. Following mitochondrial damage, PINK1 undergoes rapid autophosphorylation and recruits Parkin; the subsequent interaction with P62 and LC3 facilitates the tethering of damaged mitochondria to autophagosomes, ensuring the lysosomal degradation of impaired organelles by proteolytic enzymes. In patients with AD, mitochondrial function is impaired, while the levels of PINK1 are significantly reduced, underscoring the fundamental role of this protein in AD pathogenesis [106]. Moreover, in Aβ‐induced mouse models of AD, PINK1 gene deletion promotes mitochondrial fission and fusion, which exacerbates mitochondrial dysfunction. In vitro studies have shown that PINK1‐knockdown PC12 cells exhibit increased vulnerability to Aβ‐induced cytotoxicity, suggesting that PINK1/Parkin pathway activity is essential for the preservation of neuronal viability [107]. Studies have shown that curcumin facilitates PINK1/Parkin‐mediated mitophagy, thereby preserving mitochondrial function [108]. In zebrafish exposed to 6‐hydroxydopamine (6‐OHDA) to induce neurotoxicity, curcumin treatment reduced the expression of dynamin‐related protein 1 (DRP1) while increasing that of Mitofusin 2, thus improving mitochondrial dynamics. Additionally, curcumin upregulated the PINK1/Parkin pathway, thereby contributing to the maintenance of mitochondrial homeostasis, effectively exerting neuroprotective effects [109]. Recent studies have reported that curcumin provides protection for osteoarthritis by activating PINK1/Parkin‐mediated mitophagy, thereby upregulating mitophagy‐related markers including Beclin‐1, p62, and LC3B. This process facilitates the clearance of damaged mitochondria, preserves mitochondrial integrity, and exerts protective effects on cartilage tissue [110]. Cao et al. [111] demonstrated that curcumin alleviates hydrogen peroxide‐induced oxidative stress, intestinal epithelial barrier injury, and mitochondrial damage in a mitophagy‐dependent manner via the PINK1/Parkin signaling pathway. These findings elucidate a potential molecular mechanism by which curcumin mitigates oxidative stress‐induced mitochondrial dysfunction.

3.2.3 Apoptosis

Mitochondria participate in apoptosis through various pathways. In vertebrates, the loss of selective permeability in the OMM—precipitated by proapoptotic BCL‐2 family activity and concurrent organelle fission—results in mitochondrial outer membrane permeabilization (MOMP) [112]. This allows the efflux of intermembrane space proteins, such as cytochrome c, into the cytosol, thereby promoting caspase activation. Recent studies have found that mitochondrial fission and apoptosis are closely linked. During the apoptotic process, mitochondria undergo extensive fragmentation driven by increased recruitment of DRP1, a process crucial for its positive regulatory role in BAX/BAK‐mediated MOMP [113, 114]. Although the proapoptotic function of DRP1 is distinct from its mitochondrial structure regulatory activity, the resulting mitochondrial fragmentation likely also influences the permeability of the OMM [115]. Conversely, mitochondrial fusion is associated with the protection of cells from apoptotic death, whereas the activation of apoptosis results in the inhibition of fusion activity [116]. The regulatory network comprising BCL‐2 and proteins associated with mitochondrial dynamics may contribute to the pathogenesis of neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. Specifically, the core components of programmed cell death include the caspase cascade, the assembly of apoptotic protease activating factor‐1 (Apaf‐1) complexes, and the heterodimerization of BCL‐2 family proteins. Additionally, mitochondria function as a central node in apoptosis through the release of apoptosis‐inducing factors, such as cytochrome c and apoptosis‐inducing factor [117, 118]. In this cascade, the translocation of cytochrome c to the cytosol induces the formation of the Apaf‐1 complex, resulting in the activation of Caspase‐9 and, subsequently, other caspases, ultimately leading to apoptosis.

In vitro cultures of rat cortical neuronal cells treated with tert‐butyl hydroperoxide (t‐BHP) exhibit significant neuronal damage, characterized by the loss of MMP (ΔΨm), the efflux of cytochrome c from mitochondria into the cytosol, and the subsequent activation of Caspase‐3 and PARP cleavage, ultimately inducing apoptosis. However, curcumin intervention significantly reversed these pathological conditions, indicating that curcumin attenuates t‐BHP‐induced cortical neuronal apoptosis and mitigates mitochondrial dysfunction, resulting in neuroprotection [119]. Furthermore, Zhang et al. investigated the neuroprotective effects of curcumin in glutamate‐treated rat PC12 cells and found that curcumin reduced cytochrome c release, the BAX/BCL‐2 ratio, and Caspase‐3 activity, thereby alleviating cytotoxicity. These findings support that curcumin exerts an inhibitory effect on the apoptotic cascade [120]. A subsequent in vitro study found that Aβ impairs mitochondrial dynamics, reduces mitochondrial biogenesis, and diminishes both synaptic activity and mitochondrial function. Notably, pretreatment with curcumin promoted mitochondrial fusion while suppressing fission‐related processes, resulting in improved mitochondrial function and cell survival rates. These findings suggest that curcumin may prevent mitochondrial dysfunction and serve as a promising drug for the treatment of neurological disorders [121]. Mitochondria play a crucial role in maintaining myocardial tissue homeostasis. Mitochondrial dysfunction ultimately leads to cardiomyocyte death and further contributes to cardiovascular impairment. Sabet et al. [99] investigated the effects of bevacizumab on mitochondria and cardiomyocytes in isolated rat hearts and evaluated curcumin as a mitochondria‐ and cardio‐protective agent. The results demonstrated that curcumin significantly alleviated bevacizumab‐induced mitochondrial toxicity and inhibited apoptosis, suggesting that curcumin may help mitigate mitochondrial damage and cardiotoxicity. Zhang et al. [122] aimed to investigate the protective effects of curcumin in a rat model of cardiac arrest induced by ventricular fibrillation. Their findings showed that curcumin improved myocardial dysfunction caused by ischemia–reperfusion (I/R) injury and restored cardiopulmonary function. In addition, mitochondrial structure and energy metabolism were improved, with enhanced ATP production and suppression of mitochondrial apoptotic pathways, including downregulation of cytochrome c, Caspase‐3, and uncoupling protein 2. An in vitro study revealed that curcumin exerted a significant synergistic anticancer effect against malignant melanoma cells. Curcumin induced ROS production and mitochondrial dysfunction, ultimately leading to apoptosis and programmed necrosis. This process was accompanied by upregulation of proapoptotic proteins including Bax, p53, and Caspase‐3, as well as an increased Bax/Bcl‐2 ratio [123]. In addition, in an LPS‐induced chondrocyte model of osteoarthritis, curcumin treatment markedly restored MMP, upregulated Beclin‐1 and LC3‐II expression, inhibited Caspase‐3 activation, and modulated Bcl‐2 expression. These effects promoted the clearance of damaged mitochondria and suppressed apoptosis, thereby maintaining mitochondrial homeostasis and chondrocyte survival and exerting protective effects against osteoarthritis [124].

3.3 Curcumin Modulates the PI3K/AKT Signaling Pathway

The PI3K/AKT signaling pathway is a crucial intracellular signaling cascade involved in the transduction of signals from cytokines and the extracellular matrix (ECM) [125]. This pathway is essential for a wide range of cellular functions, including cell proliferation, adhesion, migration, invasion, metabolism, and survival [126, 127]. PI3K is present in numerous cell types and undergoes activation by receptor tyrosine kinases (RTKs), G protein‐coupled receptors (GPCRs), and Ras family proteins on the cell membrane [128]. Activated PI3K catalyzes the conversion of phosphatidylinositol 4,5‐bisphosphate (PI (4,5) P2​) into the second messenger phosphatidylinositol 3,4,5‐trisphosphate (PIP3), which subsequently binds to downstream targets [129, 130]. The generation of PIP3 is critical for the recruitment of AKT and its subsequent activation by RTKs.

AKT consists of three isoforms, each containing an N‐terminal Pleckstrin homology (PH) domain, a central kinase domain, and a C‐terminal regulatory domain. The isolated PH domains of all AKT subtypes bind PIP3 with similar affinity. This binding sequesters AKT at the membrane, enabling its phosphorylation at Threonine 308 (Thr308) by 3‐phosphoinositide‐dependent protein kinase 1 (PDK1), also recruited by PIP3, and at Serine 473 (Ser473) by mammalian target of rapamycin complex 2 (mTORC2), events that significantly increase AKT activity [131, 132, 133]. Phosphorylated AKT (p‐AKT) subsequently directly or indirectly activates key downstream effector molecules, such as mTOR, GSK‐3β, and actin‐related proteins, thereby impacting cell growth and survival. Concurrently, mTOR interacts directly with p70 ribosomal S6 kinase 1/2 (P70S6K1/2), eukaryotic translation initiation factor 4E‐binding proteins (4E‐BPs), and other proteins involved in translational regulation, initiating protein synthesis, transcription, cell growth, and proliferation [134].

The PI3K/AKT signaling pathway plays a significant role in the pathogenesis of neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases [135]. This pathway functions as a key regulator of neuronal survival and is capable of modulating both neurogenesis and synaptic plasticity [136]. Activation of PI3K/AKT pathway signaling promotes neuronal survival under conditions of hypoxia and in in vitro models of neuronal death [137]. Numerous studies have demonstrated that inhibition of the PI3K/AKT pathway exacerbates ischemic neuronal death, while its activation significantly improves outcomes in acute brain injury [138]. Furthermore, AKT is considered an important therapeutic target, promoting neuronal survival in systemic neurological diseases, especially those involving ischemia, where excitotoxicity serves as a major cause of neuronal death [139]. AKT suppresses excitotoxicity and proapoptotic signaling through the phosphorylation of substrates such as BAD, forkhead transcription factors, and proline‐rich AKT substrates, thereby exerting neuroprotective effects [140]. GSK‐3β, a direct substrate of AKT, participates in synaptogenesis and microglial activation; however, its hyperactivation results in excitotoxic neuronal injury [141]. Such overactivity facilitates synaptic abnormalities and promotes tau hyperphosphorylation, leading to the formation of neurofibrillary tangles, a pathological hallmark of AD [142]. Notably, p‐AKT phosphorylates GSK‐3β, inhibiting its activity and exerting neuroprotective effects.

Curcumin has been widely reported to exert beneficial effects in the treatment of neurological disorders. In the 1‐methyl‐4‐phenyl‐1,2,3,6‐tetrahydropyridine (MPTP)‐induced PD mouse model, curcumin protects dopaminergic neurons from damage through the activation of the PI3K/AKT pathway, resulting in neuroprotective effects [143]. Reports indicate that curcumin alleviates brain edema and reduces neuronal death following injury, improves neurological function, and increases both autophagy and AKT phosphorylation levels. Meanwhile, the PI3K inhibitor LY294002 was found to reverse the neuroprotective effects of curcumin after brain injury, suggesting that curcumin mediates its effects through the PI3K/AKT signaling pathway [144]. Similarly, in a rat model of traumatic brain injury, curcumin administration activated the PI3K/AKT pathway, which supported the recovery of neurological function [145]. Moreover, in APP/PS1 double transgenic mice, curcumin reduces Aβ generation, enhances Aβ clearance, and induces autophagy. In these mice, curcumin triggers autophagy via the inhibition of the PI3K/AKT/mTOR signaling pathway, effectively improving memory and cognitive functions [146]. The PI3K/Akt pathway has been shown to be involved in the development of cardiovascular diseases, where Akt regulates cardiovascular functions related to cardiac growth and survival, contractile performance, and coronary angiogenesis [147]. Chen et al. [148] established a mouse model of myocardial I/R injury and demonstrated that curcumin treatment improved cardiac function by increasing ejection fraction and fractional shortening, while reduced left ventricular end‐systolic diameter and left ventricular end‐systolic volume, thereby protecting the heart from I/R‐induced heart failure. This protective effect was primarily mediated through activation of the PI3K/Akt pathway and suppression of I/R‐induced autophagy and apoptosis in cardiomyocytes. Wei et al. [149] reported that curcumin attenuated high glucose–induced cardiomyocyte apoptosis by inhibiting NADPH oxidase–mediated oxidative stress. This cardioprotective effect was likely mediated by PI3K/Akt‐related signaling pathways. Curcumin acted on multiple aspects of cancer progression, including initiation, metastasis, angiogenesis, and tumor growth. The PI3K/Akt pathway was a key therapeutic target in cancer due to its role in tumor initiation, proliferation, and cell survival [150]. In one study, curcumin downregulated Akt and mTOR expression to inhibit the proliferation of non‐small cell lung cancer cells, thereby inducing two distinct forms of cell death, namely, apoptosis and autophagy [151]. In human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVECs), curcumin was found to inhibit the PI3K/Akt/mTOR signaling pathway, induce apoptosis, and suppress migration and angiogenesis in hepatocyte growth factor‐treated cells. Furthermore, in experimental mouse models, curcumin inhibited hepatocyte growth factor‐stimulated tumor growth, increased E‐cadherin expression, and decreased CD34 and vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) expression. Collectively, these findings indicated that curcumin suppresses epithelial–mesenchymal transition and angiogenesis by blocking the PI3K/Akt/mTOR pathway [152]. Additionally, curcumin alleviated joint inflammation in arthritic rats, inhibited synovial angiogenesis and abnormal fibroblast proliferation, and exerted its effects by suppressing the expression of proinflammatory cytokines TNF‐α and IL‐6, as well as inhibiting activation of the PI3K/Akt signaling pathway [153]. Hui et al. [154] investigated the ability of curcumin to alleviate systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) in two different mouse models. Their results showed that curcumin reduced neutrophil migration and modulated the PI3K/Akt signaling pathway to suppress inflammatory cytokine secretion, thereby improving SLE. This study provides a novel therapeutic target and strategy for the clinical application of curcumin.

Collectively, these findings suggest that curcumin alleviates various diseases through the modulation of the PI3K/AKT signaling pathway (Figure 3).

FIGURE 3 Mechanisms by which curcumin modulates the PI3K/AKT and MAPK pathways. Phosphoinositide 3‐kinase (PI3K) can be activated by protein tyrosine kinases and G‐protein‐coupled receptors (GPCRs). Once activated, PI3K converts phosphatidylinositol 4,5‐bisphosphate (PIP2) to phosphatidylinositol 3,4,5‐trisphosphate (PIP3), leading to the recruitment of AKT from the cytoplasm to the membrane, where it undergoes phosphorylation. Phosphorylated AKT activates mammalian target of rapamycin (mTOR) and glycogen synthase kinase‐3 beta (GSK‐3β). Curcumin alleviates cellular injury and inflammatory responses by inhibiting the PI3K/AKT signaling pathway and simultaneously modulating the MAPK signaling pathway. The binding of brain‐derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) to its receptor, TrkB, results in CREB phosphorylation. Curcumin enhances CREB phosphorylation, thereby promoting BDNF‐mediated transcriptional activity. Furthermore, curcumin activates the cAMP/protein kinase A (PKA)/CREB signaling pathway through the enhancement of 5‐HT1A receptor activity, which increases neurotransmitter release. Curcumin also influences the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway, promoting GSK‐3β‐mediated phosphorylation of β‐catenin, thereby contributing to neuronal repair.

3.4 Curcumin Modulates the MAPK Signaling Pathway

MAPKs are critical signaling molecules within eukaryotic signal transduction networks. This pathway responses to multiple diverse stimuli, linking extracellular signals with fundamental cellular signals, thereby coordinating cellular responses and intercellular communications [155]. Consequently, the regulation of MAPK activity has profound biological implications across numerous cellular processes [156], including neural differentiation, mitosis, and apoptosis. Besides transcription factors such as c‐Jun, c‐Fos, Elk‐1, and c‐Myc, MAPK substrates include membrane‐associated proteins and cytoplasmic proteins, such as kinases, cytoskeletal components, and phospholipase A2 [157]. The diversity of these target proteins indicates that MAPKs play a central role in coordinating both short‐ and long‐term cellular changes triggered by extracellular stimuli. Based on the specificity of the activating extracellular stimuli and amino acid sequence homology, MAPKs are primarily classified into three main categories: ERKs, c‐Jun N‐terminal kinases/stress‐activated protein kinases (JNK/SAPKs), and p38 MAPK [158]. Furthermore, hormones, growth factors, and cytokines accelerate MAPK phosphorylation and activation in resting cells.

Research indicates that the dysregulation of the MAPK signaling pathway is associated with the pathogenesis of neurological disorders, impacting key functions such as synaptic plasticity, learning, and memory. Elevated MAPK can lead to augmented tau hyperphosphorylation, Aβ accumulation, and memory impairments. Notably, the activity of MAPK phosphatase 1 (MKP‐1), which acts as an antagonistic regulator of MAPK via the dephosphorylation of its tyrosine and threonine residues, is downregulated in AD. The overexpression of MKP‐1 in transgenic AD mouse models has been shown to reduce Aβ production, prevent the development of neuroinflammatory plaques, diminish synaptic defects, and improve cognitive function. Zhu et al. demonstrated that inhibiting the p38 MAPK signaling pathway significantly enhances learning, memory, and motor abilities in rats following ischemic brain injury, thereby supporting neuronal recovery [159]. These findings suggest that modulating MAPK represents an effective strategy against neurological disorders. Curcumin has been shown to modulate the MAPK signaling pathway, establishing a molecular link between its neuroprotective properties and cellular activities related to neurological disorders. For example, curcumin has been found to mitigate brain injuries induced by chronic intermittent hypoxia, including brain edema and neuronal death, through the regulation of the p38 MAPK pathway [160]. The MAPK/ERK pathway, activated by glial cells during the progression of neurological disorders, participates in the transmission of cellular signals and plasticity changes, such as those related to learning and memory; these kinases serve as pivotal regulatory factors for cell proliferation and survival [161]. A separate study confirmed that curcumin reduces the release of proinflammatory cytokines, inhibits the MAPK/ERK signaling pathway, and suppresses microglial proliferation. These findings highlight the capacity of curcumin to modulate the MAPK/ERK pathway, which yields marked neuroprotective effects [162]. To enhance these effects, Huang et al. encapsulated curcumin in nanoparticles and demonstrated that curcumin reduces neuronal apoptosis through the modulation of the p38 MAPK pathway [163]. Similarly, studies have indicated that curcumin effectively alleviates neuronal damage, blood–brain barrier disruption, and brain edema by inhibiting the p38 MAPK signaling pathway, thereby improving neurological function [164]. Jeong et al. [165] established a rat model of myocardial I/R injury via ligation of the left anterior descending coronary artery. Curcumin was administered prior to ligation, and the results demonstrated that curcumin significantly reduced myocardial infarct size and effectively protected against myocardial injury by inhibiting the MAPK pathway, including p38 and JNK signaling. In another study, the effects of curcumin on human cardiac fibroblasts, fibroblast proliferation, cell cycle distribution, and collagen deposition were evaluated. The results showed that curcumin inhibited TGF‐β1‐induced proliferation, differentiation, and collagen synthesis in cardiac fibroblasts, potentially through suppression of the p38 MAPK signaling pathway [166]. Li et al. [167] investigated the effects of curcumin on thrombosis and platelet aggregation, and it was found that curcumin inhibited platelet aggregation and thrombus formation by suppressing the MAPK signaling pathway, reducing thromboxane A2 production and platelet granule release. These findings suggested potential applications in the prevention and treatment of atherosclerosis and thrombosis‐related cardiovascular diseases. Some studies have shown that curcumin promotes ROS production and upregulates p38 MAPK phosphorylation, thereby inducing apoptosis in chemotherapy‐resistant lung cancer cells and exerting antitumor effects [168]. In a study on head and neck cancer cells, curcumin activated AMP‐activated protein kinase α and p38 MAPK and upregulated peroxisome proliferator‐activated receptor gamma coactivator 1‐alpha (PGC‐1α), thereby suppressing cancer cell proliferation [169]. In a mouse model of colitis induced by dinitrobenzene sulfonic acid, curcumin inhibited activation of the p38 MAPK and NF‐κB signaling pathways and reduced IL‐1β levels, thereby alleviating intestinal inflammation [170]. In an asthma mouse model, curcumin modulated the activity of secretory phospholipase A2, reduced COX‐2 and prostaglandin D2 levels, and ultimately attenuated airway inflammation through regulation of p38 MAPK, ERK, and JNK signaling pathways [171]. Xu et al. demonstrated that curcumin significantly inhibited ERK and p38 phosphorylation as well as c‐Fos expression, thereby suppressing the MAPK/AP‐1 signaling pathway [172]. This led to reduced expression of matrix metalloproteinase‐9 (MMP‐9) and proinflammatory cytokines, resulting in alleviation of synovial inflammation, inhibition of cartilage and bone destruction, and delayed progression of RA.

In summary, these data suggest that curcumin may ameliorate various diseases through its modulatory effects on the MAPK pathway (Figure 3).

3.5 Curcumin Modulates the Nrf2/ARE Signaling Pathway

Oxidative stress, characterized by the excessive accumulation of ROS and reactive nitrogen species (RNS) within cells, plays a central role in the etiology of neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. Oxidative stress in the brain can compromise the integrity of the hippocampus and amygdala; notably, the dentate gyrus of the hippocampus regulates structural plasticity, regeneration/remodeling capacity, and neurogenesis [173]. Consequently, oxidative damage in the brain can weaken remodeling capabilities, alter structural plasticity, and affect cell proliferation and neurogenesis, collectively disrupting or destroying synaptic neurotransmission and neural circuits. Specifically, these complex oxidative stress‐induced changes in the brain can lead to the impairment of cognitive and motor functions and significantly hinder neuronal tissue regeneration, culminating in irreversible tissue damage. Thus, the regulation of oxidative stress represents an effective therapeutic strategy. Although a low level of ROS is continuously generated in cells, excessive ROS production induced by pathological stimuli or failure of antioxidant defense systems is a major cause of various vascular dysfunctions. Increasing evidence indicates that oxidative stress is a key driver of endothelial dysfunction. Moreover, oxidative stress may trigger vascular inflammation and injury by activating transcription factors, upregulating adhesion molecules, stimulating chemokine production, and promoting inflammatory cell recruitment [162, 174]. It has been reported that ROS facilitates signal transduction and promotes cancer cell proliferation, differentiation, migration, and invasion [175]. However, at high concentrations, ROS can instead impair cancer cell survival due to damage to DNA, lipids, and proteins. Previous studies have demonstrated that elevated ROS levels can induce apoptosis in multiple myeloma and colorectal cancer cells. Therefore, increasing intracellular ROS levels in cancer cells beyond a tolerable threshold to induce cell death has emerged as a promising anticancer therapeutic strategy [176, 177]. In recent years, increasing attention has been paid to the role of oxidative stress in inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. For example, in RA, oxidative stress not only promotes activation of inflammatory pathways but also contributes to joint tissue degeneration, thereby accelerating disease progression and exacerbating clinical symptoms [178, 179]. Activated immune cells, including neutrophils and macrophages, release large amounts of ROS in response to proinflammatory cytokines. In addition, enhanced ROS activity can activate MMPs and promote osteoclast differentiation, both of which accelerate bone resorption and joint tissue damage [180, 181]. Curcumin is recognized for its potent antioxidant properties, which function through dual mechanisms. On the one hand, its phenolic structure endows it with the ability to directly scavenge ROS and RNS, such as superoxide anions and hydroxyl radicals [182, 183]. On the other hand, curcumin enhances cellular tolerance to oxidative damage by activating intracellular signaling pathways, reducing lipid peroxidation, increasing GSH levels, and promoting protein synthesis [184]. This capacity of curcumin to alleviate oxidative stress emphasizes its therapeutic potential against cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases (Figure 2).

The Nrf2/ARE signaling pathway functions as a primary regulator of cellular defense against oxidative damage. Under conditions of oxidative stress, Nrf2 translocates to the nucleus and binds to the antioxidant response element (ARE). Acting as a transcription factor, Nrf2 regulates the expression of genes encoding antioxidants and detoxifying enzymes [185]. Studies suggest that curcumin facilitates Nrf2 binding to ARE by modifying the reactive thiol groups of cysteine in Kelch‐like ECH‐associated protein 1 (Keap1). Typically, the ubiquitination of Nrf2 by Keap1 and its subsequent proteasomal degradation prevent the activation of this pathway. Interaction with curcumin results in the modification of cysteine residues in Keap1, inducing conformational changes that render the inhibitory effect of Keap1 on Nrf2 ineffective [186]. Subsequently, the expression of genes encoding heme oxygenase‐1 (HO‐1), GSH peroxidase (GPx), and NAD(P)H quinone oxidoreductase 1 (NQO1) is upregulated, resulting in increased concentrations of antioxidant enzymes and GSH and the consequent mitigation of oxidative damage [187]. One study showed that curcumin reduces brain damage induced by MCAO in rats by upregulating Nrf2 and its downstream target gene HO‐1, providing new targets and a rationale for the treatment of cerebral ischemia [188]. Rajakrishnan et al. reported that curcumin significantly reverses lipid peroxidation and brain lipid modifications while increasing GSH levels, thus demonstrating neuroprotective effects in brain injury [189]. Additionally, curcumin was shown to activate the Nrf2/ARE pathway and upregulate the expression of antioxidant enzymes such as HO‐1 and GPx, which enhanced cellular antioxidant defenses [190]. A recent study reported that curcumin mitigates oxidative stress induced by traumatic brain injury, reducing the production of the lipid peroxidation marker malondialdehyde (MDA) and enhancing the activity of antioxidant enzymes such as SOD and GPx. Specifically, curcumin promotes the translocation of Nrf2 from the cytoplasm to the nucleus, consequently upregulating the expression of genes encoding HO‐1 and NQO‐1 and activating the Nrf2/ARE pathway. In one study, curcumin exerted therapeutic effects against prostate cancer by epigenetically activating Nrf2 transcription and subsequently triggering downstream antioxidant signaling pathways [191]. Zhang et al. [192] demonstrated that curcumin promoted nuclear translocation of Nrf2 via the AKT pathway and enhanced the expression of downstream antioxidant factors, including HO‐1 and glutamate–cysteine ligase catalytic subunit. These effects reduced ROS accumulation in diabetic cardiomyocytes, alleviated mitochondrial damage, and contributed to the treatment of diabetic cardiomyopathy. Another study showed that curcumin activated Nrf2 while concurrently inhibited the downstream cGAS–STING–NF‐κB signaling cascade, thereby effectively suppressing inflammatory activation, migration, and invasion of RA synovial fibroblasts and promoting apoptosis [193]. These findings contribute to the elucidation of how curcumin activates the Nrf2/ARE signaling pathway and identify the mechanism by which curcumin exerts antioxidant effects.

3.6 Curcumin Enhances Neural Plasticity

Neural plasticity is a key characteristic of the mammalian brain, defined as the inherent dynamic ability of the CNS to mature, respond to experiences, undergo structural and functional changes, and adapt following injury [194]. Notably, neural plasticity facilitates the development of neural circuits and the formation of learning and memory. Numerous studies have reported that the enhancement of neural plasticity promotes recovery from neurological disorders [195]. For instance, long‐term potentiation of synaptic transmission in the hippocampus results in the improvement of memory and cognitive function in AD patients [196]. Furthermore, increased levels of BDNF contribute to the enhancement of neural plasticity through the regulation of plasticity‐related genes, the mitigation of neuronal damage, and the reduction of depressive symptoms [197]. Research has demonstrated that curcumin treatment increases dendritic spine density in the hippocampus, a change that potentiates neural signal transmission and synaptic plasticity [198]. Overall, neural plasticity is involved in the pathogenesis of neurological disorders, and curcumin enhances neural plasticity by modulating key signaling pathways, providing a foundation for targeted therapies for these conditions (Figure 3).

3.6.1 BDNF/TrkB/CREB Signaling Pathway

BDNF represents an extensively studied neurotrophic factor in the nervous system and functions as a major regulator of neural plasticity. This factor maintains neuronal survival and regeneration through the regulation of dendritic spine morphology and adult neurogenesis in the hippocampus [199]. BDNF activity involves binding to and activating the tropomyosin receptor kinase B (TrkB), an interaction that governs cellular processes essential for the development and maintenance of brain function [200]. The neuroprotective effects of BDNF are primarily mediated through the regulation of the BDNF/TrkB signaling pathway [201]. BDNF modulates neural plasticity by regulating multiple intracellular signaling pathways necessary for neuronal health and function [202]. Furthermore, the activity of the cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) response element‐binding protein (CREB) in neurons has been linked to numerous intracellular processes, including proliferation, differentiation, neurogenesis, and neuronal plasticity [203]. BDNF signals through two classes of membrane receptors: Trk and p75 neurotrophin receptor (p75NTR). The high‐affinity binding of mature BDNF to TrkB activates downstream signaling pathways. In a rat model of chronic unpredictable mild stress (CUMS), curcumin treatment reversed the decline in BDNF expression and ERK phosphorylation levels in the hippocampus, which resulted in the improvement of depressive‐like behavior and cognitive deficits [204]. A separate study in mice demonstrated that curcumin activates the CREB/BDNF signaling pathway, mitigating cadmium‐induced neurotoxicity and promoting hippocampal neurogenesis, thus ameliorating cognitive impairments. These findings indicate that curcumin promotes neuroregeneration through the modulation of the CREB/BDNF signaling pathway [205]. In rats exposed to acrylamide, the levels of both phosphorylated CREB (p‐CREB) and BDNF were significantly reduced. The administration of curcumin markedly reversed these effects in the hippocampus, effectively alleviating cognitive deficits [206]. Similarly, Shi et al. demonstrated that curcumin protects against acrylaldehyde‐induced neurotoxicity in HT22 mouse hippocampal neurons. Notably, this effect was exerted through the activation of the BDNF/TrkB signaling pathway, thereby establishing a rationale for the use of curcumin in the treatment of neurological disorders [207]. Additionally, curcumin has shown protective effects on rat hippocampal neurons through the upregulation of the BDNF/TrkB signaling pathway [208]. Meanwhile, curcumin administration significantly increased BDNF and p‐CREB protein levels in the dentate gyrus of mice. These changes resulted in the reduction of memory impairment caused by d‐galactose and were associated with the modulation of cell proliferation and neuroblast differentiation [209].

3.6.2 CAMP/PKA Signaling Pathway

cAMP is a small hydrophilic nucleotide second messenger generated from ATP by adenylate cyclase. This molecule regulates various signaling pathways through its action on cAMP‐dependent protein kinase A (PKA) [210]. This signaling is essential for biological processes such as cell differentiation, adhesion, apoptosis, survival, and proliferation [211]. Fluctuations in cAMP concentrations can impact neuronal functions, leading to the onset of neurological disorders [212]. The PKA family of serine/threonine kinases exists as a tetramer composed of two catalytic subunits (PKA‐C) and two regulatory subunits (PKA‐R). The PKA‐C subunits contain an active site for ATP binding and hydrolysis, which is required for its kinase activity. PKA is the key mediator of cAMP signaling; consequently, abnormalities in its function result in neurodevelopmental disorders affecting learning, memory, cognition, motor function, coordination, pain perception, and social behavior [213]. The binding of cAMP to the PKA‐R subunit induces a conformational change that results in the release of the PKA‐C subunit, enabling it to phosphorylate target proteins, such as CREB, and activate downstream signaling pathways [214, 215] involved in the regulation of transcription, metabolism, and cell growth, among other cellular activities. The binding of neurotransmitters, hormones, or signaling molecules to GPCRs can alter intracellular cAMP concentrations, resulting in PKA activation. Activated PKA phosphorylates CREB, allowing it to bind to cAMP response elements in target gene promoters and enhancing BDNF transcription. This pathway plays a pivotal role in the neural plasticity associated with neurological disorders. Studies in rats have shown that curcumin mitigates the dichloroacetic acid‐induced synaptic plasticity impairment and neurotoxicity in the hippocampus via the activation of the cAMP/PKA/CREB signaling pathway, thereby improving cognitive, learning, and memory functions, and exerting neuroprotective effects [216]. The 5‐HT1A receptor, located on the somas and dendrites of serotonergic neurons in the dorsal raphe nucleus, influences cAMP/PKA activity postsynaptically [217]. In an in vitro model of cortisol‐induced neuronal injury, curcumin administration reduced neuronal toxicity and enhanced neural plasticity through the modulation of the 5‐HT1A receptor and the subsequent activation of the cAMP/PKA/CREB signaling pathway [218]. Furthermore, research has shown that curcumin derivatives display antidepressant‐like behaviors associated with increased BDNF levels in the hippocampus. For instance, Li et al. found that curcumin increases cAMP/PKA/pCREB/BDNF protein expression in the hippocampus of mice, suggesting that its antidepressant effects are associated with this signaling axis [219]. Collectively, the beneficial effects of curcumin on neurological disorders are attributed to its regulatory influence on the cAMP/PKA signaling pathway.

3.6.3 Wnt/β‐Catenin Signaling Pathway

Substantial evidence supports that the Wnt/β‐catenin pathway is required for normal brain development, particularly for the regulation of neuronal functions within the CNS [220]. In the CNS, the Wnt pathway coordinates several neuronal processes, including differentiation, neurogenesis, synaptogenesis, and neuroprotection [221, 222, 223]. Given that the Wnt/β‐catenin pathway is essential for synaptic plasticity, which is required for cognitive functions such as learning and memory, its dysfunction is associated with the cognitive impairments observed in neurological disorders. Wnt proteins are necessary for synaptogenesis and the modulation of neurotransmission, both pre‐ and postsynaptically [224]. Additionally, the Wnt/β‐catenin pathway is critical for the formation of the blood–brain barrier and the maintenance of its integrity and functionality. Importantly, the disruption of this barrier is a notable pathogenic feature of neurological disorders [225]. In a healthy CNS, Wnt/β‐catenin signaling serves as a microenvironmental sensor that maintains the balance between cell survival and apoptosis [226]. Specifically, the binding of Wnt ligand and their agonists to frizzled receptors and low‐density lipoprotein receptor‐related protein 5/6 (LRP5/6) inhibits the glycogen synthase kinase‐3β (GSK‐3β)‐mediated phosphorylation of β‐catenin and its subsequent proteasomal degradation. This inhibition triggers Wnt pathway activation, allowing stabilized β‐catenin to accumulate in the cytoplasm and subsequently translocate to the nucleus. Here, it activates the expression of Wnt target genes involved in neuronal survival and plasticity, thereby preserving neuronal integrity. Cancer initiation and progression may be driven by genomic mutations. The Wnt signaling pathway is a key regulator of cell growth, differentiation, and migration, and it plays a critical role in promoting tumor proliferation. Studies have shown that Wnt/β‐catenin signaling is a central pathway governing these processes, and its overexpression can prevent chemotherapy‐induced cancer cell death [193]. In recent years, the Wnt signaling pathway has also been recognized as a crucial regulator of cardiac development and homeostasis. Aberrant activation of this pathway can lead to cardiac hypertrophy, fibrosis, and ultimately heart failure. Evidence suggests that inhibition of Wnt signaling may improve cardiac function and reduce complications associated with cardiovascular diseases [227].

Investigations into the therapeutic potential of curcumin in neurological disorders have identified the modulation of Wnt/β‐catenin signaling as a viable pharmacological strategy. Specifically, Lou et al. indicated that curcumin promotes adult neurogenesis in Aβ1–42 mice by activating the Wnt/β‐catenin pathway, an effect that is abolished following the administration of a Wnt/β‐catenin inhibitor [228]. In a model of intermittent hypoxia‐induced neuronal injury, curcumin treatment significantly ameliorated morphological damage in hippocampal neurons and restored proliferative capacity. These effects were accompanied by the activation of the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway and a reduction in both cytotoxicity and tissue damage, suggesting that curcumin exerts a protective role through the modulation of this pathway [229]. Another study demonstrated that curcumin treatment upregulated the protein expression of Wnt and β‐catenin while downregulating that of GSK‐3β, thereby promoting neuronal differentiation, inducing neurogenesis, and reversing cognitive impairments [230]. In an AD model involving the cotransfection of APPswe and BACE1‐mycHis plasmids into SH‐SY5Y neuroblastoma cells, Zhang et al. found that curcumin treatment inhibited GSK‐3β activity. This inhibition led to the activation of the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway and upregulated the protein expression of β‐catenin and cyclin D1, ultimately providing neuroprotection [231]. Additionally, it has been reported that curcumin significantly improves cognitive deficits following cerebral ischemia by upregulating the Wnt/β‐catenin pathway and stimulating neural stem cell proliferation [232]. Combined, these findings imply that curcumin alleviates neurological disorders by modulating the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway. One study demonstrated that curcumin attenuated vascular intimal hyperplasia and restenosis, thereby exerting cardiovascular protective effects, through lncRNA H19‐mediated activation of the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway. Specifically, curcumin reduced the expression of β‐catenin, c‐Myc, and cyclin D1, thereby inhibiting the proliferation and migration of vascular smooth muscle cells [233]. Emerging evidence indicates that curcumin significantly suppresses the proliferation and invasion of cervical cancer cells, induces G2/M phase cell cycle arrest and apoptosis, thereby inhibiting tumor growth and metastasis. These effects are mediated through downregulation of the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway [234]. Yuan et al. found that curcumin effectively ameliorated osteoarthritis and inhibited Wnt/β‐catenin signaling in chondrocytes, suggesting that curcumin may represent a promising therapeutic candidate and a potential new direction for osteoarthritis treatment [235].

In summary, the therapeutic effects of curcumin in Multiple diseases are mediated via a multifaceted profile that includes anti‐inflammatory activity, the modulation of mitochondrial dysfunction, apoptosis regulation, PI3K/AKT and MAPK signaling pathway modulation, antioxidant effects, and enhancement of neural plasticity. Thus, curcumin remains a viable candidate for advancing the treatment of neurological disorders.

4 Multiple Strategies for Improving Curcumin Bioavailability

Despite the potential of curcumin to improve neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases, its clinical translation is hindered by negligible water solubility, chemical instability, and rapid systemic metabolism, which severely limit its oral bioavailability [8]. Following oral administration, curcumin exhibits low bioavailability due to the first‐pass metabolism and, to some extent, intestinal metabolism. In particular, curcumin undergoes glucuronidation and sulfation, resulting in the formation of curcumin glucuronide and sulfate conjugates. These metabolic transformations reduce the levels of free curcumin in the circulation, thereby limiting the ability of the parent compound to reach target tissues at therapeutically effective concentrations and exert its therapeutic effects [236]. Studies have reported that the oral administration of 3.6 g of curcumin results in a peak plasma concentration of only 11.1 nmol/L after 1 h [228]. Additionally, although a portion of curcumin is absorbed by the human body, its therapeutic utility is constrained by rapid circulatory clearance and insufficient targeted delivery efficiency. Notably, the prolonged intake of high doses of curcumin may diminish its health‐promoting effects [228]. To address these challenges, advanced drug delivery systems have been developed to optimize the pharmacodynamic profile of the compound. Nanotechnology‐based platforms—including liposomes and various nanoparticles such as protein–polysaccharide (PRO–POL), poly (lactic‐co‐glycolic acid) (PLGA), solid lipid (SLNs), and gold (AuNPs) variants—have demonstrated potential in improving the stability, targeted delivery, and CNS permeating ability of curcumin. Additionally, innovative formulations such as exosomes, micelles, and cyclodextrin inclusion complexes are being explored to optimize the therapeutic effects of curcumin in neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. The following section reviews strategies for refining curcumin delivery systems, with a particular focus on nanotechnology‐based methods aimed at improving its oral bioavailability (Figure 4).

FIGURE 4 Multiple strategies for improving curcumin bioavailability.

4.1 Nanoparticles

4.1.1 Protein–Polysaccharide

Studies have noted that PRO–POL complexes function as effective delivery vehicles for curcumin [8]. Polysaccharides and proteins can be assembled into these carriers via hydrogen bonding, electrostatic interactions, and covalent bonding, which allows for the precise regulation of electrostatic interactions and interparticle repulsion.
