This structural organization effectively inhibits protein aggregation while enhancing the solubility and stability of the resulting complex [237]. Meng et al. [238] synthesized a poly‐chitosan (CS)–casein hydrophobic peptide conjugate emulsification system employing the Maillard reaction. Their findings indicated that the curcumin‐loaded emulsion exhibited excellent thermal stability, amphiphilic properties, and emulsifying capabilities. Ovalbumin (OVA) is a globular monomeric phospholipid protein abundantly found in egg white. The hydrophobic amino acid residues within OVA bind to the hydrophobic moieties of curcumin, thereby mitigating its low aqueous solubility and associated pharmacokinetic limitations.

In one study, the sol‐oil method was used to prepare curcumin nanoparticles encapsulated within lactoferrin. These nanoparticles enhanced the specificity and efficacy of curcumin in the brain, ultimately augmenting its neuroprotective effects [239]. In other research, Niu et al. prepared curcumin‐encapsulating OVA/carboxymethyl cellulose sodium (CMC) nanoparticles (OVA/CMC NPs) using a combination of complex coacervation and thermal induction. Compared with free curcumin, these OVA/CMC NPs demonstrated superior stability and antioxidant capacity [240]. Furthermore, Yang et al. synthesized CS and BSA nanoparticles (CS–BSA NPs) coated with curcumin for the treatment of AD. Using an in vitro system, they reported that these curcumin‐loaded nanoparticles significantly enhanced the ability of curcumin to cross the BBB, extended the drug's half‐life, and decreased the frequency of administration. These nanoparticles also exhibited anti‐inflammatory and neuroprotective effects [35]. Importantly, leveraging the properties of these CS–BSA NPs may amplify the neural plasticity‐promoting effects of curcumin. In addition, lipid‐modified albumin nanoparticles loaded with curcumin have been shown to increase ROS levels, induce extensive DNA fragmentation and MMP depolarization, thereby promoting apoptosis in MDA‐MB‐231 cells. These nanoparticles also exhibit strong tumor‐suppressive effects in 4T1 tumor‐bearing mouse models [241]. Furthermore, curcumin delivered via albumin‐based nanoparticles has demonstrated significant inhibitory effects on breast cancer cells, highlighting its potential as an effective nanocarrier‐mediated therapeutic strategy [242].

4.1.2 Polylactic Acid‐Glycolic Acid

Polylactic acid‐glycolic acid nanoparticles (PLGA NPs) encapsulating curcumin have been demonstrated to significantly enhance the concentrations of curcumin in splenic and pulmonary tissues. Compared with free curcumin, curcumin‐loaded PLGA NPs (Cur–PLGA NPs) exhibit prolonged retention in the cerebral cortex and hippocampus, with increases of 96% and 83%, respectively [243]. Furthermore, Sanaz et al. reported that Cur–PLGA NPs can mitigate hydrogen peroxide‐induced oxidative stress and prevent GSH depletion, thereby conferring protection against neuronal injury [244]. Paka et al. synthesized GSH‐functionalized nanoparticles (GSH‐NPs) encapsulating curcumin and observed that they displayed significantly enhanced neuroprotective efficacy compared with free curcumin. These nanoparticles evaded lysosomal degradation and enhanced the absorption of the formulation [245]. In another strategy, curcumin‐loaded PLGA NPs were incorporated into a three‐dimensional (3D)‐printed scaffold composed of sodium alginate and gelatin. This formulation was designed for rapid sublingual dissolution, thereby bypassing hepatic first‐pass metabolism, which ordinarily limits bioavailability. The formulation also provides improved stability and reduced dosing frequency. Notably, PLGA NPs exhibit no cytotoxicity and exert significant neuroprotective effects, highlighting their therapeutic potential in neurological disorders [246]. Fan et al. engineered brain‐targeting nanoparticles by conjugating PLGA–polyethylene glycol (PEG) with B6 peptides for curcumin encapsulation. These nanoparticles reduced curcumin particle size, enhanced cellular uptake, and demonstrated excellent blood compatibility. Compared with free curcumin, PLGA–PEG–B6–curcumin nanoparticles significantly improved spatial learning and memory in mice [247].

One study introduced H‐ferritin nanocages (HFn) as a novel nanoformulation for curcumin delivery. The authors reported that HFn–curcumin exhibited superior water dispersibility, lacked cytotoxicity, and effectively penetrated the BBB. In mice, this formulation significantly inhibited microglial proliferation and was associated with improved cognitive function [248]. Anial et al. developed water‐soluble PLGA‐coated curcumin nanoparticles conjugated with Tet‐1 peptides. The resulting PLGA–Tet‐1–curcumin nanoparticles effectively disrupted amyloid aggregation, demonstrated strong antioxidant activity without cytotoxic effects, and showed significantly enhanced neuronal uptake. Consequently, these PLGA–Tet‐1–curcumin nanoparticles constitute a promising strategy for targeted drug delivery to specific brain regions [249]. Nanoparticles used as drug delivery systems can facilitate drug penetration across the BBB through surface modification or functional coating. Heba et al. demonstrated that combining curcumin with zinc oxide nanoparticles (ZnONPs) significantly improved its pharmacokinetic profile and solubility. Compared with curcumin alone, curcumin–ZnONPs exhibited notably enhanced antioxidant activity [250]. Furthermore, the encapsulation of curcumin within PLGA nanoparticles shields it from environmental degradation, including enzymatic breakdown and pH‐related instability, thereby substantially improving its bioavailability, solubility, and chemical stability [251]. Researchers have developed curcumin‐loaded biocompatible PLGA nanoparticles to improve the bioavailability and therapeutic efficacy of curcumin, thereby enhancing its antitumor activity. The results showed that after administration of curcumin nanoparticles, the inhibition rate in gastric cancer cells increased from 7% within 24 h to 69%. In addition, compared with native curcumin, nano‐formulated curcumin significantly increased apoptosis after 72 h of treatment [252]. Leila et al. [253] prepared curcumin‐loaded PLGA nanoparticles using an oil‐in‐water (O/W) emulsion solvent diffusion method. The nanoparticles were subsequently coated with polyethyleneimine and complexed with Bcl‐2 siRNA. The formulations were characterized in terms of zeta potential, particle size distribution, and drug encapsulation efficiency. The results demonstrated enhanced cytotoxicity against T47D cells and reduced Bcl‐2 gene expression, indicating a synergistic anticancer effect between siRNA‐mediated gene silencing and curcumin. Sindhu et al. [254] encapsulated curcumin in a PLGA‐based drug delivery system for targeted delivery to atherosclerotic plaques. The Cur–PLGA nanoparticles exhibited good biocompatibility with THP‐1 monocyte‐derived macrophages and reduced intracellular cholesterol content, with 250 µg/mL identified as the optimal concentration. In addition, Cur–PLGA nanoparticles effectively inhibited the formation of atherosclerotic foam cells. Another study synthesized PLGA atorvastatin curcumin (PLGA–AT–Cur) conjugated nanoparticles. In vivo results demonstrated that oral administration of PLGA–AT–Cur nanoparticles reduced the progression of atherosclerotic lesions in mice. Compared with free curcumin, PLGA–AT–Cur nanoparticles decreased aortic lipid accumulation, oxidative stress, and serum high‐density lipoprotein levels. These findings suggest that PLGA–AT–Cur nanoparticles may have potential therapeutic benefits in cardiovascular disease [255]. In a further study, curcumin and short‐chain fatty acids were coencapsulated in PLGA nanoparticles (Cur–PLGA NPs@SCFAs), achieving an encapsulation efficiency of 66% and sustained release over 48 h. In vitro, Caco‐2 cell assays showed good cytocompatibility, with cell viability exceeding 81.5% at 40 µg/mL. In vivo, high‐dose treatment significantly improved disease symptoms, restored colon length and spleen index, and reduced inflammatory cytokine levels. These findings indicate that the nano‐delivery system provides significant protective effects against intestinal inflammation and barrier dysfunction [256].

4.1.3 Solid Lipid Nanoparticles

Solid lipid nanoparticles (SLNs) are drug delivery systems developed in the early 1990s [229]. Structurally, SLNs consist of a solid hydrophobic core surrounded by a phospholipid monolayer, with surfactants incorporated to enhance stability. Unlike polymeric nanoparticles, SLNs are mainly composed of physiologically compatible lipid components, which reduces the risk of acute and chronic toxicity [229]. Additionally, SLNs exhibit greater physicochemical stability and lower rates of drug leakage issues than liposomes [229]. Curcumin‐loaded SLNs (Cur–SLN) were prepared using a microemulsion technique, yielding an average particle size of 136.4 nm and a drug loading capacity of 10%, allowing for sustained curcumin release over 7 days. Pharmacokinetic evaluation in rats demonstrated that orally administered Cur–SLN achieved significantly higher bioavailability than free curcumin [230].

Ganesan et al. assessed the efficacy of Cur–SLN in BV‐2 microglial cells under LPS‐induced neuroinflammatory conditions. Compared with conventional curcumin, Cur–SLN more effectively reduced nitric oxide and proinflammatory cytokine levels in a dose‐dependent manner. These findings highlight the potential of SLNs to serve as a delivery platform for curcumin in the context of microglia‐mediated neuroinflammation [231]. Furthermore, the intraperitoneal administration of curcumin‐encapsulated SLNs in AD model mice resulted in blood–brain barrier penetration, enabled the modulation of tissue transglutaminase (TG2) subtype expression, promoted cell repair, and reduced neuroinflammation, thereby improving cognitive dysfunction [232]. In an aluminum‐induced AD mouse model, Cur–SLN effectively attenuated aluminum‐induced neurotoxicity by inhibiting lipid peroxidation, restoring antioxidant enzyme activity, and reducing acetylcholinesterase (AChE) levels. Behavioral assessments demonstrated that Cur–SLN (1–50 mg/kg) improved spatial memory more effectively than free curcumin (50 mg/kg). Moreover, Cur–SLN inhibited lipid peroxidation by 97.46% and reduced AChE levels to 73% of the model peak, significantly exceeding the effects observed with free curcumin. These findings suggest that SLNs efficiently preserve and deliver bioactive curcumin, achieving therapeutic effects at substantially lower doses than unencapsulated curcumin [257]. Huang et al. prepared Cur–SLN using an emulsification‐evaporation method, and found that it enhanced both curcumin dispersibility and brain delivery efficiency while alleviating oxidative stress‐induced neuronal apoptosis, thus demonstrating neuroprotective effects [161]. In another study, a temperature‐sensitive Cur–SLN in situ nasal gel was developed for intranasal delivery targeting AD. The formulation demonstrated appropriate particle size, high encapsulation efficiency, and sustained‐release characteristics. In vitro studies revealed that loading curcumin onto nanocarriers and gel formulations reduced its free radical scavenging rate while facilitating prolonged drug release and enhancing brain bioavailability [258]. Prabhu et al. further employed Cur–SLN‐loaded composite microneedles to improve curcumin bioavailability and therapeutic efficacy. Cur–SLN was prepared via a microemulsion method using glyceryl monostearate as the lipid matrix and Tween 80 as a stabilizer. The results indicated that the excipients exhibited good compatibility with curcumin and produced no skin irritation. Furthermore, the formulation significantly improved motor deficits in an animal model of PD, enhancing motor coordination and balance. These results provide a theoretical basis for the development of novel Cur–SLN formulations for the treatment of neurological disorders [259]. Another study reported that Cur–SLNs exhibited small and uniform particle size, high encapsulation efficiency, favorable in vitro sustained‐release profiles, and excellent stability. Compared with free curcumin, Cur–SLNs significantly enhanced cellular uptake efficiency, thereby improving the inhibition of proliferation, migration, and invasion of MHCC‐97H cells, while also increased apoptosis [260]. Vinit et al. [261] evaluated the anticancer effects of curcumin‐loaded SLNs conjugated with concanavalin A (ConA) in A549 human lung cancer cells. Cur–SLNs improved the recognition and uptake by lung cancer cells, thereby enhancing the inhibitory effect of curcumin on A549 cell proliferation. These findings suggest that Cur–SLNs may serve as a potential targeted drug delivery system for lung cancer therapy. Arora et al. [262] investigated the protective effects of curcumin and Cur–SLNs in complete Freund's adjuvant‐induced rat model of arthritis. The results showed that arthritic rats exhibited significantly reduced paw withdrawal thresholds and shortened hot‐plate response time. In addition, peripheral white blood cell counts, oxidative/nitrosative stress levels, tumor necrosis factor‐α (TNF‐α), C‐reactive protein (CRP), and radiographic changes in the tibio‐tarsal joint were markedly increased. Compared with free curcumin, Cur–SLNs significantly alleviated arthritic symptoms, improved biochemical parameters, preserved radiological joint integrity, and demonstrated dose‐dependent therapeutic effects. Another study evaluated the potential of Cur–SLNs for the treatment of sepsis. Mice were pretreated with either free curcumin or Cur–SLNs prior to intraperitoneal LPS administration. Compared with free curcumin, Cur–SLNs more effectively reduced the expression of proinflammatory cytokines IL‐1β, IL‐6, and TNF‐α, while enhanced the expression of the anti‐inflammatory cytokine IL‐10. Moreover, Cur–SLN treatment significantly alleviated sepsis‐induced organ failure [263]. In addition, a Cur–SLN hydrogel was developed for the topical treatment of skin inflammation. The formulation exhibited small particle size, high encapsulation efficiency, and good stability, as well as a certain occlusive and moisturizing effect. In vitro release and ex vivo permeation studies demonstrated that the Cur–SLN hydrogel significantly improved drug release, skin penetration, and skin retention compared with free curcumin gel. In a mouse wound inflammation model, Cur–SLNs markedly promoted wound healing, reduced lipid peroxidation levels, and increased antioxidant markers such as GSH and SOD. Histopathological analysis further confirmed superior anti‐inflammatory and tissue‐repair effects compared with free curcumin gel [264].

4.1.4 Gold Nanoparticles

AuNPs exhibit unique properties owing to their small size and distinctive structure. The diameter of AuNPs typically ranges from 1 to 100 nm, enabling them to interact with biomolecules at the cellular and molecular levels. Additionally, AuNPs are recognized for their excellent biocompatibility and relative inertness, which minimizes the risk of adverse immune reactions or cytotoxicity when used in biological systems [265]. They can be functionalized with various molecules, such as targeting ligand neurological disorders, antibodies, or drugs, allowing for their customization for specific applications, thereby enhancing their targeting and therapeutic capabilities [266] and offering promising strategies for optimizing drug delivery and improving treatment outcomes.

Zhou et al. modified AuNPs with peptides via gold‐sulfur, creating peptide‐functionalized AuNPs that selectively bind copper ions, facilitating the formation of Aβ–copper complexes, thereby mitigating cellular toxicity [267]. Notably, advanced delivery systems have been developed to enhance the neuroprotective effects of curcumin in neurological disorders. For instance, one study evaluated the activity of curcumin against CNS amoebic infections by coupling curcumin with AuNPs (Cur–AuNPs). This formulation resulted in a 56% increase in the inhibition of amoebic activity relative to free curcumin, with no detectable cytotoxicity at concentrations of 5 and 10 µM. The authors proposed that curcumin–AuNPs represent a novel potential therapeutic agent for treating cerebral amoebic infections [268]. Moreover, low‐intensity pulsed ultrasound combined with Cur–AuNPs was found to exert neuroregulatory effects in an Aβ‐induced AD mouse model. Cur–AuNPs traversed the blood–brain barrier without inducing hepatic toxicity. Compared with standalone curcumin therapy, Cur–AuNPs effectively reduced the levels of nitrite in the hippocampus and cortex and downregulated NF‐κB expression in the hippocampus [269]. Zeynab et al. [270] reported a Cur–AuNPs and evaluated its biological effects in a doxorubicin‐induced acute toxicity model in BALB/c mice. The results demonstrated that Cur–AuNPs exerted significantly stronger cardioprotective effects than free curcumin. After 24 h of intervention, Cur–AuNPs reduced serum biomarkers of myocardial injury and apoptotic proteins, increased the antiapoptotic protein Bcl‐2, and showed no evidence of interstitial hemorrhage or cellular gaps in myocardial histology. Previous studies have shown that in a chicken embryo oxidative stress model, 300 µg/mL of Cur–AuNPs significantly reduced cardiac lipid peroxidation and ROS levels, while restored the activity of antioxidant enzymes such as SOD and catalase (CAT), suggesting that curcumin alleviated acute cardiotoxicity by modulating oxidative stress [271]. In another study, curcumin was used as both a reducing and stabilizing agent, and Cur–AuNPs were integrated into a conductive nanocomposite hydrogel to develop a biocompatible and multifunctional porous nanocomposite material. In vitro antioxidant assays demonstrated that Cur–AuNPs exhibited dose‐dependent free radical scavenging activity, reaching a peak at 50 µg/mL. In an isoproterenol‐induced myocardial injury rat model, Cur–AuNP administration significantly alleviated cardiac damage, accompanied by reductions in inflammation, oxidative stress, and infarct size. These findings indicate that curcumin‐mediated AuNPs possess strong therapeutic potential in cardiovascular diseases [272]. Wang et al. combined the anticancer properties of curcumin with AuNPs and encapsulated them within a ZIF‐8 framework to construct a composite drug delivery system (Cur/Au@ZIF‐8). The drug loading efficiency of curcumin in this system reached 47.16%, and the release profile showed a faster release under conditions of pH 5.5, with a cumulative release rate of 72.34%. In a rat hepatocellular carcinoma model, Cur/Au@ZIF‐8 exhibited enhanced antitumor efficacy, achieving a tumor inhibition rate of 46.81% [273]. In addition, an injectable decellularized ECM scaffold combined with AuNPs was developed. In vitro evaluations using L929 mouse fibroblasts and human chondrocytes demonstrated good biocompatibility, ROS scavenging capacity, and chondroprotective effects. Under IL‐1β stimulation, the system effectively reduced intracellular ROS levels and alleviated apoptosis in chondrocytes [274].

4.2 Liposomes

Liposomes are specialized vesicles typically composed of cholesterol and phospholipids, among other substances, capable of encapsulating therapeutic agents. They serve as versatile platforms for biotherapy and for overcoming bioavailability constraints. Liposomes can encapsulate a variety of pharmacological agents, protecting them from degradation while enhancing pharmacokinetics and minimizing adverse reactions. These advantages stem from their particle size, lipid bilayer structure, and inherent biocompatibility. Consequently, liposome‐based delivery systems are among the most extensively investigated methodologies in modern pharmacology, with significant applications in the treatment of neurological disorders, oncology, and pain management [275]. Advancements in liposomal technology have enabled the evolution of liposomal platforms from passive to active targeting systems, while conventional liposomes are being transformed into long‐circulating formulations. Recently, numerous modified liposomes constructed from specific materials have been designed to achieve targeted delivery across the blood–brain barrier and for intervention in key signaling pathways or targets. In clinical research, several novel liposomal drug delivery methods have been approved for therapeutic use, offering new avenues for the treatment of neurological disorders.

In applications within the nervous system, surface modifications of liposomes primarily aim to overcome the challenges posed by the blood–brain barrier [276]. The blood–brain barrier is a physiological barrier formed by brain microvascular endothelial cells, astrocytes, and neurons that effectively prevents most drugs from entering the CNS, thereby maintaining neurological stability. However, this barrier also hinders the efficient delivery of treatments for neurological disorders, often resulting in poor efficacy or increased side effects. Liposomes are self‐assembled colloidal structures featuring an aqueous core surrounded by single or multiple lipid bilayers, synthesized through a variety of methods, including ethanol injection, thin‐film hydration, and extrusion [277, 278]. Their amphiphilic nature allows for the encapsulation of hydrophilic drugs in the aqueous core and hydrophobic drugs within the lipid layers, thus broadening the range of drug delivery strategies for ND treatment [279]. Seeking to enhance the therapeutic effects of curcumin in neurological disorders, Chen et al. delivered curcumin to the brain via NLCs and reported that it significantly extended its half‐life from 3.1 to 5.7 h and increased curcumin plasma concentrations by 6.4‐fold [280]. Further investigations involving the incorporation of curcumin into NLCs (CUR–NLCs) revealed that, compared with rats given free curcumin, those administered these NLCs demonstrated enhanced neuronal survival in the cortex and hippocampus, along with significant inhibition of neuroinflammation through the downregulation of proinflammatory factors in the brain. These outcomes can be attributed to the ability of CUR–NLCs to cross the BBB and efficiently deliver curcumin to the brain, thereby enhancing its neuroprotective effects [281]. Similarly, research involving curcumin encapsulated in liposomes containing cardiolipin (CL) and wheat germ agglutinin (WGA) (WGA–CL–CUR) showed that this formulation significantly enhanced the ability of curcumin to traverse the BBB while also mitigating potential curcumin‐induced cytotoxicity and attenuating neuronal apoptosis [282]. Soroor et al. demonstrated that encapsulating curcumin within SLNs and NLCs significantly enhanced the uptake of curcumin into the brain, resulting in a greater than fourfold increase in curcumin absorption [283]. Meanwhile, Yan et al. encapsulated curcumin into lipid–PLGA nanobubbles using the melt crystallization method, yielding curcumin‐loaded lipid–PLGA nanobubbles (Cur–NBs). They noted that these nanobubbles were effective at releasing curcumin, achieving a release rate of up to 30% within 6 h. Furthermore, the Cur–NBs were capable of penetrating the BBB, enabling curcumin to reach the brain core, while also significantly enhancing its solubility [284]. Li et al. [285] developed E‐selectin and intercellular adhesion molecule‐1 (ICAM‐1) targeted liposomes (T‐AC–Lipo) for curcumin delivery to enhance drug targeting and antiatherosclerotic efficacy. Therapeutic effects were evaluated in both in vitro endothelial inflammatory model and in vivo ApoE−/− atherosclerosis model. The T‐AC–Lipo formulation significantly reduced the percentage of aortic atherosclerotic lesions and decreased inflammatory cytokine levels and lipid profiles. In addition, T‐AC–Lipo increased intracellular curcumin concentration and effectively reduced cellular cytotoxicity. Zhu et al. [286] prepared curcumin‐loaded liposomes (Cur–Lips) using a microfluidic chip technique, achieving an encapsulation efficiency of 99.33%. Cur–Lips increased skin penetration by 2.76‐fold, which further increased to 4.48‐fold when combined with the transdermal peptide TD‐1. This delivery system enhanced drug permeation by interacting with stratum corneum lipids and keratin, increasing hydration, and facilitating transport via hair follicles and sweat glands. In vitro and in vivo studies confirmed that the TD‐1 + Cur–Lips hydrogel significantly inhibited melanoma growth and induced apoptosis, providing a safe and effective strategy for localized skin cancer therapy. In another study, Narayanan et al. encapsulated curcumin in liposomes and evaluated its chemopreventive effects in a male B6C3F1/J mouse model of prostate cancer. The results showed that the liposomal formulation significantly increased curcumin concentrations in serum and prostate tissue. In vitro experiments demonstrated that curcumin effectively inhibited cell proliferation and induced apoptosis, and in vivo studies further confirmed significant suppression of prostate tumor growth [287]. Dyandevi et al. developed a curcumin–piperine liposomal hydrogel for the topical treatment of atopic dermatitis. Ex vivo skin permeation studies showed a drug retention rate of 74.23 ± 1.12% in the skin, with minimal transdermal penetration, supporting its suitability for local therapy. Antioxidant assays demonstrated strong free radical scavenging activity. Compared with free curcumin, the liposomal hydrogel significantly reduced erythema, scratching behavior, ear thickness, and spleen index. Overall, the curcumin liposomal hydrogel exhibited synergistic antioxidant and anti‐inflammatory effects [288].

4.3 Nanoemulsions

NEs are colloidal dispersion systems composed of two immiscible liquids stabilized by ionic or nonionic surfactants, with dispersed droplet diameters ranging from 20 to 500 nm [289]. Surfactants are frequently utilized in NE structures to prevent aggregation via electrostatic and steric stabilization [290]. Within these systems, the nano‐sized droplets display sustained Brownian motion, which enables them to counteract gravitational effects and enhances resistance to droplet coalescence, thus maintaining kinetic stability. However, NEs also exhibit thermodynamic instability, primarily attributed to Ostwald ripening, a process where smaller droplets gradually enlarge and promote phase separation, consequently affecting long‐term dispersion stability. Generally, an NE comprises three components: oil, water, and emulsifiers, mixed in appropriate ratios to form either simple or multilayer structures. Simple NEs are further categorized into O/W or water‐in‐oil (W/O) emulsions [291], while multilayered NEs contain an additional internal droplet within the continuous phase, resembling the dispersed medium, which can be either O/W/O or W/W. Hence, the composition of the system plays is the primary determinant of the resulting NE type. Compared with other nano‐drug delivery systems, NEs offer distinct advantages, such as high solubility, flexibility in composition, ease of preparation, rapid drug release, high stability, uniform particle size, and high drug loading capacity [292].

Curcumin is a lipophilic molecule, and it displays low aqueous solubility and limited oral bioavailability. Consequently, NEs serve as a unique pathophysiological‐targeted drug delivery system for neurological disorders, offering new solutions to current therapeutic challenges. Research has shown that curcumin NEs (Cur–NEs) improve neurological function scores in rat models of cerebral I/R injury, restore redox homeostasis in the brain, reduce the expression of inflammatory factors, and attenuate cell apoptosis, thereby effectively alleviating neuronal damage [293]. Sheng et al. developed a Cur–NE using emulsification methods to enhance the water solubility and oral bioavailability of curcumin. Using an animal model of CUMS, they assessed the antidepressant activity of Cur–NE and subsequently analyzed its pharmacokinetic behavior. The optimization of Cur–NE yielded uniformly small particle sizes, which significantly improved the pharmacokinetics of curcumin in rats. Furthermore, Cur–NE notably ameliorated depressive and anxiety‐like behaviors in CUMS model mice, exerting antidepressant effects through the modulation of the serotonergic system [294]. Another study found that the relative bioavailability of Cur–NE reached 11.18 times that of a curcumin suspension, enhancing both the solubility and stability of curcumin, and representing a promising drug delivery system [295]. Additionally, Manoj et al. prepared nano‐emulsified curcumin droplets, which significantly improved curcumin's solubility and oral absorption rates and improved memory and cognitive abilities in mice while reducing serum cortisol levels [296]. A study evaluated the protective effects of curcumin‐loaded NEs against doxorubicin‐induced cardiotoxicity. After encapsulation of curcumin in 100 nm NEs, cardiomyocyte viability increased by approximately 35–40%. In addition, lipid peroxidation products and inflammatory cytokine levels were significantly reduced, while the expression of the anti‐inflammatory cytokine IL‐10 was increased [297]. Bharmoria et al. developed a gelatin‐coated olive O/W NE encapsulating curcumin (G‐Cur–NE). This formulation effectively inhibited curcumin autoxidation and significantly improved its stability and cellular uptake. In MDA‐MB‐231 triple‐negative breast cancer cells, G‐Cur–NE selectively induced cancer cell death while exhibited low cytotoxicity toward normal L929 cells, demonstrating its potential as a safe and efficient nanoplatform for breast cancer therapy [298]. Another study investigated a NE codelivery system containing FU and curcumin (CU/FU–NE). The results showed that the area under the curve (AUC0‐t) of CU/FU–NE was 8.85‐fold and 8.59‐fold higher than that of curcumin (CU) and FU, respectively. The IC50 values of CU/FU–NE against HepG2 cells were 4.6‐ and 4.9‐fold lower than those of CU and FU. In vivo antitumor studies demonstrated a tumor inhibition rate of 49.29% for CU/FU–NE, compared with 24.84% for CU and 4.72% for FU. These findings indicated that CU/FU–NE exhibited enhanced efficacy and reduced toxicity, representing a promising anticancer formulation [299]. Lei et al. prepared a curcumin/emodin NE (Cur/EMO NE), which was subsequently coated with a CS–sodium alginate crosslinked layer. In vitro release studies demonstrated pH‐responsive controlled release behavior. Pharmacodynamic studies in vitro and in vivo showed that Cur/EMO NE primarily targeted macrophages, exerting anti‐inflammatory effects by inhibiting proinflammatory cytokine secretion and promoting anti‐inflammatory cytokine production. In addition, Cur/EMO NE exhibited favorable intestinal mucosal repair properties by upregulating the expression of tight junction proteins ZO‐1 and occludin, thereby improving epithelial barrier integrity [300].

4.4 Polymer Micelles

Amphiphilic block copolymers self‐assemble into micelles in aqueous solutions through hydrophobic interactions, serving as carriers for insoluble therapeutic agents. These structures comprise a hydrophobic core and a hydrophilic shell, and their size and shape can be adjusted by varying the length and proportion of the respective hydrophilic and hydrophobic segments [301]. The hydrophobic core enhances the solubility of hydrophobic drugs, while the hydrophilic shell forms extensive hydrogen bonds with water molecules in solution, creating a protective and compact exterior that reduces protein adsorption and cellular adhesion. Notably, micelles are less likely to be recognized and captured by the endothelial reticular system in the bloodstream, thereby prolonging their circulation time. These properties render them one of the most promising nano‐drug carrier systems currently available. Studies have explored the applicability of triblock copolymers as polymeric nanocarriers for passive targeting to the brain. Li et al. prepared amphiphilic triblock copolymer (PLA–PEG–PLA) micelles loaded with curcumin via film hydration methods and subsequently investigated their neuroprotective effects following bilateral common carotid artery occlusion. The resulting micelles displayed a spherical morphology with a hydrodynamic radius of 96.67 nm and a polydispersity index (PDI) of 0.167, indicative of uniform dispersion and good solubility. Whereas free curcumin exhibited rapid dissolution and diffusion, curcumin was slowly released from the micelles. Thus, PLA–PEG–PLA micelles may serve as an effective system for the sustained release of curcumin in the brain [302]. In a separate study, deoxycholic acid–polyethylenimine‐modified micelles were prepared to encapsulate curcumin in a hydrophobic core, yielding small, stable, and nontoxic carriers, while achieving brain‐targeted delivery and significantly enhancing curcumin's water solubility and cellular delivery efficiency [303]. Stephanie et al. compared the impact of natural curcumin and curcumin micelles on mitochondrial function in NMRI mice, PC12 cells, and isolated mouse mitochondria. Their findings revealed that micellar encapsulation achieved a 10–40‐fold increase in curcumin concentrations in mouse plasma and brains compared with that observed for the unencapsulated compound [304]. Similarly, an amphiphilic DSPE–PEG2000 block copolymer was used as a carrier to prepare curcumin polymer micelles via solvent evaporation, yielding micelles with sizes ranging from 70 to 110 nm exhibiting good stability and low cytotoxicity [305]. Kang et al. developed an acid‐responsive curcumin‐loaded polymeric micelle system that could rapidly release curcumin and restore its fluorescence in the acidic microenvironment of osteoarthritis. The curcumin micelles significantly protected joint structures and reduced ECM degradation by inhibiting TNF‐α and IL‐1β, showing superior efficacy compared with free curcumin. This pathology‐triggered responsive nanoplatform provides a new strategy for anti‐inflammatory therapy in osteoarthritis [306]. Carlson et al. [307] prepared curcumin‐loaded polymeric micelles using a solvent casting method. The resulting micelles enabled stable and uniform dispersion of curcumin and exhibited favorable controlled‐release properties. In cardiomyocytes, they showed a pronounced antagonistic effect against toxicity, reducing ROS levels and Caspase‐3/7 activity, thereby alleviating doxorubicin‐induced cardiac injury. Another study employed a one‐step solid dispersion method to encapsulate curcumin into monomethyl poly (ethylene glycol)–poly(ε‐caprolactone)–poly (trimethylene carbonate) (MPEG‐P(CL‐co‐TMC)) micelles. The obtained curcumin micelles had a small particle size of 27.6 ± 0.7 nm and a low PDI of 0.11 ± 0.05, with a drug loading of 14.07 ± 0.94% and an encapsulation efficiency of 96.08 ± 3.23%. In vitro experiments showed that, compared with free curcumin, the micelles significantly enhanced cellular uptake in CT26 cells and increased apoptosis induction. In vivo, the micelles more effectively inhibited the growth of subcutaneous CT26 colon tumors, including suppression of tumor proliferation and angiogenesis, while promoted tumor cell apoptosis. In addition, the curcumin‐loaded micelles exhibited low systemic toxicity [308].

4.5 Exosomes

Exosomes are endogenous nanovesicles composed of a natural lipid bilayer rich in adhesion proteins that can interact with cell membranes [309]. Initially, exosomes were thought to be responsible for carrying waste produced by cells; however, they are now known to serve as essential mediators of intercellular communication, transporting functional genetic information and proteins between cells. Notably, their significance in the treatment of neurological disorders is now widely recognized. Given their cellular origin, exosomes possess inherent biocompatibility and degradability, which minimizes the risks of immune rejection and adverse reactions, making them a safer drug delivery option than synthetic carriers [310, 311]. Moreover, their lipid bilayer structure confers stability while also enabling them to cross the blood–brain barrier for the targeted delivery of small‐molecule drugs to the nervous system [312]. The protective potential of miRNA‐126‐modified exosomes following cerebral ischemic injury has been explored in a rat model of MCAO. The authors injected miRNA‐126 exosomes into MCAO model rats, resulting in enhanced neurogenesis and angiogenesis alongside the suppression of microglial activation and inflammatory responses [313]. These findings indicate that miRNA‐126‐enriched exosomes may contribute to the regulation of neurogenesis and neuroinflammatory processes. Mohammad et al. assessed the effects of curcumin‐loaded exosomes on streptozotocin (STZ)‐induced learning and memory impairments and neuroinflammation in rats. Compared with untreated mesenchymal stem cell‐derived exosomes, curcumin‐pretreated exosomes significantly improved memory and learning performance, reduced neuronal degeneration, and more effectively promoted microglial polarization toward the M2 phenotype [314]. In another study, investigators developed human endometrial stem cell‐derived exosome formulations loaded with curcumin, which exhibited uniform particle size and morphology, good stability, and sustained release characteristics. In a PD mouse model, these curcumin exosome formulations were able to cross the blood–brain barrier, ameliorated motor coordination deficits in mice, and enhanced the neuroprotective effects of curcumin against neuronal loss and α‐Syn aggregation [315]. Ding et al. further engineered exosomes modified with DSPE–PEG2000‐C3 peptides and triphenylphosphine (TPP) to encapsulate curcumin (C3/TPP–EXO–Cur) and administered them intravenously to tauP301S transgenic mice. C3/TPP–EXO enabled the targeted delivery of curcumin to neuronal mitochondria, thereby enhancing intracellular drug uptake efficiency. In vivo experiments demonstrated that C3/TPP–EXO–Cur treatment improved memory deficits in tauP301S mice by suppressing tau phosphorylation and inhibiting the expression of proapoptotic genes, providing a novel and potentially effective therapeutic strategy for AD [316]. Zhang et al. constructed neutrophil‐derived exosomes capable of selectively targeting the brains of AD mice in response to inflammatory signals. This platform facilitated the selective intracellular release of therapeutic agents, including curcumin, in the presence of elevated levels of MMP‐2 in the AD microenvironment. In vivo studies demonstrated that curcumin‐loaded exosomes significantly improved memory and cognitive performance in AD mice and inhibited mitochondrial autophagy, thereby restoring mitochondrial metabolic functions in neurons, alleviating mitochondrial dysfunction, and delaying the progression of AD [317]. In addition, curcumin was incorporated into immature DC‐derived exosomes, and this formulation significantly reduced α‐Syn expression, increased neuronal survival, and markedly improved motor performance in PD model mice, thus demonstrating pronounced neuroprotective effects [318]. Sun et al. [319] demonstrated that curcumin encapsulated within exosomes can be preferentially taken up by inflammation‐activated monocyte‐derived myeloid cells. Exosomal delivery significantly improved the solubility, stability, and in vivo bioavailability of curcumin. This system markedly suppressed the expression of proinflammatory cytokines IL‐6 and TNF‐α, reduced inflammatory cell infiltration, and conferred protection in the LPS‐induced septic shock mouse model, ultimately improving survival. These findings suggest that curcumin‐loaded exosomes can target inflammatory cells and enhance anti‐inflammatory activity, providing a novel therapeutic strategy for inflammation‐related diseases. He et al. [320] evaluated the therapeutic effects of curcumin‐loaded mesenchymal stem cell‐derived exosomes (Curc–Exos) on RA synovial fibroblasts (HIG‐82). The exosomal formulation enhanced cellular uptake and stability, significantly inhibited LPS‐induced cell proliferation, and promoted apoptosis. In addition, it downregulated antiapoptotic proteins IAP1 and IAP2, as well as inflammatory mediators including IL‐6, TNF‐α, MMP‐1, and PGE2. This strategy provides a new direction for targeted anti‐inflammatory delivery of curcumin in RA. In recent years, exosomes have emerged as important mediators in cancer therapy. Xue et al. developed engineered exosomes comodified with hyaluronidase PH20 (Exos‐PH20) and curcumin to achieve targeted drug delivery and remodel the tumor microenvironment, thereby enhancing tumor penetration. Rather than eliminating CAFs, this system aimed to “normalize” them and enhance antitumor efficacy. The results showed that Exos‐PH20@Cur leveraged the tumor‐targeting ability of exosomes and PH20‐mediated hyaluronan degradation to disrupt crosstalk between CAFs and cancer cells mediated by TGF‐β and IL‐6, thereby suppressing tumor invasion [321].

Overall, significant progress has been made regarding the use of nanomedicine to overcome the inherent pharmacological limitations of curcumin, including its low aqueous solubility, rapid systemic metabolism, and poor gastrointestinal stability. These delivery systems have substantially improved the therapeutic utilization and efficacy of the compound. However, research focused on improving brain‐targeted delivery efficiency remains relatively limited. There is an urgent need to develop specialized carriers with higher drug‐loading capacities and enhanced delivery efficiency [322]. Furthermore, many existing platforms are designed exclusively for curcumin as a monotherapy, which may partially account for their limited therapeutic efficacy. Combining curcumin with other natural compounds or drugs could represent a promising strategy for enhancing therapeutic outcomes. Additionally, the selection of different administration routes can significantly influence the therapeutic efficacy of curcumin by modulating its pharmacokinetic profile. Consequently, the development of disease‐specific drug‐loading and administration strategies is critical for maximizing the bioavailability and neuroprotective potential of curcumin [8]. Table 1 summarizes the strategies employed for the generation of drug delivery systems aimed at enhancing curcumin bioavailability.

5 Therapeutic Effects of Curcumin in the Treatment of Multiple diseases

The development of nanoformulations has significantly enhanced the bioavailability and stability of curcumin, enabling both controlled release and targeted delivery. These advancements ensure that curcumin maintains effective systemic and cerebral concentrations, thereby improving therapeutic outcomes. Overall, preclinical in vivo and in vitro studies have confirmed the positive effects of curcumin in ameliorating neurological disorders, cancer, cardiovascular diseases, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. A deeper understanding of the mechanisms underlying the effects of curcumin is crucial for the development of treatment strategies and its integration into clinical trials. Table 2 summarizes the therapeutic effects observed in both in vivo and in vitro studies.

TABLE 2 Preclinical application of curcumin in the treatment of various diseases.

5.1 Neurological Disorders

5.1.1 Alzheimer's Disease

AD is a progressive neurodegenerative disorder primarily characterized by the impairment of memory and cognitive functions. It typically manifests as short‐term memory loss, cognitive deficits, alterations in behavior and mood, and executive dysfunction. The pathological hallmarks of AD include the formation of Aβ plaques and the accumulation of tau protein, which forms neurofibrillary tangles in the brain [323]. The misfolding of Aβ peptides is closely associated with the pathogenesis of neurological disorders, including AD. This process contributes to elevated calcium influx, heightened oxidative stress, cellular inflammation, and the dysregulated expression of ion channel and receptor genes [324]. Intracellular tau tangles involve the hyperphosphorylation of tau protein, resulting in the destabilization of neurofilament microtubules, the disruption of cellular structure and transport systems, and the impairment of normal neuronal function. Accordingly, the elimination of Aβ to reduce its cerebral deposition, along with the inhibition of tau protein hyperphosphorylation, represents a critical strategy in anti‐AD drug development [325].

5.1.1.1 In Vitro Studies

Curcumin is recognized for its potent antioxidant properties. In vitro studies have demonstrated that curcumin protects neuronal cells against Aβ‐induced neurotoxicity. For instance, the pretreatment of PC12 cells with curcumin has been shown to ameliorate neuronal damage by reducing oxidative stress, attenuating Aβ‐induced DNA damage, lowering intracellular calcium levels, and inhibiting tau protein hyperphosphorylation [326]. Activated microglia may be important mediators of Aβ production and can promote the release of cytokines, which enhances the immune response in the brains of patients with AD [327]. Research has indicated that curcumin can suppress the expression of IL‐6, IL‐1β, and TNF‐α, as well as inhibit the phosphorylation of ERK1/2 and P38 in Aβ‐activated microglia, thereby attenuating the microglia‐mediated inflammatory response in the brain [328]. Moreover, Zhang et al. demonstrated that supplementation with curcumin nanoparticles significantly reduced the expression of the proinflammatory factors TNF‐α (51.89 ± 8.61%) and IL‐6 (38.52 ± 4.97%) in LPS‐treated BV‐2 microglial cells [329]. One study established an AD cell model by transfecting APPswe and BACE1‐mycHis plasmids into SH‐SY5Y cells. Curcumin treatment inhibited GSK‐3β expression in the transfected cells, thereby activating the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway. These findings provide a potential therapeutic strategy for AD [225].

5.1.1.2 In Vivo Studies

Animal studies have demonstrated that, in Aβ‐induced AD model mice, curcumin restores damaged neuronal populations and enhances cognitive function and spatial memory. These effects are associated with the inhibition of cellular inflammatory responses, reduced MDA levels, increased SOD activity, and enhanced AMPK phosphorylation [330]. Research has also shown that curcumin reverses AD‐like alterations induced by aluminum chloride in mice. These changes include reduced AChE activity, attenuation of hippocampal oxidative stress, and the suppression of neuroinflammation, characterized by a decrease in the release of TNF‐α and IL‐6 and a concomitant increase in IL‐4 secretion [331]. Notably, the administration of low‐dose curcumin was particularly effective in decreasing both the Aβ content and the plaque load in the brain [332]. It has also been reported that curcumin ameliorated the spatial memory impairment induced by Aβ1−42​ in AD model rats, achieved through the inhibition of glial fibrillary acidic protein expression and the suppression of astrocyte proliferation in the hippocampal CA1 region [333]. Furthermore, Jearjaroen et al. investigated the effects of oral hexahydrocurcumin (HHC) administration (40 mg/kg for 4 weeks) on cognitive dysfunction induced by two‐vessel occlusion in rats [334]. They found that HHC promotes nuclear factor erythroid 2‐related factor 2 (Nrf2) activation, thereby alleviating oxidative stress, downregulating Aβ production, and reducing the levels of phosphorylated tau protein, thereby protecting neurons from injury and ultimately improving dementia in the animals.

Combined, the above findings suggest that the therapeutic effects of curcumin in AD are mediated via the clearance of Aβ deposits, decreasing tau protein phosphorylation levels, and protecting neurons by suppressing neuroinflammation and oxidative stress.

5.1.2 Depression

Depression is a mental disorder characterized by persistent feelings of sadness, diminished interest in normal activities, and physiological dysfunction. Its symptoms include difficulty concentrating, changes in appetite, and disrupted sleep patterns [335]. In severe cases, depression can lead to self‐denial and cognitive distortions, which can ultimately result in suicidal behavior [336]. Numerous studies have indicated that experiencing depression significantly increases the risk of developing other neurological diseases, including stroke, epilepsy, and AD [337]. The etiology of depression is multifaceted. While the mechanisms underlying this condition remain incompletely understood, it is widely accepted that its development arises from the complex interplay among multiple components, including psychological factors, genetic susceptibility, social stress, and comorbidities, among others [338]. Widely recognized mechanisms underlying depression include the monoamine neurotransmitter hypothesis [339], hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal axis hyperactivity [340], neuroinflammation [341], oxidative stress [342], and gut–brain axis dysregulation [343]. Research into these mechanisms holds promise for providing effective methods for the treatment of depression.

5.1.2.1 In Vitro Studies

A growing body of evidence suggests that the MAPK signaling pathway, particularly the ERK subfamily, plays a critical role in the pathogenesis of depression. Research indicates that ERK signaling is markedly decreased in the prefrontal cortex and hippocampus of patients with depression [344]. Gao et al. assessed the effect of curcumin using a PC12 cell model of depression and found that curcumin significantly enhanced the viability of PC12 cells that had been exposed to corticosterone. Furthermore, western blot analysis indicated that curcumin effectively and dose‐dependently suppressed corticosterone‐induced ERK1/2 phosphorylation in the treated cells, thereby mitigating corticosterone‐induced cytotoxicity [345]. The N‐methyl‐d‐aspartate receptor (NMDAR) is a well‐documented crucial binding partner for CaMKII within the postsynaptic density. The binding of phosphorylated CaMKII to NMDAR leads to the activation of a variety of postsynaptic proteins involved in memory processes [346]. An in vitro study reported that curcumin pretreatment effectively attenuates cortisol‐induced neuronal cell death in hippocampal neurons, downregulates NMDAR expression, and reduces excessive CaMKII phosphorylation, thereby safeguarding neurons from cortisol‐induced neurotoxicity [347].

5.1.2.2 In Vivo Studies

In a rat model of depression induced by chronic restraint stress, curcumin treatment significantly ameliorated depressive‐like behaviors. This therapeutic intervention also led to an improvement in memory function impairment, likely through the regulation of cortisol levels and the positive modulation of BDNF expression in the hippocampus [348]. Similarly, Fan et al. reported that curcumin effectively reversed the reduction in sucrose consumption and the increase in serum corticosterone levels induced by CUMS in rats. Curcumin also enhanced the levels of cAMP in the brain and reversed the decline in serotonin (5‐HT) contents, thereby exerting a protective effect against stress‐induced depressive‐like behaviors in the animals [349]. Another study reported that curcumin effectively alleviated LPS‐induced depressive‐like behaviors in mice by inhibiting microglial activation and suppressing the release of proinflammatory cytokines, including IL‐1β, TNF‐α, iNOS, and COX‐2. Additionally, curcumin attenuated the activation of the NF‐κB signaling pathway in the hippocampus and prefrontal cortex [350]. BDNF plays a key protective role in the pathophysiology of depression by promoting neuronal plasticity and survival, synaptic growth and differentiation, and the production of neurotransmitters [351]. Animal studies have demonstrated that prolonged curcumin treatment reverses the decrease in sucrose preference observed in rats subjected to CUMS. Additionally, curcumin upregulates BDNF expression in the hippocampal region. The ERK signaling pathway is highly responsive to stress and plays an essential role in regulating cognitive and emotional processes [352, 353]. Research has shown that curcumin can inhibit ERK phosphorylation, enhance cognitive function, and improve learning and memory capabilities in rats. These data suggest that curcumin exerts its antidepressant effects by upregulating BDNF expression and modulating the ERK signaling pathway [199]. In a study involving a rat model of depression, curcumin administration significantly reduced immobility time in the forced swimming test. Additionally, curcumin treatment effectively reversed the decreases in 5‐HT, DA, and NA levels in the hippocampus and prefrontal cortex of rats subjected to bilateral olfactory bulbectomy, thereby ameliorating depressive‐like behaviors in the animals [354]. Excessive ROS accumulation in the brain can severely impair neuronal function [355]. Rinwa and Kumar exposed mice to CUMS and reported that supplementation with curcumin effectively prevented oxidative damage by increasing the expression of GSH and CAT and reducing the concentrations of MDA and nitrite [356]. Similarly, Zhang et al. demonstrated that in rats with CUMS‐induced depression, curcumin pretreatment increased synaptic density and the surface density of amygdala neurons, while also restoring PSD‐95 and BDNF levels, resulting in significant antidepressant effects [357].

Collectively, these findings indicate that curcumin elevates monoamine neurotransmitter levels, upregulates the expression of BDNF, and promotes neuronal plasticity and survival. In addition, curcumin inhibits the activation of microglia and the release of proinflammatory cytokines, which may help protect neurons and potentially delay the onset and progression of depression.

5.1.3 Ischemic Stroke

Ischemic stroke is an acute cerebrovascular disorder typically caused by the sudden occlusion of cerebral blood vessels, resulting in the interruption of blood supply to the brain. This leads to ischemic lesions in the affected arterial regions, manifesting as neuronal damage or cerebral edema [358]. The clinical manifestations of ischemic stroke primarily include facial nerve paralysis, language impairment, limb weakness, and sensory disturbances [359]. Considering the constrained regenerative potential of the adult brain and the frequently irreversible nature of neuronal injury, the prompt reperfusion of ischemic regions is essential for ameliorating the neuronal damage elicited by ischemic stroke [360]. Notably, cerebral I/R injury following the restoration of blood flow can initiate a cascade of detrimental reactions, ultimately resulting in cellular injury and inflammatory responses within the affected tissue [361].

5.1.3.1 In Vitro Studies

Damage to white matter is a crucial aspect of the pathophysiological processes of ischemic stroke, potentially leading to long‐term sensorimotor deficits and cognitive impairment [362]. Neuroinflammation plays a significant role in the damage and repair of white matter. It ultimately contributes to secondary brain injury primarily through the recruitment of peripheral immune cells, the activation of glial cells, and the release of proinflammatory cytokines [363]. In a study involving primary mouse microglia, curcumin treatment significantly suppressed the activation of NF‐κB and the NLRP3 inflammasome, thereby diminishing the expression of the pyroptosis‐related proteins GSDMD and Caspase‐1 and the proinflammatory cytokines IL‐18 and IL‐1β [63]. Xie et al. explored the effects of curcumin on ischemic stroke using N2a cells subjected to oxygen–glucose deprivation/reperfusion (OGD/R). Treatment with 25 µmol/L curcumin significantly increased the protein levels of BCL‐2 while reducing the expression of BAX and Caspase‐3, thereby suppressing mitochondrial apoptosis. This inhibitory effect on apoptosis highlights the neuroprotective potential of curcumin in the context of ischemic stroke [47]. Yang et al. reported that curcumin‐loaded gelatin nanoparticles (Cur@GAR NPs) protected SH‐SY5Y cells against hydrogen peroxide‐induced oxidative stress and consequent cell death. Notably, these effects were attributed to the potent antioxidant properties of curcumin [364]. A recent study revealed that curcumin inhibits the LPS‐ and interferon‐gamma (IFN‐γ)‐induced M1 polarization in BV2 microglial cells, thereby reducing the expression of proinflammatory cytokines such as TNF‐α, IL‐1β, and IL‐6 [365]. Meanwhile, Lan et al. established an OGD/R model using BV2 and TH22 cells to simulate I/R injury. Pretreatment with curcumin resulted in the downregulation of iNOS protein levels and the upregulation of the M2 microglial markers CD206 and Arg‐1, leading to a significant decline in the release of proinflammatory factors. These data indicated that curcumin exerts significant anti‐inflammatory effects by promoting the polarization of microglia from an M1 to an M2 phenotype. In addition, curcumin modulates neuroinflammatory responses by inhibiting the NF‐κB/MAPK signaling pathway, thereby alleviating PANoptotic neuronal death [366]. Similarly, curcumin was reported to inhibit apoptosis, reduce ROS generation and LDH levels, and restore the damage induced by OGD/R in brain microvascular endothelial cells in vitro [367].

5.1.3.2 In Vivo Studies

In MCAO model mice, combination treatment with curcumin and human umbilical cord‐derived mesenchymal stem cells (hUC‐MSCs) led to a reduction in the cerebral infarction area and brain edema. This combined therapy afforded neuroprotection by reducing ROS levels and downregulating the expression of oxidative mediators and proinflammatory cytokines, effects that were mediated through the Nrf2 pathway [368]. Another group investigated the effects of curcumin on cerebral I/R injury using a similar MCAO model in rats. Compared with untreated MCAO animals, those administered curcumin presented significant reductions in cerebral infarction volume, neurological dysfunction, and brain edema. Furthermore, curcumin downregulated the expression of inflammation‐related markers (NF‐κB, MMP‐9, and ICAM‐1) as well as that of the apoptosis‐related marker Caspase‐9. This implies that the neuroprotective effects of curcumin against cerebral I/R injury may be associated with its anti‐inflammatory and antiapoptotic properties [369]. It was also shown that curcumin nanoparticles improved motor function and muscle strength in rats following ischemic stroke. These nanoparticles effectively enhanced the activity of antioxidant enzymes, including SOD, GSH, and CAT, thereby reducing oxidative stress and providing neuroprotection [370]. Additionally, in mice with MCAO‐induced stroke, tetrahydrocurcumin (THC) treatment significantly improved functional coordination, while simultaneously reducing cerebral infarction volume, brain edema, and microvascular leakage in the brain parenchyma. Furthermore, this curcumin metabolite restored the levels of homocysteine metabolic enzymes, alleviated mitochondrial dysfunction—including mitochondrial oxidative stress and mitochondrial DNA methylation—and normalized MMP‐9 activity in the brain, thereby protecting the mice against I/R injury [371]. Mo et al. simulated I/R injury using a MCAO/reperfusion (MCAO/R) model. The authors reported that curcumin reversed the MCAO/R‐induced increase in calcium ion concentrations and the disruption of the blood–brain barrier, while also protecting against cerebral I/R injury through PKC signaling [372]. Another study demonstrated that curcumin alleviated MCAO‐induced oxidative stress in rats by activating the AKT/Nrf2 signaling pathway. Moreover, curcumin significantly decreased the cerebral infarction area and minimized ischemic neuronal damage, thus exerting neuroprotective effects [373].

Combined, observations from in vitro, in vivo, and clinical trials suggest that curcumin mitigates neuronal ischemic injury and inhibits the progression of ischemic stroke by promoting microglial polarization, suppressing neuroinflammation, reducing oxidative stress, inhibiting cell apoptosis, and alleviating mitochondrial dysfunction.

5.1.4 Parkinson's Disease

PD is a complex neurodegenerative disorder, pathologically defined by the loss of dopaminergic neurons in the substantia nigra pars compacta of the midbrain, the presence of Lewy bodies in neurons, and the aggregation of cytoplasmic inclusions consisting of insoluble α‐Syn [374]. Patients additionally present with progressive impairment of voluntary motor control, characterized by a range of symptoms such as tremor, bradykinesia, difficulty in walking, autonomic dysfunction, and cognitive decline [375]. Furthermore, the prevalence of PD steadily increases with age [376]. Factors such as oxidative stress, mitochondrial dysfunction, neuroinflammation, and deficiencies in neurotrophic factors all significantly influence the onset and progression of the disease [377].

5.1.4.1 In Vitro Studies

It is increasingly clear that impairment of the autophagy–lysosome pathway is a significant contributor to the pathogenesis of PD. Transcription factor EB (TFEB), the master regulator of this pathway, has emerged as a promising therapeutic target for this condition. The treatment of N2a cells with the curcumin derivative E4 (1 µM) for 24 h resulted in increased TFEB phosphorylation and reduced α‐Syn protein levels, suggesting that this treatment exerted a neuroprotective effect [378]. Pirunkaset et al. [379] established a model of PD in human neuroblastoma (SH‐SY5Y) cells via rotenone treatment and examined the ROS scavenging activity and potential neuroprotective effects of the diacetyldicurcumin–manganese complex (DiAc–Cp–Mn). The addition of this complex significantly diminished intracellular ROS release, boosted the activities of SOD and CAT, and suppressed the expression of the neuroinflammatory factors iNOS, KEAP1, and IL‐1β. Similarly, Wu et al. established a PD cell model by treating SH‐SY5Y cells with MPTP. Curcumin treatment significantly increased both the survival of dopaminergic neurons and the clearance of intracellular α‐Syn relative to the model group. Notably, these neuroprotective effects were mediated through the upregulation of TFEB and the autophagy‐related proteins LAMP2A and LC3‐II [380]. In a separate study, the authors established a cell model of PD in SH‐SY5Y cells via the deletion of PINK1. Cells pretreated with curcumin displayed enhanced viability, improved mitochondrial dysfunction—characterized by increased MMP and mitochondrial respiration—and reduced levels of apoptosis [381]. It has also been reported that curcumin inhibits 6‐OHDA‐induced apoptosis in PC12 cells, suppresses cytotoxicity, upregulates the expression of the autophagy markers LC3‐II and Beclin‐1, and exerts neuroprotective effects via the inhibition of the AKT/mTOR signaling pathway [382].

5.1.4.2 In Vivo Studies

In mouse models of PD, curcumin has demonstrated potential in mitigating the death of dopaminergic neurons and the loss of dopaminergic axons in the striatum. Specifically, research has shown that the intraperitoneal injection of curcumin (50 mg/kg/day) for 5 consecutive days inhibits JNK phosphorylation and modulates the protein expression of BCL‐2 and BAX, resulting in significant neuroprotective effects following the onset of PD [383]. Furthermore, curcumin can diminish α‐Syn accumulation in a 6‐OHDA‐induced rat model of PD as well as inhibit AKT/mTOR signaling pathway‐mediated autophagy. These findings underscore the potential value of curcumin in the treatment of PD [382]. In MPTP‐induced PD model mice, curcumin effectively ameliorated motor deficits, suppressed glial cell activation, and reduced α‐Syn aggregation. Meanwhile, gut microbiota analysis revealed that curcumin significantly increased the abundance of Lactobacillaceae and Turicibacteraceae while decreasing that of Bifidobacteriaceae and Staphylococcaceae. Additionally, curcumin treatment elevated the serum levels of tyrosine, methionine, sarcosine, and creatine [384]. Consistent with these findings, in mice with MPTP‐induced PD, curcumin was reported to alleviate motor deficits and dopaminergic neuron loss, improve intestinal dysbiosis, and restore short‐chain fatty acid levels [385]. Furthermore, in the 6‐OHDA‐induced rat PD model, curcumin increased the survival of tyrosine hydroxylase (TH)‐positive striatal fibers and neurons in the substantia nigra pars compacta, elevated dopamine levels, and reduced abnormal rotational behavior [386]. Given that microglia‐mediated neuroinflammation is closely associated with the pathophysiology of PD, the anti‐inflammatory properties of curcumin likely underlie its significant neuroprotective effects in this disorder. In a rotenone‐induced PD mouse model, curcumin alleviated rotenone‐induced behavioral deficits, mitigated the loss of dopamine neurons in the substantia nigra, suppressed microglial activation, and reduced mitochondrial fission. The neuroprotection afforded by this polyphenol in this model was attributed to its inhibitory effects on the NF‐κB signaling pathway, the NLRP3 inflammasome, and the neuroinflammation mediated by proinflammatory cytokines (IL‐1β, IL‐6, and IL‐18) [387]. In other PD animal models, curcumin has been demonstrated to effectively suppress the upregulation of the NF‐κB signaling pathway and inhibit the expression of proinflammatory cytokines (TNF‐α, IL‐1β, and IL‐6), iNOS, and key regulators of the intrinsic apoptotic pathway (BAX, BCL‐2, Caspase‐3, and Caspase‐9). Furthermore, curcumin treatment reportedly boosted the levels of GSH and oxidoreductases and prevented the aggregation of α‐Syn in dopaminergic neurons [388].

In summary, studies to date have shown that curcumin mitigates PD progression by inhibiting α‐Syn protein aggregation, suppressing the expression of neuroinflammatory factors, alleviating oxidative stress, and modulating the gut microbiota. These multifaceted mechanisms together contribute to its neuroprotective effects.

5.1.5 Multiple Sclerosis

MS is a disease characterized primarily by inflammation and demyelination in the CNS [389]. This condition typically manifests between the ages of 20 and 40 years and shows a higher prevalence in females. The clinical presentations of MS are varied, which complicates its diagnosis. Imaging studies have revealed varying degrees of inflammatory lesions in both the gray and white matter of the brains of affected patients, accompanied by oligodendrocyte death and myelin destruction [390]. In the early stages of the disease, myelin‐specific reactive T cells and peripheral immune cells, such as macrophages, infiltrate the CNS through the compromised blood–brain barrier [391]. T helper 17 (Th17) cells play a significant immunological role in the pathophysiology of MS. Once activated, Th17 cells aberrantly secrete IL‐17 and IL‐22, cross the blood–brain barrier, exacerbate barrier damage, and interfere with neuronal signal transmission by activating inflammatory responses within the CNS. A variety of immunosuppressive and immunomodulatory therapies are widely employed in clinical settings to modulate immune responses, acting through multiple mechanisms. However, these treatments often exhibit limited efficacy and are frequently associated with side effects. Curcumin has demonstrated significant efficacy against various inflammatory diseases, including neurological disorders. In this section, we explore the potential therapeutic effects of curcumin in MS based on both in vitro and in vivo studies.

5.1.5.1 In Vitro Studies

Liu et al. isolated CD4+ T cells from mouse spleens and induced their differentiation into Th1, Th17, and regulatory T cell (Treg) subtypes. Following treatment with varying concentrations of curcumin, they induced apoptosis in these cells by downregulating the expression of the antiapoptotic protein BCL‐2 and upregulating that of the proapoptotic proteins BAX and Caspase‐3, thereby providing a basis for the use of curcumin as a therapeutic agent for MS [392]. In LPS‐stimulated BV‐2 microglial cells, curcumin alleviated inflammatory responses by inhibiting the AXL/JAK2/STAT3 signaling pathway [393]. Astrocytes, the most abundant glial cell type in the brain, play a fundamental role in the maintenance of CNS homeostasis and are closely related to MS when activated in response to stimuli. Furthermore, curcumin treatment was demonstrated to dose‐dependently inhibit MMP9 activity, IL‐6 secretion, and MCP‐1 transcription in LPS‐activated astrocytes, thereby mitigating inflammatory responses and exerting neuroprotective effects [394]. In an in vitro experiment, the treatment of activated T cells with curcumin inhibited the IL‐12‐induced phosphorylation of JAK2 and STAT3, resulting in diminished Th1 cell differentiation, suggesting that curcumin holds potential for application in MS treatment [395].

5.1.5.2 In Vivo Studies

In experimental autoimmune encephalomyelitis (EAE) and copper chloride (CPZ)‐induced MS mouse models, curcumin treatment significantly reduced the infiltration of inflammatory cells and demyelination in the spinal cord, accompanied by an improvement in neurological function. Furthermore, curcumin alleviated oxidative stress, which ultimately promoted remyelination, improved behavioral abnormalities, and repaired tissue damage, thus enhancing MS outcomes [396]. Moreover, curcumin was reported to mitigate EAE‐induced cognitive and motor impairments, oxidative stress, and neuroinflammation through the activation of the AMPK/SIRT1 signaling pathway [397]. Additionally, in CPZ‐induced MS mouse models, curcumin exerted neuroprotective effects by downregulating AXL expression and alleviating astrocyte‐mediated inflammatory responses [398]. In the EAE model, curcumin inhibited the expression of the AXL receptor, thereby blocking the phosphorylation of JAK2 and STAT3, reducing microglia‐mediated neuroinflammation, and alleviating neurological damage in mice [393]. In one study, the authors encapsulated curcumin in nanoparticles to enhance its bioavailability and efficacy following administration via intraperitoneal injection. The evaluation of this formulation in MS animal models revealed that preventive treatment at the onset of EAE symptoms mitigated disease progression and delayed relapse. This intervention also suppressed proinflammatory factor expression in the spinal cord, enhanced anti‐inflammatory and antioxidant effects, reduced demyelination severity, and increased the numbers of neurotrophic factors and progenitor cells involved in myelin repair [399]. Separately, investigators employed high‐density lipoprotein peptide‐phospholipid scaffolds (HPPS) as carriers to encapsulate curcumin (HPPS–Cur). This delivery method effectively reduced the infiltration of inflammatory monocytes across the blood–brain barrier, inhibited microglial proliferation, and restricted the infiltration of other effector immune cells, resulting in a 70% reduction in EAE incidence. This effect was attributed to the immunomodulatory action of HPPS–Cur on inflammatory monocytes, which inhibited NF‐κB activation and downregulated the expression of adhesion and migration‐related molecules [400]. Saravanan et al. demonstrated that curcumin alleviated EAE by modulating Th1/Th17 cell differentiation, inhibiting IL‐12/IL‐23, and upregulating Th2/Treg responses, highlighting the significance of curcumin in the treatment of MS [401]. Furthermore, results from an EAE SJL/J mouse model showed that curcumin alleviated spinal cord inflammatory infiltration and demyelination via the inhibition of IL‐12‐mediated JAK/STAT signaling pathways [395].

In conclusion, curcumin exhibits the potential to suppress neuroinflammation in EAE models, thereby improving the prognosis of MS.

5.1.6 Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis

ALS is a neurodegenerative disease characterized by the progressive loss of upper and lower motor neurons, leading to muscle weakness, paralysis, and ultimately respiratory failure within 3–5 years of symptom onset [402]. The global incidence of ALS is approximately 1.75 per 100,000 person‐years, making it the most common motor neuron disease among adults. The pathogenesis of ALS involves a complex interplay between genetic susceptibility—particularly mutations in SOD1, TAR DNA‐binding protein, and fused in sarcoma (FUS)—and environmental factors. This interplay results in multiple pathological mechanisms, including protein misfolding, oxidative stress, and neuroinflammation. Despite extensive research, treatment options remain limited. Recent studies have confirmed that curcumin can serve as an adjunctive therapy to slow the progression of ALS, offering promising avenues for the treatment of this condition.

5.1.6.1 In Vitro Studies

Bhatia et al. evaluated the protective effects of curcumin nanoparticles against the toxicity of SOD1 aggregates in human monocytic THP‐1 cells. These nanoparticles altered the aggregation process by binding to protein aggregates, leading to the formation of smaller, more disordered, and less toxic aggregates. This structural modification significantly reduced the toxicity to THP‐1 cells, providing a potential direction for the application of curcumin as a therapeutic agent for ALS [403]. Additionally, researchers identified a novel potent polyphenolic compound, bis‐demethoxycurcumin, which effectively reduced the incidence of ALS in experimental models by preventing the abnormal misfolding and aggregation of SOD1 into amyloid‐like aggregates [404]. Moreover, in a motor neuron‐like cell line (NSC‐34), curcumin derivatives reversed TDP‐43‐induced action potential and sodium channel dysfunction. These effects were achieved by alleviating oxidative stress and improving mitochondrial dysfunction, without evidence of cytotoxicity [405].

5.1.6.2 In Vivo Studies

Hajime et al. evaluated the effects of curcumin derivatives on the H46R mutant SOD1 transgenic mouse model of ALS. They revealed that the administration of these derivatives significantly slowed the progression of motor dysfunction and reduced the accumulation of highly aggregated SOD1, further protecting neurons in the spinal cord of the mice [406]. Furthermore, Cur–SLNs have shown potential as an estrogen substitute therapy in the Prp–TDP–43A315T transgenic mouse model of ALS. Cur–SLNs improved the survival rate of ALS mice through the modulation of the enzymatic activities of CYP19A1 and CYP3A4, thereby suppressing TDP‐43 aggregation [407].

In summary, these findings indicate that curcumin significantly improves ALS symptoms both in vitro and in vivo, providing a novel therapeutic direction for enhancing ALS prognosis.

5.2 Cancer

5.2.1 Colon Cancer

Colorectal cancer, a prevalent malignancy of the digestive system, exhibits a high global incidence and ranks among the primary causes of cancer mortality [408]. While primarily affecting middle‐aged and older populations, evolving lifestyle factors indicate a discernible trend toward earlier onset. Clinical presentations of colorectal cancer are strongly correlated with tumor location; right‐sided colon cancers often manifest as anemia, fatigue, and nonspecific abdominal discomfort, whereas left‐sided colon cancers are more frequently associated with altered bowel habits, rectal bleeding, and stool caliber reduction. Diagnostic imaging may identify colonic wall lesions of varying sizes, characterized by mucosal erosion and mural infiltration. During disease progression, immune cells within the tumor microenvironment, including tumor‐associated macrophages and Tregs, facilitate immune escape, thereby fostering tumor proliferation and metastasis [409]. Current clinical practice employs comprehensive treatment modalities such as surgical resection, chemotherapy, radiotherapy, and immunotherapy; however, the effectiveness of these interventions is frequently constrained by drug‐induced toxicity and acquired resistance. Research has indicated that curcumin holds promise as a therapeutic agent for diverse gastrointestinal malignancies [410]. Consequently, this section will provide a systematic review of curcumin's potential therapeutic applications in both in vitro and in vivo investigations of colorectal cancer.

5.2.1.1 In Vitro Studies

Using CT26 and RKO colorectal cancer cell lines, Zhou et al. demonstrated curcumin's inhibitory effect on cell viability (IC50 values of 23.52–13.62 and 26.3–14.22 µM, respectively), its induction of apoptosis, and its causation of G2 phase arrest, utilizing cell viability assays and apoptosis analysis [411]. Guéguinou et al. [412] employing MSI colorectal cancer cell lines like HCT116 and methods such as mitochondrial function assays, concluded that curcumin induces apoptosis by inhibiting the mitochondrial sodium‐calcium‐lithium exchanger, resulting in mitochondrial calcium overload, elevated ROS, membrane depolarization, and diminished ATP production. Wang et al. [413] utilized human colorectal cancer cell lines, including HCT116, and employed techniques like noncoding RNA regulation and signaling pathway analysis to establish that curcumin inhibits CRC cell proliferation, migration, and metastasis by modulating noncoding RNA to downregulate heparanase expression, suppress the IL‐6/STAT5 signaling pathway, and disrupt the tumor microenvironment and epithelial–mesenchymal transition (EMT) process. Elbadawy et al. [414] employed human colorectal cancer cell lines and methods including ERK phosphorylation and tumor stem cell marker detection to further show that amorphous curcumin inhibits CRC by suppressing ERK phosphorylation and its downstream c‐MYC and cyclin‐D1 signaling, reducing tumor stem cell markers CD44, LGR5, and CD133, and inducing cell cycle arrest and apoptosis.

5.2.1.2 In Vivo Studies

In a mouse xenograft model of MSI colorectal cancer, Guéguinou et al. [412] observed that curcumin impedes tumor progression and augments apoptosis by downregulating the NCLX target. This mechanism induces mitochondrial calcium overload, elevates ROS, causes membrane depolarization, and reduces ATP levels, as evidenced by mitochondrial function assays. The study also indicated a significant correlation between elevated NCLX expression and recurrence risk in MSI patients. Elbadawy et al. [414] employing a mouse xenograft model derived from human colorectal cancer organoids, utilized techniques such as ERK phosphorylation and tumor stem cell marker detection. Their findings showed that amorphous curcumin suppresses ERK phosphorylation and its downstream c‐MYC and cyclin‐D1 signaling pathways, leading to reduced expression of tumor stem cell markers (CD44, LGR5, CD133). This inhibition results in cell cycle arrest, apoptosis, necrotic lesions, and an overall antitumor effect. A systematic review of 22 animal studies by Jia et al. which analyzed colorectal cancer animal models comprehensively, corroborated that curcumin significantly reduces tumor volume and weight. Its mechanisms include modulating gut microbiota, mitigating intestinal inflammation, inhibiting cell proliferation, promoting apoptosis, preventing metastasis, and enhancing chemotherapy sensitivity. Notably, oral administration of curcumin demonstrated superior antitumor efficacy compared with other routes [415].
