The therapeutic efficacy of curcumin in colorectal cancer, as supported by the cited research, is principally mediated by several mechanisms: suppression of cell viability, induction of cell cycle arrest and apoptosis, modulation of mitochondrial calcium homeostasis and ROS metabolism, regulation of noncoding RNAs and associated signaling pathways, and reduction of tumor stem cell markers. Additionally, its capacity to inhibit tumor progression and improve chemosensitivity has been corroborated in in vivo experimental models.

5.2.2 Breast Cancer

Breast cancer, a highly prevalent malignancy in women, ranks globally as the most frequent cancer incidence and a primary cause of cancer‐related mortality [416]. While predominantly affecting individuals over 40 years of age, lifestyle shifts are contributing to an increasing incidence in younger demographics. The clinical presentation of breast cancer is varied, with early, nonspecific symptoms complicating early diagnosis. Examination via imaging and histopathology identifies malignant proliferative lesions of varying severity within breast tissue, modulated by molecular subtypes linked to hormone receptor status (ER/PR/HER2) [417]. The tumor microenvironment exhibits enhanced infiltration of tumor‐associated macrophages polarized to an M2 phenotype, fostering an immunosuppressive milieu that facilitates tumor advancement. Current clinical management employs surgery, chemoradiotherapy, endocrine therapy, and targeted therapy; however, therapeutic resistance and treatment‐induced toxicity persist as considerable clinical obstacles. Research has substantiated curcumin's cardioprotective role in adjuvant breast cancer therapy and its capacity to ameliorate tamoxifen‐induced hepatotoxicity. Consequently, this review will investigate curcumin's prospective therapeutic utility in breast cancer management.

5.2.2.1 In Vitro Studies

In a study utilizing the MDA‐MB‐231 triple‐negative breast cancer cell line and whole‐genome methylation sequencing, Nirgude et al. [418] identified that curcumin derivatives ST08 and ST09 induce methylation changes in promoter regions, including the HOX family and genes such as CACNAH1 and CDH23. These modifications were shown to modulate the ECM signaling pathway and cell cycle pathway, respectively, leading to antiproliferative, proapoptotic, and antimigratory effects. Hasan et al. [419] explored the use of CS‐coated salmon lecithin‐derived nanoliposomes for curcumin delivery in the MCF‐7 human breast cancer cell line. This nanocarrier achieved an 88% drug loading efficiency and, at 20 µM, reduced cell growth fourfold compared with controls. The enhanced antiproliferative activity was attributed to the nanocarrier's improved curcumin bioavailability. Pourmadadi et al. [420] developed a curcumin‐loaded Fe3O4/CS/agarose dual‐emulsion hydrogel nanocarrier, also using the MCF‐7 cell line. This nanocomposite demonstrated sustained and pH‐responsive release properties, promoting a high rate of cell apoptosis, with its antitumor efficacy linked to the enhanced delivery of curcumin by the hydrogel nanocarrier. Romero et al. [421] investigated the T47D human breast cancer cell line using carbon dots synthesized from curcumin as a precursor, in conjunction with blue light irradiation. Under irradiation, these carbon dots functioned as both photosensitizers and photothermal agents, eliciting significant photodynamic cytotoxic effects through the generation of ROS and localized heating (40–50°C) [421].

5.2.2.2 In Vivo Studies

In a study employing a 4T1 tumor‐bearing mouse model, Zhao et al. investigated a curcumin‐mediated photodynamic therapy strategy. Their findings indicate that this therapy generates ROS, leading to the inactivation of the mitochondrial outer membrane protein TOM70. This disruption impedes MIC60 transport into mitochondria, causing significant mitochondrial damage and effectively inhibiting breast cancer progression [422]. In a NOD scid gamma mouse model engrafted with MDA‐MB‐231 cells, Wang et al. administered luteolin (10 mg/kg/day) and curcumin (20 mg/kg/day) via intraperitoneal injection every other day for 5 weeks. Through transcriptomic sequencing, Western blotting, and histopathological analysis, the study revealed that the combination of luteolin and curcumin synergistically activated the Type I interferon signaling pathway, attenuated the TGF‐β signaling pathway, and downregulated c‐Myc and Notch1 protein levels, leading to effective suppression of tumor growth in triple‐negative breast cancer [423].Further research by Li et al. using a triple‐negative breast cancer mouse model (4T1 cell implantation) and curcumin intervention focused on the SLC7A11/NF‐κB/TGF‐β signaling pathway. They demonstrated that curcumene inhibits tumor growth by downregulating this pathway, reversing EMT, reducing lung metastasis, and inducing ferroptosis, characterized by decreased MMP and increased accumulation of lipid ROS [424].

Curcumin and its analogs exhibit antitumor activity in breast cancer through epigenetic alterations, improved nanodelivery and photoresponsive characteristics, modulation of ECM and cell cycle pathways, and suppression of Hedgehog/Gli1 and SLC7A11/NF‐κB/TGF‐β signaling. These mechanisms collectively inhibit proliferation, migration, and EMT, while inducing apoptosis and ferroptosis, thereby demonstrating potent anticancer effects across in vitro and in vivo experimental models.

5.2.3 Skin Cancer

Skin cancer, a prevalent malignant neoplasm globally, leads all cancers in incidence and is a primary driver of dermatological mortality [425]. The disease predominantly affects individuals over 50 years of age, especially those with prior ultraviolet (UV) radiation exposure, who exhibit a markedly elevated risk. Clinical presentations of skin cancer are varied, potentially manifesting in early stages as new cutaneous growths, alterations in pigmented nevi, ulcerations, or nodules. Advanced disease can involve local ulceration, hemorrhage, and regional lymph node metastasis, complicating early detection [426]. Imaging and histopathological analyses indicate varying degrees of malignant proliferation within cutaneous tissues, characterized by aberrant melanocyte proliferation, basal cell malignant transformation, and dysregulated activation of multiple oncogenic signaling cascades. Within the tumor microenvironment, impaired function of tumor‐associated immune cells and a perturbation of proinflammatory and anti‐inflammatory mediators establish an immunosuppressive milieu, thereby facilitating tumor progression and dissemination. Current investigations have established curcumin's potential therapeutic utility in managing skin cancer. A meta‐analysis of 40 studies encompassing 989 animal subjects revealed that curcumin significantly attenuated tumor volume and mass in melanoma models. Consequently, this section will investigate curcumin's potential therapeutic efficacy as demonstrated in in vitro and in vivo investigations of skin cancer.

5.2.3.1 In Vitro Studies

Szlasa et al. utilized curcumin‐mediated photodynamic therapy on A375 and C32 cell lines, observing a reduction in cell viability to 8.98–12.42% at 50 µM and 48 h, accompanied by a significant increase in apoptotic and necrotic cells. However, this approach lacked selectivity for normal skin cells [427]. In studies with a human cSCC cell line, Khan et al. demonstrated that curcumin modulates Skp2, CDKI, and KLF4 expression. Specifically, it inhibits Skp2, upregulates p21 and KLF4, and suppresses cell proliferation, colony formation, and spheroid formation through ROS‐mediated apoptotic pathways. Furthermore, curcumin enhances sensitivity to chemotherapeutic agents like cisplatin and doxorubicin [428]. Trochopoulos et al. [429] using CTCL cell lines, investigated the combination of micellar curcumin with erufosine. Their analysis of signaling pathways and antibacterial activity confirmed synergistic antitumor effects by inhibiting TWIST1 and Akt expression, reducing NF‐κB activation, inducing apoptosis, and regulating GSH levels. This combination also demonstrated enhanced anti‐Staphylococcus activity and inhibited biofilm formation.

5.2.3.2 In Vivo Studies

Fontes et al. [430] administered intraperitoneal curcumin (20 mg/kg) and disulfiram (60 mg/kg) to C57BL/6 mice bearing B16‐F10 melanoma. This combination regimen synergistically elevated mitochondrial superoxide and enhanced oxidative stress, resulting in significant tumor growth inhibition. Specifically, the combination group achieved a 28.8% tumor inhibition rate, surpassing monotherapy groups (17.0–19.8%). The regimen also effectively induced apoptosis and DNA fragmentation. In a separate study on a subcutaneous B16‐F10 melanoma mouse model, Ricken et al. combined curcumin and silver nanoparticles within DPPC–F127 liposomes and applied photodynamic therapy. This drug delivery system significantly inhibited tumor growth and improved survival in tumor‐bearing mice by augmenting singlet oxygen production [431]. Malathi et al. [432] investigated TPGS‐stabilized PLGA nanopatterned films loaded with curcumin in a DMBA/castor oil‐induced squamous cell carcinoma model in Swiss albino mice. The study demonstrated that these nanopatterned films inhibited skin tumor progression (mechanism unspecified), and in vitro assays showed potent cytotoxic activity against the A431 human squamous cell carcinoma cell line.

5.3 Autoimmune Diseases

5.3.1 Rheumatoid Arthritis

Rheumatoid arthritis (RA) is a chronic autoimmune inflammatory condition defined predominantly by symmetrical polyarticular synovitis. Affecting approximately 0.5–1.0% of the global population, RA significantly contributes to joint deformity and functional disability. This condition disproportionately impacts women between the ages of 30 and 50 years, with a notable increase in risk among individuals with a familial predisposition. Initial RA symptoms include prolonged morning stiffness, joint swelling, and pain; delayed intervention can lead to joint erosion and irreversible functional deficits [433]. Diagnostic evaluations, including imaging and serological assays, may indicate joint space narrowing, bone erosion, and the presence of rheumatoid factor and anti‐CCP antibodies. During disease progression, M1 macrophages, Th17 cells, and activated fibroblast‐like synoviocytes infiltrate the synovium. These cells perpetuate inflammation by secreting mediators like TNF‐α, IL‐6, and MMPs, which promote synovial hyperplasia and cartilage degradation [434]. Current therapeutic strategies for RA primarily involve NSAIDs, DMARDs, biologics, and targeted synthetic agents, though prolonged treatment may increase infection susceptibility and lead to drug resistance [435]. Research has indicated that curcumin exhibits anti‐inflammatory, immunomodulatory, and chondroprotective properties in RA models through the modulation of signaling pathways such as NF‐κB and STAT3. This review will comprehensively detail the pharmacological actions and underlying mechanisms of curcumin in both in vitro and in vivo investigations of RA.

5.3.1.1 In Vitro Studies

Research by Yan et al. employing a RAW264.7 macrophage model demonstrated that the codelivery of prednisolone and curcumin via human serum albumin nanoparticles resulted in a synergistic suppression of proinflammatory factors and an elevation in the anti‐inflammatory factor IL‐10 [436]. In a separate study, Wang et al. [437] utilized RAW264.7 macrophages to show that ROS‐responsive, folate‐modified curcumin micelles specifically target and activate macrophages, consequently inhibiting inflammatory factor release and osteoclast differentiation. Chen et al. [438] working with primary rat articular chondrocytes, observed that curcumin mitigates IL‐1β‐induced chondrocyte apoptosis through autophagy activation and NF‐κB signaling pathway inhibition. Furthermore, He et al. [320] utilized the rabbit HIG‐82 synovial cell line and demonstrated that curcumin‐loaded exosomes derived from mesenchymal stem cells improve stability and cellular uptake. This approach synergistically inhibited the expression of antiapoptotic proteins IAP1/IAP2 and inflammatory factors, including IL‐6, TNF‐α, MMP1, and PGE2, thereby more effectively inducing synovial cell apoptosis. Collectively, these findings suggest that curcumin exerts anti‐RA effects in vitro by modulating inflammatory responses, apoptosis, and autophagy pathways via multiple molecular targets.

5.3.1.2 In Vivo Studies

Preclinical investigations have corroborated the therapeutic efficacy of curcumin in RA models. Hemshekhar et al. demonstrated in a collagen‐induced arthritis (CIA) mouse model that highly bioavailable curcumin attenuated cathelicidin (CRAMP) and calprotectin (S100A8/S100A9) levels within joint tissues. These reductions were found to correlate linearly with serum levels of TNF‐α, IFN‐γ, and MCP‐1, indicating curcumin's capacity to mitigate both localized and systemic inflammation [439]. In a CIA rat model, Sun et al. established that curcumin‐mediated photodynamic therapy induces ferroptosis in synovial fibroblasts via the downregulation of the Nrf2/xCT/GPX4 pathway, consequently ameliorating joint inflammation, synovial hyperplasia, and collagen deposition [440]. Furthermore, Wang et al. utilized a CIA rat model to show that ROS‐responsive folate‐modified curcumin micelles enhance drug circulation time, exhibit targeted accumulation in inflamed joints, and suppress serum inflammatory factors and cartilage degradation, thereby effectively controlling RA progression [437].

In summary, the therapeutic efficacy of curcumin in RA is principally mediated by the inhibition of synovial cell proliferation and the release of inflammatory cytokines. Additional mechanisms include the induction of autophagy and ferroptosis, modulation of signaling cascades (e.g., NF‐κB and Nrf2/xCT/GPX4), and augmentation of anti‐inflammatory cytokine IL‐10 expression. These observed effects, encompassing the targeting of inflamed joints, amelioration of cartilage degradation, and suppression of systemic inflammation, have been substantiated in vivo.

5.3.2 Systemic Lupus Erythematosus

SLE is a chronic autoimmune disorder marked by immune system dysregulation, affecting an estimated 3.4 million individuals globally. This condition is a primary driver of multiorgan damage and premature mortality [441], disproportionately impacting women of reproductive age and exhibiting higher incidence in non‐Hispanic Black populations compared with White populations. SLE presents with significant clinical heterogeneity, manifesting across multiple organ systems, including renal (proteinuria, hematuria, progressive renal dysfunction in lupus nephritis), cutaneous (malar rash), neurological, and cardiovascular systems, with neuropsychiatric lupus potentially causing cognitive deficits and seizures [442]. Pathogenically, aberrant Type I interferon pathway activation and autoreactive T and B cell dysfunction precipitate autoantibody formation and immune complex deposition. This subsequently activates complement and inflammatory pathways, leading to damage in renal, cutaneous, cardiovascular, and neuropsychiatric tissues. Current standard first‐line therapy with hydroxychloroquine effectively mitigates disease activity and mortality; for moderate to severe cases, combination therapy with immunosuppressants and biologics such as mycophenolate mofetil, azathioprine, cyclophosphamide, and belimumab may be employed [443]. Curcumin has demonstrated immunomodulatory and anti‐inflammatory properties relevant to SLE treatment. Consequently, this section will investigate curcumin's therapeutic potential in managing SLE.

5.3.2.1 In Vitro Studies

Zhao et al. demonstrated in murine renal podocyte cell line studies that curcumin substantially inhibits NLRP3 inflammasome activation and the resultant Caspase‐1/IL‐1β signaling pathway triggered by anti‐dsDNA antibodies. This inhibition serves to attenuate podocyte injury and ameliorate lupus nephritis [444]. Concurrently, Handono et al. [445] isolated CD4+ T cells from both SLE patients and healthy donors. Employing flow cytometry and ELISA, their findings indicated that low‐dose curcumin (0.1–1 µg/mL) specifically reduced the proportion of Th17 cells and IL‐17A secretion from SLE patient CD4+ T cells, while concurrently augmenting Treg cell proportion and TGF‐β1 expression. This modulation effectively rebalanced the Th17/Treg immune equilibrium. Notably, these effects were not observed in cells from healthy controls.

5.3.2.2 In Vivo Studies

In MRL/lpr and R848‐induced lupus mouse models, flow cytometry and RNA sequencing by Xu et al. demonstrated that curcumin administration promoted double‐negative T cell (DNT cell) apoptosis, restored T cell subset homeostasis, and subsequently improved serological and immunological parameters while ameliorating lupus nephritis‐associated pathological damage [446]. In the NZBWF1 lupus mouse model, Dent et al. observed via oral administration and renal function assessment that curcumin effectively reduced spleen weight, decreased glomerulosclerosis, and lowered blood urea nitrogen levels, maintaining stable body weight, thus attenuating autoimmune activity and renal injury [447].

In summary, the therapeutic efficacy of curcumin in SLE is principally mediated by the inhibition of NLRP3 inflammasome activation and the resultant Caspase‐1/IL‐1β signaling cascade. Furthermore, curcumin modulates the Th17/Treg immune cell equilibrium and induces apoptosis in double‐negative T‐cells. These mechanisms, supported by observed enhancements in serological and immunological indices and a reduction in renal pathology and autoimmune phenomena, have been substantiated in preclinical in vivo studies.

5.3.3 Psoriasis

Psoriasis is characterized as a chronic, inflammatory dermatosis, etiologically linked to a confluence of genetic predispositions and environmental triggers. Affecting an estimated 2–3% of the global population, it constitutes a significant contributor to epidermal barrier impairment and arthropathy [448]. The disease typically presents with bimodal peaks in incidence, observed most prominently in individuals between 15–35 and 50–60 years of age, with notable interethnic variability. Clinically, it manifests as well‐demarcated erythematous plaques adorned with silvery‐white scales, predominantly localized to the scalp, elbows, knees, and lumbosacral area. In more severe presentations, the development of pustules, erythroderma, and psoriatic arthritis can complicate timely diagnosis [449]. Diagnostic modalities including dermoscopy and histopathological analysis reveal epidermal dyskeratosis, acanthosis, and elongation of dermal papillae, alongside associations with susceptibility loci such as HLA‐C and dysregulation within the IL‐23/Th17 axis. Disease pathogenesis involves a positive feedback cascade among DCs, Th17 lymphocytes, and keratinocytes, promoting hyperkeratinization and sustaining an inflammatory milieu. Current therapeutic strategies for mild psoriasis predominantly employ topical corticosteroids, vitamin D analogs, and novel nonsteroidal agents. For moderate‐to‐severe disease, systemic agents such as methotrexate, cyclosporine, apremilast, or biologic therapies targeting TNF‐α, IL‐17, or IL‐23 may be indicated [450]. Curcumin possesses demonstrated anti‐inflammatory, antioxidant, and immunomodulatory properties relevant to psoriasis management. Accordingly, this section will delineate the potential therapeutic utility of curcumin in the treatment of psoriasis.

5.3.3.1 In Vitro Studies

In studies utilizing THP‐1 macrophages and NCTC‐2544 keratinocytes, Serini et al. demonstrated via ELISA and cell viability assays that curcumin–linolenic acid SLNs effectively suppress imiquimod (IMQ)‐induced inflammatory factor expression, keratinocyte proliferation, apoptosis, and ferroptosis markers, ameliorating psoriasis‐like inflammation [451]. Xu et al. [452] employing HaCaT keratinocytes and macrophage cell lines, confirmed through cell viability assays, inflammatory cytokine, and histological staining that curcumin nanocomposites reduce TNF‐α, IL‐17, IL‐22, and IL‐23 expression, thereby mitigating keratinocyte hyperproliferation, inflammatory responses, and improving psoriasiform lesions. Furthermore, Niu et al. [453] utilizing an M5‐stimulated HaCaT keratinocyte model, curcumin improves psoriasiform lesions by inhibiting the PI3K–Akt signaling pathway, consequently downregulating excessive keratinocyte proliferation and inflammatory cytokine expression.

5.3.3.2 In Vivo Studies

In a murine model of psoriasis, Wu et al. established a micromot‐induced psoriatic mouse model and utilized histopathology and ELISA to show that Cur–TPP, a mitochondria‐targeted curcumin derivative, effectively suppressed epidermal hyperplasia, reduced Ki67‐positive cell counts, and downregulated IL‐17 and TNF‐α levels, thereby ameliorating psoriasiform inflammation [454]. Zhang et al. employed a psoriatic mouse model and supercritical solvent‐free fluidized‐bed coating technology to formulate curcumin–POPG phospholipid complex‐coated sponge bone needles. Their findings indicated that these needles effectively penetrated the stratum corneum, released nanoparticles to inhibit DC activation, and induced regulatory T‐cell proliferation, consequently alleviating psoriasis symptoms [455]. Similarly, Kumar et al. utilized a psoriasis‐induced mouse model and methods including PASI scoring, ELISA, and tissue staining to observe that a curcumin solid‐lipid nanoparticle gel significantly downregulated IFN‐α, IL‐23, IL‐17, and TNF‐α levels and improved dermal tissue architecture, thus effectively mitigating psoriasiform inflammation [456].

5.3.4 Osteoarthritis

Osteoarthritis, the most prevalent chronic joint disease, exhibits a rising global incidence attributed to an aging demographic and increased obesity rates, resulting in significant joint pain and disability [457]. Predominantly affecting women over 50 years of age, knees, hips, and hands are the most commonly involved joints. Initial presentations are often subtle, characterized by pain associated with activity, transient morning stiffness, and functional impairments. With disease progression, joint effusion, deformity, and restricted range of motion may emerge, complicating early detection [458]. Diagnostic modalities, including imaging and pathological assessments, reveal hallmark findings such as joint space narrowing, osteophyte formation, subchondral sclerosis, and synovial inflammation. Advanced stages involve a vicious cycle of chondrocyte senescence, synovial inflammation, and subchondral bone remodeling, which exacerbates cartilage matrix degradation and joint destruction. Current therapeutic strategies encompass nonpharmacological interventions, oral analgesics (acetaminophen, NSAIDs), and intra‐articular injections; arthroplasty is reserved for severe presentations, though chronic medication use is associated with gastrointestinal and cardiovascular adverse events [459]. Curcumin demonstrates anti‐inflammatory, antioxidant, and chondroprotective properties relevant to osteoarthritis. Consequently, this section will investigate the therapeutic potential of curcumin in managing osteoarthritis.

5.3.4.1 In Vitro Studies

In studies utilizing IL‐1β‐induced primary rat chondrocytes and molecular docking, Xu et al. [460] determined that curcumin mitigates chondrocyte injury and decelerates osteoarthritis progression by upregulating SIRT5 expression and facilitating ACSL4 deacetylation, consequently inhibiting the ferroptosis pathway. Employing human chondrosarcoma SW‐1353 cells, Ghiselli et al. [461] leveraged molecular docking, cAMP quantification, and ROS detection to establish that turmeric resin ameliorates oxidative stress and inflammatory responses via activation of cannabinoid receptor 2 (CB2), inhibition of NF‐κB and ERK signaling, and downregulation of IL‐6 and COX‐2 expression, thereby improving osteoarthritis‐like lesions. Further investigation involving primary chondrocytes and an IL‐1β‐induced inflammatory model, revealed that curcumin targets the TRPM2/NLRP3 signaling axis, suppressing pyroptosis‐related protein expression and inflammatory factor secretion, thus alleviating chondrocyte damage [462].

5.3.4.2 In Vivo Studies

Hridayanka et al. established an osteoarthritis model in adult male Sprague–Dawley rats via intra‐articular injection of sodium iodoacetate into the knee joint. Employing histopathology and cell coculture, they observed that curcumin NE attenuated joint edema, suppressed cartilage degradation and synovial inflammation, and diminished macrophage infiltration, thereby ameliorating the osteoarthritis microenvironment [463]. Jhun et al. [464] employed a monosodium iodoacetate‐induced osteoarthritis model in Wistar rats. Behavioral assessments, histopathology, and micro‐computed tomography (micro‐CT) revealed that curcumin synergized with Omega‐3 fatty acids to alleviate pain, preserve cartilage architecture, downregulate MMP expression, and enhance mitochondrial function markers, consequently retarding osteoarthritis progression. Zheng et al. [465] utilized a C57BL/6 mouse model of osteoarthritis induced by medial meniscal instability. Through behavioral testing, micro‐CT, and histopathological analysis, they determined that the curcumin C3 complex, in conjunction with chondroitin sulfate and glucosamine, effectively maintained subchondral bone microstructural integrity, decreased osteoarthritis research society international (OARSI) histological scores, and improved locomotor function.

5.4 Cardiovascular Diseases

5.4.1 Heart Failure

Heart failure is the end stage of numerous cardiovascular diseases, characterized by impaired cardiac pumping function that fails to meet the metabolic demands of the body [466]. Based on left ventricular ejection fraction, heart failure can be classified into heart failure with reduced ejection fraction and heart failure with preserved ejection fraction [467]. Myocardial fibrosis, cardiomyocyte hypertrophy, energy metabolism disorders, and apoptosis are key pathological mechanisms in the progression of heart failure [468]. Curcumin and its derivatives regulate these pathological processes through multiple targets, demonstrating potential for the treatment of heart failure.

5.4.1.1 In Vitro Studies

Cardiomyocyte hypertrophy is an important feature of pathological cardiac remodeling in heart failure. Pyrazole curcumin reduces CDK9 kinase activity by disrupting the interaction between CDK9 and Cyclin T1, thereby indirectly inhibiting p300 HAT activity and RNA polymerase II transcriptional elongation, and suppressing phenylephrine induced cardiomyocyte hypertrophy at a lower concentration than curcumin [469]. Liposomes co loaded with SGLT2 inhibitors and curcumin, constructed using PVP coating and oleanolic acid to stabilize the lipid bilayer, exhibited sustained and delayed drug release properties in vitro, suggesting their potential as multifunctional targeted platforms for heart failure therapy [470]. Nicotinic acid–curcumin (NC) inhibited LPS induced inflammation and exosome secretion in HUVECs, regulated the levels of miR 142 3p, miR 126 3p and miR 143 3p in exosomes, and thereby protected vascular smooth muscle cell function [471].

5.4.1.2 In Vivo Studies

Curcumin and its derivatives have shown significant protective effects in animal models of heart failure. BDMC competitively inhibited the binding of TGF‐β to TGFBR1, blocked SMAD2/3 phosphorylation, suppressed cardiac fibroblast activation, reduced myocardial fibrosis, and improved cardiac function in mouse models of heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF) [472]. In a doxorubicin‐induced cardiotoxicity rat model, curcumin pretreatment upregulated apelin expression and downregulated NF‐κB mRNA expression in heart tissue, significantly ameliorated electrocardiographic QT interval prolongation, reduced cardiac troponin levels, and alleviated oxidative stress and histopathological damage, thereby exerting cardioprotective effects [473]. The novel curcumin analog SJ‐12 reduced the O‐GlcNAcylation level of SP1 by inhibiting OGT activity, thereby stabilizing SERCA2a and regulating calcium signaling pathways; it alleviated cardiac hypertrophy and fibrosis and improved cardiac function in a STZ‐induced diabetic cardiomyopathy mouse model, and the antifibrotic effect was absent in SERCA2a‐ or OGT‐silenced cardiomyocytes, indicating that SJ‐12 exerts cardioprotection by targeting the OGT/SP1/SERCA2a axis [474]. THC regulated the Nrf2–SIRT3 signaling pathway, reduced ROS generation, preserved mitochondrial function, and thereby attenuated postinfarction cardiac dysfunction, infarct size, myocardial fibrosis, cardiac hypertrophy, and cardiomyocyte apoptosis in mice; these protective effects were reversed in Nrf2‐ or SIRT3‐silenced cardiomyocytes [475]. The novel amorphous curcumin formulation ASD–Cur exhibited significantly higher bioavailability than Theracurmin in both rats and humans, and achieved comparable improvement in postinfarction heart failure at a lower dose [476]. In a Dahl salt‐sensitive rat model of hypertension‐induced left ventricular hypertrophy, curcumin inhibited p300 HAT activity, reduced GATA4 acetylation levels, and significantly attenuated left ventricular hypertrophy without affecting blood pressure or systolic function [477].

5.4.2 Atherosclerosis

Atherosclerosis is a chronic inflammatory vascular disease characterized by lipid deposition, inflammatory cell infiltration, foam cell formation, and plaque development [478]. Curcumin shows potential in the prevention and treatment of atherosclerosis through multiple mechanisms, including anti‐inflammatory, antioxidant, and lipid‐regulating effects.

5.4.2.1 In Vitro Studies

In vitro studies have shown that osteopontin targeted liposomes co delivering curcumin and perfluoro n pentane achieved acoustic cavitation induced rupture and rapid curcumin release upon exposure to low intensity focused ultrasound, significantly reduced IL 1α and TNF‐α levels in foam cells, induced apoptosis, and exerted anti‐inflammatory effects by regulating the TLR, NF‐κB, and MAPK3 signaling pathways [479]. Iron curcumin coordination polymers (Fe Cur CPs), constructed by coordinating curcumin with ferric ions, effectively scavenged excessive ROS in endothelial cells and macrophages, reduced monocyte adhesion, attenuated the production of inflammatory factors, and inhibited foam cell formation in vitro [254].

5.4.2.2 In Vivo Studies

In animal models of atherosclerosis, iron curcumin coordination polymers (Fe Cur CPs) administered intravenously to ApoE/mice significantly reduced plaque burden, suppressed macrophage aggregation, decreased ROS and inflammatory cytokine levels, enhanced collagen deposition and α SMA expression, and downregulated MMP9, thereby synergistically stabilizing atherosclerotic plaques [480]. BDMC promoted autophagy by inhibiting the PDK1/Akt/mTOR signaling pathway in vitro, reduced lipid accumulation in ox‐LDL stimulated vascular smooth muscle cells, and alleviated inflammation and lipid accumulation in ApoE/mice in vivo, thus exerting anti atherosclerotic effects [481]. The novel curcumin analog CACN136 regulated iNOS and Arg‐1 expression in LPS induced RAW264.7 cells, reduced cholesterol levels in ox‐LDL induced foam cells, and modulated ABCA1/CD36/SRA1 expression in vitro; in ApoE/mice, it significantly ameliorated aortic plaques and reduced lipid and inflammatory levels through regulating macrophage polarization and lipid metabolism, with superior safety and efficacy compared with the same dose of simvastatin [482]. Sparstolonin B (SSNB) and curcumin modulated Th17 stromal cell interactions via the IL‐17RA/TAK1/NF‐κB pathway; SSNB primarily targeted TLR4/CD36 to inhibit DC activation, Th17 differentiation, and vascular smooth muscle cell inflammation, and inhibited TAK1 phosphorylation, whereas curcumin more markedly suppressed macrophage inflammation and directly inhibited NF κB p65 phosphorylation; together, they reduced aortic plaque lesions and inflammatory infiltration in ApoE/mice [483]. Cadmium exposure exacerbated atherosclerotic plaque formation in ApoE /mice by inducing gut dysbiosis, increasing trimethylamine N oxide (TMAO) synthesis, and promoting M1 type macrophage polarization; curcumin gavage reversed the cadmium‐induced pathological progression by restoring the gut microbiota [484]. Curcumin promoted TFEB nuclear translocation, blocked P300‐mediated BRD4‐dependent super enhancer formation and liquid–liquid phase separation, thereby restoring autophagy, optimizing lipid catabolism, and inhibiting inflammation in ox‐LDL treated foam cells; the key role of the TFEB P300 BRD4 axis was validated in ApoE/mice via bone marrow transplantation [485]. In a mouse model of atherosclerosis complicated by UC, curcumin inhibited the TLR4/PAD4 signaling pathway, reduced the formation of macrophage extracellular traps (METs), decreased the levels of inflammatory cytokines and the collagen degrading enzyme MMP9, thereby reducing necrotic core size, increasing collagen content, and significantly stabilizing vulnerable plaques [486].

5.4.3 Atrial Fibrillation

Atrial fibrillation is the most common sustained cardiac arrhythmia, characterized by disordered atrial electrical activity and rapid, irregular excitation. Atrial fibrosis is a key pathological basis for the development and maintenance of atrial fibrillation [487]. Curcumin shows promise in the prevention and treatment of atrial fibrillation through its antifibrotic effects.

5.4.3.1 In Vitro Studies

In vitro studies have shown that curcumin inhibits the activation, proliferation, and differentiation of cardiac fibroblasts into myofibroblasts, reduces collagen synthesis, and thereby suppresses the progression of atrial fibrosis. In porcine primary hepatocytes, coadministration of curcumin with warfarin downregulated warfarin‐induced CYP3A protein expression and inhibited the enzymatic activities of CYP1A2 and CYP3A, indicating a food–drug interaction between curcumin and warfarin [488].

5.4.3.2 In Vivo Studies

Curcumin exhibits antifibrotic and antiarrhythmic effects in animal models of atrial fibrillation. Curcumin reduced the expression of mature‐mt_tRNA‐Val‐TAC_CCA_end in the atria of aged mice, decreased ROS levels, inflammatory responses, and cellular senescence, thereby ameliorating age‐related atrial fibrosis and reducing atrial fibrillation susceptibility [489]. By integrating data mining and network pharmacology, TGFBR1 was identified as a key gene in valvular atrial fibrillation; molecular docking confirmed a good binding affinity between curcumin and TGFBR1, and curcumin significantly reduced myocardial fibrosis by modulating the TGFβ/Smad signaling pathway, thereby improving valvular atrial fibrillation [490]. Curcumin inhibited p300 expression, blocked the formation of the p53/Smad3 complex, reduced the senescence ratio of atrial fibroblasts, and attenuated atrial fibrosis and atrial fibrillation susceptibility in aged mice and elderly patients with atrial fibrillation [491]. Curcumin gavage significantly shortened the duration of atrial fibrillation, reduced left atrial fibrosis, and decreased serum levels of IL‐17A, IL‐1β, IL‐6, and TGF‐β1 in an Ach‐CaCl2‐induced rat model of atrial fibrillation; the mechanism was related to regulation of the IL‐17 signaling pathway and key genes such as Col1a1 and Fasn [492].

5.4.4 Hypertension

Hypertension is a clinical syndrome characterized by elevated systemic arterial pressure and is a major risk factor for cardiovascular diseases [493]. Curcumin exerts antihypertensive effects through multiple mechanisms, including improving vascular endothelial function, suppressing vascular inflammation, and regulating the gut–brain axis.

5.4.4.1 In Vitro Studies

In vitro experiments have shown that curcumin promotes endothelial nitric oxide production, improves vasodilation, and inhibits the abnormal proliferation and migration of vascular smooth muscle cells. Carvedilol and curcumin coloaded in Soluplus micelles achieved sustained and controlled release in vitro while preserving the antioxidant activity of curcumin [494]. Coadministration of curcumin with amlodipine produced a stronger vasorelaxant effect than amlodipine alone in isolated rat aortic rings [495]. Network pharmacology and molecular docking revealed that curcumin targets genes such as PPARG, NOS3, IL6, INS, and ADIPOQ, thereby regulating multiple signaling pathways and miRNAs involved in metabolic syndrome and its components, exerting therapeutic effects [496].

5.4.4.2 In Vivo Studies

Curcumin and its formulations exhibit antihypertensive and target organ protective effects in animal models of hypertension. The novel transdermal curcumin formulation VASCEPTOR improved vascular health in a rat model of hypertension and enhanced exercise tolerance, motor coordination, diastolic function, and reduced frailty and expression of certain age‐related markers in aged mice [497]. Curcumin inhibited PTP1B, DPP‐4, lipase, and α‐amylase activities, activated the insulin signaling pathway, and regulated glycogen metabolic enzymes, significantly reducing blood glucose, HbA1c, oxidative stress markers, and blood lipids in rats with high‐fat, high‐fructose diet‐induced obesity and Type 2 diabetes, while also reducing food intake by 21% [498]. Curcumin reduced blood pressure and scopolamine‐induced cognitive impairment in spontaneously hypertensive rats (SHR/Izm) by inhibiting the expression of ACE and AT1 receptor in brain tissue, decreasing angiotensin II levels, and increasing muscarinic acetylcholine receptor expression and acetylcholine content [499]. Curcumin reshaped the gut microbiota composition, increased plasma butyrate levels, and activated GPCR 43 (GPR43) in the hypothalamic paraventricular nucleus, thereby improving intestinal pathology and integrity, reducing neuroinflammation and oxidative stress, and lowering blood pressure in spontaneously hypertensive rats [500]. Curcumin prevented renal damage in an l‐NAME‐induced hypertensive rat model by reducing MMP‐2 and MMP‐9 activities, decreasing oxidative stress and ED1 expression, improving renal function and glomerular structure, and directly inhibiting MMP‐2 activity in vitro [501].

5.4.5 Cardiomyopathy

Cardiomyopathy refers to a group of diseases involving structural and functional abnormalities of the myocardium, with diabetic cardiomyopathy being a common type [502]. Curcumin exerts protective effects in the treatment of cardiomyopathy by inhibiting programmed cell death pathways such as pyroptosis and ferroptosis. A meta‐analysis of 32 animal studies showed that curcumin dose‐dependently improved cardiac function indices (LVEF, LVFS), cardiac injury markers, heart‐to‐body weight ratio, and blood glucose in animal models of diabetic cardiomyopathy through antioxidant, anti‐inflammatory, autophagy‐enhancing, and antiapoptotic mechanisms, with greater efficacy at doses >200 mg/kg [503].

5.4.5.1 In Vitro Studies

In a high glucose‐induced cardiomyocyte injury model, curcumin treatment alleviated cell damage, suppressed inflammasome activation and the expression of pyroptosis‐related proteins. Curcumin activated SIRT1, upregulated the expression of mitochondrial biogenesis‐related genes including Pgc1α, Tfam, and Nrf2, and inhibited Drp1 translocation to mitochondria, thereby improving mitochondrial morphology and function; it reduced oxidative stress and cardiac dysfunction in LPS‐stimulated HL‐1 cells and in a mouse model of sepsis [504]. The novel curcumin analog SJ‐12 reduced the O‐GlcNAcylation level of SP1 by inhibiting OGT activity, thereby stabilizing SERCA2a; it alleviated cardiac hypertrophy and fibrosis and improved cardiac function in an STZ‐induced diabetic cardiomyopathy mouse model, and the antifibrotic effect was absent in SERCA2a or OGT‐silenced cardiomyocytes [505]. Curcumin promoted Nrf2 nuclear translocation, increased the expression of HO‐1 and Gpx4, reduced lipid peroxide accumulation, inhibited high glucose‐induced ferroptosis in cardiomyocytes, and alleviated myocardial fibrosis and cellular disarray in a diabetic rabbit model [505]. The novel curcumin derivative JM‐2 inhibited NF‐κB activation, alleviated cardiac functional and structural impairment, inflammation and fibrosis in STZ‐induced Type 1 diabetes and HFD‐induced Type 2 diabetes mouse models, and suppressed high glucose‐induced cardiomyocyte hypertrophy and fibrosis in H9c2 cells [506]. Curcumin activated the Nrf2/HO‐1 signaling pathway, reduced ROS generation, increased antioxidant enzyme activity, and inhibited cardiomyocyte apoptosis in high glucose‐treated H9c2 cells and in a high‐fat diet plus STZ‐induced Type 2 diabetic rat model, thereby exerting cardioprotective effects [507].

5.4.5.2 In Vivo Studies

Curcumin and its derivatives exhibit significant cardioprotective effects in animal models of cardiomyopathy. Curcumin pretreatment upregulated apelin expression and downregulated NF‐κB mRNA expression in cardiac tissue, significantly ameliorating doxorubicin‐induced QT interval prolongation, cardiac troponin elevation, oxidative stress, and histopathological damage in rats [473]. Network pharmacology and molecular docking identified AKT1, Bad, and CASP3 as potential targets of curcumin in heatstroke‐induced myocardial injury; in a heatstroke mouse model, curcumin activated the Akt/Bad/Caspase‐3 pathway and alleviated cardiomyocyte edema and mitochondrial swelling [508].Curcumin inhibited NLRP3‐mediated pyroptosis via the AKT/Nrf2/ARE pathway, thereby ameliorating diabetic cardiomyopathy [192]. In a rat model of diabetic cardiomyopathy, both 4 weeks of high‐intensity interval training (HIIT) and curcumin supplementation improved the expression of autophagy‐related genes in cardiomyocytes, with HIIT being more effective than curcumin [509]. Using GEO microarray data and WGCNA analysis, TLR1 was identified as a key target; curcumin downregulated TLR1, inhibited NF‐κB phosphorylation, reduced inflammation and improved cardiac function in a rat model of sepsis. THC inhibited JNK/ERK phosphorylation, reduced proinflammatory cytokine release, activated Nrf2 and other antioxidant proteins, downregulated gp91phox, and modulated the Bcl‐2/Bax/Caspase‐3 pathway, thereby alleviating oxidative stress, inflammation and cardiomyocyte apoptosis in LPS‐induced septic mice and H9c2 cells [510]. Molecular docking identified curcumin as a potent CB2 cannabinoid receptor agonist; in a mouse model of diabetes combined with isoproterenol‐induced myocardial infarction, curcumin improved hemodynamics, lipid profile, antioxidant capacity and inflammatory factors via CB2R activation, restoring cardiac function, while the CB2R antagonist AM630 attenuated these protective effects [511]. THC upregulated MKP‐1, inhibited JNK/ERK phosphorylation, reduced proinflammatory cytokine release, activated Nrf2 and other antioxidant proteins, downregulated gp91phox, and modulated the Bcl‐2/Bax/Caspase‐3 pathway, thereby alleviating oxidative stress, inflammation, and cardiomyocyte apoptosis in LPS‐induced septic mice and H9c2 cells, and improving cardiac function [512].

5.5 Inflammatory Conditions

5.5.1 Enteritis

5.5.1.1 In Vitro Studies

In a hydrogen peroxide‐induced oxidative damage model in Caco‐2 cells, pretreatment with the curcumin–glycyrrhizic acid self‐assembled nanoparticle‐based inulin hydrogel (CURG@IN) significantly increased cell survival from 54.2 to 89.7%, while reducing ROS levels by 68.5% [513]. In the RAW264.7 macrophage study, CURG@IN downregulated the expression of the M1 marker iNOS, which is induced by LPS, by 72.3%, while upregulating the expression of the anti‐inflammatory M2 marker Arg‐1 by 3.1‐fold [514]. A recent study utilized single‐cell RNA sequencing to investigate in depth the regulation of PANoptosis (pan‐apoptosis) in intestinal epithelial cells by curcumin. The study found that LPS stimulation significantly upregulated the expression of pan‐apoptosis‐related markers (ASC, NLRP3, NLRP12, and Caspase‐8) in Caco‐2 cells. Meanwhile, curcumin–CS microspheres (CCM) at concentrations of 20, 40, and 80 µM effectively inhibited LPS‐induced cell death in Caco‐2 cells while significantly reducing the levels of inflammatory cytokines such as IL‐18 and IL‐1β [515].

5.5.1.2 In Vivo Studies

In a mouse model of DSS‐induced colitis, the Disease Activity Index (DAI) in the CURG@IN treatment group decreased from 7.8 to 2.1, and the degree of colonic shortening improved by 64.7% compared with the control group [513]. In another study, the combination of curcumin and fecal microbiota transplantation significantly improved colonic histopathological damage in UC‐model mice. Compared with the control group, levels of the proinflammatory cytokines IL‐1β and TNF‐α in the colonic tissue of mice in the combination group decreased by 64.3 and 61.5%, respectively, while levels of the anti‐inflammatory cytokines IL‐4 and IL‐10 increased by 2.1‐fold and 1.8‐fold, respectively. At the same time, the expression levels of tight junction proteins occludin and ZO‐1 were restored by 58.3 and 52.6%, respectively [516]. In a DSS‐induced mouse colitis model, studies have shown that curcumin not only alleviates inflammation but also significantly improves abnormal intestinal angiogenesis by inhibiting the VEGF‐mediated JAK2/STAT3 signaling pathway. Furthermore, it promotes the expansion of Tregs, shifting the immune microenvironment from a Th1/Th17‐driven proinflammatory state to an anti‐inflammatory state [517].

5.5.2 Gastritis

5.5.2.1 In Vitro Studies

Mechanistic studies have revealed that the oxidation products of curcumin can specifically bind to VacA (vacuolating toxin A), a key virulence factor of Helicobacter pylori, thereby directly inhibiting its ability to induce vacuolation in gastric mucosal epithelial cells [518]. A study systematically evaluated the anti‐Helicobacter pylori activity of curcumin extract (CLE). The results showed that CLE completely inhibited the growth of the Helicobacter pylori SS1 strain in a dose‐dependent manner and exhibited significant synergistic effects when used in combination with amoxicillin [519]. In addition, curcumin derivatives have also demonstrated significant potential; the copper and zinc metal complexes of diacetylcurcumin (DAC2‐Cu and DAC2‐Zn) have shown strong anti‐Helicobacter pylori activity, specifically inhibiting the growth of this bacterium and its urease activity [520].

5.5.2.2 In Vivo Studies

Animal model studies have shown that curcumin can inhibit H. pylori virulence and reduce levels of inflammatory cytokines and oxidative stress [518, 521]. In a mouse model of Helicobacter pylori infection, the extent of gastric mucosal inflammation was significantly reduced in the curcumin‐treated group. Compared with the infected group, levels of the proinflammatory cytokines IL‐1β and TNF‐α in the serum of mice in the curcumin group decreased by 46.2 and 52.8%, respectively, levels of the oxidative stress marker MDA decreased by 41.3%, while the activities of SOD and CAT increased by 38.7 and 45.6%, respectively [522]. In a mouse model of H. pylori infection, curcumin not only significantly reduced proinflammatory factors, but more importantly, in long‐term administration studies, it demonstrated strong potential to prevent the malignant progression of chronic atrophic gastritis to gastric cancer (as a chemopreventive agent) by modulating the host's innate and adaptive immune responses, without causing significant histological toxicity [523].

5.5.3 Dermatitis

5.5.3.1 In Vitro Studies

Treatment of human immortalized keratinocytes (HaCaT) with a novel “curcumin–ionic liquid–liposome (Cur–Bet–IL–Lip)” compound was found to significantly downregulate the expression of IL‐17A, IL‐17F, and IL‐22, while promoting the synthesis of Collagen I in the dermis, with a cumulative transdermal penetration rate reaching an impressive 49% [288]. To address the challenge of skin penetration faced by natural compounds such as curcumin in the treatment of psoriasis, researchers developed a nanostructured lipid carrier (NLC) coencapsulating curcumin and sesame oil. This NLC has a particle size of approximately 132.4 nm and an encapsulation efficiency as high as 93%, enabling 24‐h sustained release. Furthermore, MTT assays confirmed its good biocompatibility and the absence of toxic side effects on human fibroblasts [524].

5.5.3.2 In Vivo Studies

In a study of mitochondria‐targeted curcumin microneedles (Cur–TPP@Mil MNs), in a mouse model of IMQ‐induced psoriasis, epidermal thickness decreased significantly from 125 to 38 µm (a 70% reduction) after 10 days of treatment. Key inflammatory factors in the lesions, including IL‐17A, decreased by 60%, while TNF‐α and IL‐6 decreased by 42.1 and 53.8%, respectively [454]. This treatment increased the apoptosis rate of keratinocytes to 35.7%, while the number of cells positive for the proliferation marker Ki67 decreased by 61.3%. In addition, a nano‐composite hydrogel patch composed of natural derivatives possesses dual functions: chemically scavenging ROS and physically absorbing radiation, thereby mitigating radiation damage. At the same time, the patch exhibits strong ROS‐scavenging capacity and promotes M2 polarization of macrophages; and the high‐water contents of the GelMA hydrogel enables it to physically absorb low‐energy X‐rays, thereby providing radiation shielding [525].

5.5.4 Pneumonia

5.5.4.1 In Vitro Studies

In an A549 alveolar epithelial cell model, pretreatment with curcumin (20 µmol/L) effectively reversed LPS‐induced glycocalyx damage, restored heparan sulfate (HS) expression, and inhibited the excessive activation of HS protease (HPSE) [526]. In RAW264.7 macrophages, curcumin (5–20 µmol/L) alleviates LPS‐induced damage by modulating MMP and the TLR4/NF‐κB signaling pathway [527]. Curcumin inhibits the maturation of immature DCs derived from the bone marrow and reduces the expression of their costimulatory molecules (CD80/CD86), thereby cutting off the amplification of inflammation at the antigen‐presentation stage [528].

5.5.4.2 In Vivo Studies

In a mouse model of LPS‐induced acute lung injury (ALI), treatment with curcumin (200 mg/kg) significantly reduced pulmonary edema and inflammatory infiltration. Compared with the control group, mice in the curcumin group exhibited significantly lower total cell counts, neutrophil counts, and protein concentrations in bronchoalveolar lavage fluid (BALF), along with a marked decrease in myeloperoxidase (MPO) activity and MDA levels in lung tissue [526]. In a mouse model of COPD, following inhalation of PEG‐modified curcumin SLNs (PEG–Cur–FL), the concentrations of the proinflammatory cytokines IL‐1β and IL‐6 in BALF decreased by 54.2 and 63.1%, respectively, while the expression of the protective receptor ACE2 was upregulated [529]. Curcumin can also influence the morphology and function of DCs by activating the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway, thereby alleviating asthma symptoms and inflammatory responses [530]. In an allergen‐induced asthma/airway remodeling mouse model, curcumin specifically activates the Wnt/β‐catenin signaling pathway. This not only alters the morphology and function of airway DCs but also significantly alleviates airway hyperresponsiveness (AHR) and eosinophil infiltration in the lungs.

6 Clinical Trials

In both in vitro and in vivo preclinical studies, curcumin has demonstrated promising therapeutic effects in neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. To assess the translational potential of curcumin during the progression of these conditions, recent advancements in its clinical application in this context are summarized, aiming to clarify its therapeutic value in the field.

6.1 Neurological Disorders

Hizomi et al. reported three cases of patients with AD who were administered oral turmeric powder capsules over 12 weeks. Following treatment, all patients demonstrated significant improvements in cognitive performance, as measured by the Mini‐Mental State Examination Assessment Scale, along with the alleviation of negative emotions based on the NPI‐Q Subscale. These outcomes contributed to improvements in quality of life and activities of daily living [531]. In a randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial, 40 patients with AD, aged 50–84 years, were randomly allocated to either a curcumin capsule (90 mg) treatment group or a placebo group for a treatment duration of 18 months. The results showed that, compared with patients in the placebo group, those in the curcumin treatment group exhibited improvements in short‐term memory and cognitive abilities. FDDNP‐PET scan results indicated that curcumin reduced amyloid and tau protein accumulation in the amygdala and hypothalamus, suggesting that the treatment potentially prevents or delays the progression of neurodegeneration and mitigates symptoms at AD onset [532].

The efficacy of combined treatment with curcumin extract and saffron was evaluated in a 12‐week randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled study. Compared with the placebo, the combination treatment significantly improved depressive symptoms and significantly reduced the STAI and IDS‐SR 30 scores of patients [533]. In another trial, 108 patients with depression were administered 1000 mg of curcumin capsules daily for 6 weeks. Patients receiving curcumin had significantly lower HDRS‐17 and MADRS scores than the controls. Moreover, compared with the placebo treatment, curcumin supplementation significantly lowered the levels of the proinflammatory cytokines IL‐1β and TNF‐α, reduced cortisol secretion, and increased plasma BDNF contents. These findings provided novel insights into the therapeutic potential of curcumin for depression [534]. Panahi et al. conducted a double‐blind, randomized controlled study to evaluate the efficacy of curcumin in patients with major depressive disorder. A total of 111 subjects were randomized into a curcumin–piperine treatment group and a control group, with both groups receiving their respective treatments for 6 weeks. Compared with the control group, the curcumin–piperine group demonstrated significantly lower scores on the HADS and the BDI‐II [535]. Furthermore, in a randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled clinical study, the therapeutic effect of curcumin was investigated in 56 patients with depression. Following 8 weeks of continuous treatment, assessments based on the IDS‐SR 30 and the STAI indicated that curcumin was more effective than the placebo in improving mood‐related symptoms [536].

In a randomized controlled clinical trial, 66 patients with ischemic stroke were randomly assigned to receive either curcumin–piperine tablets or placebo tablets for a total of 12 weeks. At the conclusion of treatment, compared with the placebo, curcumin–piperine supplementation effectively reduced hs‐CRP, TC, and TG, accompanied by a decline in diastolic and systolic blood pressure. Additionally, this treatment enhanced total antioxidant capacity (TAC), contributing to improved health status in patients with ischemic stroke [537].

Donadio et al. investigated the effects of curcumin on the misfolding and accumulation of α‐Syn protein in skin biopsies from patients with PD who were administered a curcumin–phospholipid complex for 12 consecutive months. Patients receiving curcumin treatment exhibited reduced scores on the COMPASS‐31 and NMSS questionnaires, along with decreased α‐Syn accumulation. These findings suggest that the curcumin–phospholipid complex effectively improved clinical parameters by crossing the blood–brain barrier, and also reduced α‐Syn accumulation in the skin of PD patients [538]. In a 9‐month randomized, triple‐blind, placebo‐controlled experiment, 60 patients with PD aged over 30 years of age were randomly assigned to either a curcumin treatment group (n = 30) or a placebo group (n = 30). After 90 days of curcumin treatment (80 mg/day), scores on the MDS‐UPDRS were significantly decreased relative to those of the placebo controls, suggesting that the quality of life of the patients had significantly improved [539].

Dolati et al. investigated the effects of nanocurcumin on the regulation of abnormal miRNA expression profiles, transcription factors, and proinflammatory factors in peripheral blood mononuclear cells of patients with relapsing‐remitting MS. The results showed that after 6 months of oral nanocurcumin administration, the expression levels of miRNA‐145, miRNA‐132, and miRNA‐16 were reduced, alongside decreases in the secretion levels of IL‐6, IL‐1β, INF‐γ, and CCL‐5. Overall, compared with the placebo, the group supplemented with nanocurcumin showed significant improvements in Expanded Disability Status Scale scores [540]. In another 12‐month double‐blind, randomized, placebo‐controlled trial, 54 ALS patients were allocated in a 1:1 ratio to either a nanocurcumin group (n = 27) or a placebo group (n = 27), receiving daily doses of 80 mg of nanocurcumin or a placebo. Nanocurcumin was found to be well‐tolerated and improved survival probability, particularly for patients with poor prognoses exhibiting bulbar symptoms, thus establishing it as an effective adjunctive therapeutic agent for ALS patients [541]. In a separate study, 42 ALS patients were treated with 600 mg of oral curcumin and coenzyme Q10 for 6 months, and their Revised ALS Functional Rating Scale (ALS‐FRS‐r) scores remained stable, suggesting that curcumin exerts a protective effect on respiratory function. Additionally, significant reductions in advanced oxidation protein products (AOPPs) and lactate levels were noted, indicating that curcumin may alleviate oxidative stress and improve aerobic metabolism, thereby highlighting its potential therapeutic value for ALS [542]. Julio et al. randomly assigned selected ALS patients to either an intervention group receiving oral liposomal curcumin combined with dutasteride for 2 months or a control group receiving placebo treatment. They reported that, compared with the control group, patients in the intervention group experienced significant muscle bundle stabilization and improvements in muscle capability, which subsequently enhanced their quality of life [543].

6.2 Cancer

Clinical investigations into curcumin for colorectal cancer have largely concentrated on its potential as an adjuvant therapy and chemopreventive agent; however, existing data indicate considerable uncertainty regarding its therapeutic effectiveness. In the context of adjuvant chemotherapy, a 2025 randomized controlled trial (RCT) assessed the efficacy of an oral nano‐curcumin formulation in patients with metastatic colorectal cancer. This trial involved 94 patients undergoing XELOX/FOLFOX bevacizumab chemotherapy, who were randomized to receive either nano‐curcumin (40 mg thrice daily) or placebo for six cycles. The study found no statistically significant differences between the nano‐curcumin and placebo groups in carcinoembryonic antigen levels or RECIST‐defined imaging response rates, implying that nano‐curcumin did not substantially enhance treatment outcomes for metastatic colorectal cancer at the administered dosage [544]. However, the clinical application of nanocurcumin in metastatic colorectal cancer remains in the exploratory stage, with limited sample size and insufficient supporting data. Furthermore, variations in curcumin dosage, formulation types, and therapeutic strategies may affect its absorption efficiency, bioavailability, and distribution, resulting in a lack of statistically significant effects. Therefore, future studies should include larger sample size and optimize dosing regimens to further clarify the clinical value of curcumin in colorectal cancer treatment. Concerning the concurrent use of preoperative chemoradiotherapy, a Phase II randomized double‐blind trial (NCT00745134) targeting locally advanced rectal cancer investigated curcumin's efficacy when combined with preoperative chemoradiotherapy. Twenty‐two patients were allocated to receive either curcumin (4 g twice daily) or placebo throughout the chemoradiotherapy course and for 6 weeks postoperatively. The findings indicated that the pathological complete response rate in the curcumin arm was not significantly greater than in the placebo arm (p = 0.18). Furthermore, pharmacokinetic analyses demonstrated substantial interindividual variability in curcumin plasma concentrations (ranging from 8.8 to 258.1% of baseline 1 h postadministration) and highly variable tissue concentrations (0.1–4765.7 ng/mg tissue). This unpredictable bioavailability represents a critical obstacle to its therapeutic efficacy [545]. Within the scope of chemoprevention, curcumin has primarily been examined for its role in preventing colorectal adenomas, which are precancerous lesions. Separately, Tolentino et al. developed a CS bioadhesive film loaded with curcumin to assess its safety and efficacy for topical skin cancer treatment. This evaluation encompassed in vitro release kinetics, skin penetration studies, cell viability assays, and irritation testing. The developed film demonstrated robust physical stability at ambient temperatures for 90 days and controlled drug release, with 51.6–65.6% released within 8 h. Curcumin penetration into the skin's stratum corneum and deeper tissues was significantly enhanced, reaching 1.3–1.9 and 1.7–2.7 µg/cm2, respectively. In metastatic melanoma MeWo cells, curcumin monotherapy exhibited greater inhibition of cell viability compared with radiotherapy (4–12 Gy) (IC50 = 24.4 µg/mL); however, no synergistic effect was observed with their combined administration. Furthermore, the film demonstrated a lack of irritancy in the chicken embryo chorioallantoic membrane assay, and CS exhibited no cytotoxicity against HaCaT keratinocytes. Collectively, this CS film represents a safe and stable topical delivery system for curcumin, showing significant potential for skin cancer therapeutics [546].

6.3 Autoimmune Diseases

A randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial comprising 48 women diagnosed with RA investigated the effects of a daily 500 mg curcumin supplement administered over 8 weeks. The curcumin cohort demonstrated a statistically significant augmentation in serum TAC, a concomitant reduction in MDA concentrations, and diminished Disease Activity Score‐28 (DAS‐28) scores. Furthermore, notable improvements were observed in joint tenderness, swollen joint count, and pain intensity, collectively suggesting that curcumin can potentiate antioxidant defenses and attenuate disease activity [547]. Conversely, a meta‐analysis incorporating seven RCTs involving 314 patients revealed that curcumin and its derivatives did not yield significant improvements in DAS‐28, ESR, or CRP levels among individuals with RA and SLE, with standardized mean differences of −2.34, −1.49, and −1.65, respectively. Substantial heterogeneity was noted across all outcome measures, indicating that the anti‐inflammatory efficacy of curcumin in these conditions is likely constrained and variable [548]. A randomized, triple‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial enrolling 62 patients diagnosed with SLE demonstrated that a daily oral regimen of 1000 mg curcumin administered over 10 weeks resulted in a statistically significant reduction in anti‐double‐stranded DNA (anti‐dsDNA) and IL‐6 levels. Specifically, change scores for anti‐dsDNA were −9.30 in the curcumin group versus −2.55 in the placebo group (p = 0.018), and for IL‐6, they were −25.62 versus −7.34 (p = 0.043). These findings suggest that curcumin functions as a safe and efficacious adjunctive therapeutic agent, capable of ameliorating autoimmune activity and inflammatory status [549]. Furthermore, a meta‐analysis incorporating seven RCTs with a cumulative participant count of 314 patients assessed the impact of curcumin and turmeric extract on inflammatory markers in individuals with RA and SLE. While no significant intergroup differences were observed for DAS‐28, erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR), and CRP levels, substantial heterogeneity was noted across all outcome measures. This heterogeneity implies that the observed anti‐inflammatory effects were constrained and exhibited inconsistency [548]. A Phase I clinical investigation in five individuals with mild‐to‐moderate psoriasis demonstrated significant amelioration of erythema and scaling in skin lesions following a 4‐week regimen of topical curcumin–vesicle nanocarrier application (CUR 15 µM). Concurrently, a reduction in the expression of IL‐17, IL‐23, IL‐22, TNF‐α, S100A7, S100A12, and Ki67 was observed in lesional tissue. These findings suggest that the antipsoriatic efficacy of this nanoparticle formulation is mediated through the inhibition of the IL‐17/IL‐23 signaling pathway [550]. In an 8‐week randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial, 101 patients diagnosed with knee osteoarthritis were administered 500 mg of standardized curcumin extract (Curcugen) twice daily. The findings indicated that, in comparison with the placebo group, curcumin administration led to a significant reduction in KOOS knee pain scores and numerical pain scores. Furthermore, it enhanced performance on the timed up‐and‐go test and the 6‐min walk test, improved JOA total scores, and decreased the need for analgesics in 37% of participants. These outcomes suggest the potential efficacy of curcumin in alleviating osteoarthritis pain and enhancing joint function [551]. A separate 12‐week, three‐arm, randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial involved 201 patients with osteoarthritis who received either a curcumin complex (CuraMed) or a curcumin–boswellic acid complex (Curamin) orally, three times daily. The study revealed that both formulations resulted in improvements in joint pain and physical function. Notably, Curamin demonstrated superior performance compared with the placebo in both pain indices and physical performance tests, implying a synergistic interaction between curcumin and boswellic acid [552].

6.4 Cardiovascular Diseases

Clinical studies provide evidence for the antiatherosclerotic effects of curcumin. In a RCT involving 72 patients with Type 2 diabetes and an ASCVD risk score ≥5%, curcumin significantly reduced systolic and diastolic blood pressure, LDL‐C, TNF‐α, and MDA levels, increased HDL‐C, improved ASCVD risk classification, and was well tolerated, but had no significant effect on HbA1c, fasting blood glucose, total cholesterol, or triglycerides [553]. In a randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial involving 227 obese patients with Type 2 diabetes, 12 months of curcumin intervention significantly reduced pulse wave velocity, LDL‐C, small dense LDL‐C, high‐sensitivity CRP, IL‐1β, IL‐6, and TNF‐α levels, indicating that curcumin effectively reduces atherogenic risk in this population [554]. In a double‐blind, RCT involving 50 patients with stable coronary heart disease, curcumin (45 mg/day) administered from 7 days before to 2 days after percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) did not show a significant difference compared with placebo, but within‐group analysis showed that curcumin significantly reduced serum hs‐CRP (p = 0.006) and sCD40L (p = 0.002) levels 48 h after PCI [555]. In a 12‐week randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial involving 42 overweight or obese patients with coronary slow flow phenomenon, nano‐curcumin (80 mg/day) significantly improved all domains of the Seattle Angina Questionnaire score and reduced triglycerides, the triglyceride/HDL ratio, visceral adiposity index, and waist‐triglyceride index, but had no significant effect on endocan, adropin, or homocysteine levels [556].

Clinical studies have evaluated the preventive effect of curcumin on postoperative atrial fibrillation. In a randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial involving 80 patients undergoing coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery, postoperative oral administration of curcumin–piperine for 5 consecutive days significantly reduced CRP levels, increased TAC, and slightly reduced creatine kinase‐MB levels, but had no significant effect on Troponin I, lactate dehydrogenase, ejection fraction, or atrial fibrillation [557]. In a randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled trial involving 234 patients undergoing CABG surgery, perioperative administration of a nano‐curcumin formulation (240 mg/day) from 3 days before to 4 days after surgery did not reduce the incidence of postoperative atrial fibrillation, nor did it alter hs‐CRP, MDA, or GSH levels [558]. The findings were limited by the short treatment duration and the lack of evaluation of cytokine and transcription factor expression. Moreover, variations in sample size, curcumin dosage, formulation type, and bioavailability may influence the clinical efficacy. Therefore, more comprehensive studies with optimized designs are required in the future to further elucidate the therapeutic potential of curcumin.

Systematic reviews and meta‐analyses provide evidence for the blood pressure‐lowering effect of curcumin. A systematic review and meta‐analysis of 20 RCTs showed that nano‐curcumin supplementation significantly improved waist circumference, fasting blood glucose, HbA1c, QUICKI, total cholesterol, LDL‐C, HDL‐C, diastolic blood pressure, TAC, MDA, TNF‐α, IL‐6, and hs‐CRP in patients with metabolic syndrome [559]. In a 12‐week double‐blind, randomized clinical trial involving 104 infertile women with polycystic ovary syndrome, a DASH diet combined with curcumin (500 mg twice daily) significantly reduced insulin levels and insulin resistance, but had no significant effect on fasting blood glucose or insulin sensitivity [560].

6.5 Inflammatory Conditions

Several meta‐analyses have confirmed that curcumin, when used as an adjunctive therapy, can significantly improve clinical remission rates and endoscopic improvement rates in patients with UC. Furthermore, the combination of curcumin and piperine has been shown to improve body composition in patients with IBD [561]. A meta‐analysis published in 2025, which synthesized 13 placebo‐controlled randomized clinical trials, confirmed that curcumin, when used as an adjunctive therapy, significantly improves clinical remission and response rates in patients with UC, with no significant difference in adverse events compared with the placebo group [562]. As a novel local delivery strategy, the enema formulation, when used in combination with mesalazine suppositories in a 2025 randomized, open‐label trial of patients with distal UC, resulted in significantly higher clinical remission rates (60%) and endoscopic healing rates (53.3%) compared with the group treated with mesalazine alone (remission rate: 36.7%; healing rate: 30%) [37]. A 2025 RCT showed that daily oral administration of 1000 mg of curcumin combined with 10 mg of piperine for 12 weeks significantly improved mild‐to‐moderate inflammatory bowel disease and patients’ body composition, while increasing muscle mass [563]. An RCT on functional dyspepsia showed that curcumin is as effective as omeprazole in significantly reducing pain and bloating [564]. In addition, a Phase IIb clinical trial of Meriva (a curcumin–phospholipid complex) is investigating the role of curcumin in preventing gastric cancer in patients with chronic atrophic gastritis. A 2025 RCT involving ICU patients with sepsis (n = 66) demonstrated that the combination of curcumin and piperine significantly reduced inflammatory markers such as CRP and ESR within 7 days, and improved laboratory parameters such as bilirubin and hemoglobin [565]. Another study showed that curcumin nanomicelles can significantly reduce serum levels of IL‐17 and IL‐22, as well as SOFA and MODS scores, in patients with sepsis, suggesting their potential for modulating the immune response and improving organ function [566]. A 2025 triple‐blind RCT (n = 144) involving patients with mild COVID‐19 found that oral curcumin (500 mg per dose, three times daily) was similarly effective to ginger extract in significantly reducing CRP and ESR levels, confirming their value in regulating inflammation [567].

The accumulated evidence provides a basis for the clinical application of curcumin in the treatment of neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. Current delivery methods primarily involve oral administration, frequently as an adjunct to standard pharmacological therapies. To address issues regarding bioavailability, some studies have utilized nanoformulations. Curcumin has shown potential in alleviating disease symptoms, delaying disease progression, and improving survival rates, suggesting that it represents a promising candidate for enhancing treatment outcomes in neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. However, most research to date has focused on the use of curcumin as a therapeutic intervention rather than as a preventive agent. Notably, studies investigating its prophylactic potential remain limited. Nevertheless, in vitro studies involving curcumin pretreatment in cell models have demonstrated that it can delay occurrence of disease by reducing oxidative stress, decreasing proinflammatory factor release, and improving mitochondrial function. This evidence supports the potential use of curcumin as an early preventive intervention strategy for neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. warranting further in vivo and clinical studies to validate its efficacy as a preventive agent. Tables 3 summarizes the therapeutic effects observed in clinical trials.
