7 Conclusion and Prospects

Neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases, are regarded as complex multifactorial disorders. These impairments adversely affect health and quality of life. Increasing evidence supports that persistent neuroinflammation, oxidative stress, mitochondrial dysfunction, and progressive neuronal degeneration play critical roles in the onset and progression of these diseases. Activation of the TLR4 inflammasome can trigger inflammatory responses and induce the production of proinflammatory cytokines as well as other mediators involved in inflammatory activation [568].

Given the key role of mitochondria in the regulation of cell survival and death, these organelles are often considered potential therapeutic targets in a variety of diseases. In multiple diseases, oxidative stress mediated by mitochondrial dysfunction can impair the ability of cells to produce sufficient ATP to maintain homeostasis, ultimately leading to apoptosis and the accumulation of protein aggregates [569]. Furthermore, neuroplasticity is a fundamental characteristic of the nervous system. Following neuronal injury in the CNS, functional recovery is achieved through neuroplasticity, which facilitates the formation of new memories and learning processes [570]. Understanding these mechanisms provides crucial insights for targeted therapeutic strategies in the management of diseases.

Current clinical pharmacological strategies for neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases, primarily prioritize symptom management rather than addressing the underlying pathological mechanisms [571]. These conventional approaches are often associated with significant adverse effects and have limited efficacy in halting disease progression. Moreover, these therapies fail to modulate the key signaling pathways that are increasingly recognized as drivers of neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases. In contrast, extensive preclinical research has established that curcumin and its derivatives have the potential to serve as neuroprotective agents capable of targeting signaling pathways associated with neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases [572]. Mechanistic studies on neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases indicate that the therapeutic advantages of curcumin stem from its ability to modulate multiple signaling pathways, including the TLR4/MyD88/NF‐κB, JAK/STAT, and PI3K/AKT pathways, as well as Nrf2/ARE‐mediated antioxidant responses. Notably, curcumin can enhance neuroplasticity by modulating neuro‐signaling cascades, thus increasing dendritic spine density in the hippocampus, mediated through pathways such as BDNF/TrkB/CREB, cAMP/PKA, and Wnt/β‐catenin. Additionally, curcumin exhibits low toxicity, with long‐term usage eliciting significantly fewer side effects than traditional medications [7], and is characterized by a favorable safety profile.

Despite the considerable therapeutic potential of curcumin for neurological disorders, cardiovascular diseases, cancer, inflammatory conditions, and autoimmune diseases, significant challenges remain in translating discoveries from basic research to clinical applications, particularly those stemming from its low bioavailability, which greatly limits therapeutic efficacy. Nanotechnology‐based drug delivery systems offer strategies for enhancing curcumin bioavailability. Compared with free curcumin, nanoformulations demonstrate improved stability and biocompatibility, increased blood–brain barrier permeating ability, and significantly enhanced solubility, which together facilitate the specific delivery of curcumin to the brain. Owing to the numerous advantages of nanocarriers, nanoparticles have been widely employed in the development of curcumin nano‐delivery systems. Common carriers include PRO–POL complexes, PLGA, SLNs, and AuNPs, which significantly improve stability and enable controlled drug release. Furthermore, novel nanoformulations such as liposomes, NEs, polymeric micelles, and exosomes have shown marked efficacy in enhancing the pharmacokinetics of curcumin. These delivery strategies circumvent the limitations of the chemical properties of curcumin, potentially avoiding neurotoxicity and enhancing neuronal survival, thereby exhibiting more robust neuroprotective characteristics.

Supported by advancements in scientific technology, substantial progress has been made in curcumin research. Despite the extensive accumulation of in vitro, in vivo, and clinical data, clinical translation remains constrained by the limitations of human studies. For instance, many current clinical trials have inadequate sample sizes and short follow‐up durations, generating uncertainty regarding the long‐term efficacy of curcumin for patients with neurological disorders. During clinical application, potential drug–drug interactions resulting from the combination of curcumin with other medications have attracted increasing attention, particularly among patients receiving pharmacological treatments. For example, warfarin, a commonly used anticoagulant, has a narrow therapeutic window and its efficacy and safety can be influenced by alterations in drug metabolism and pharmacodynamic responses. Studies have suggested that curcumin may affect the pharmacokinetics of warfarin by modulating CYP450‐related metabolic pathways [573]. However, clinical studies have shown that the combined use of curcumin and warfarin does not result in reduced coagulation function, suggesting that the clinical significance of potential interactions between curcumin and conventional medications requires further investigation over longer periods [574]. Furthermore, in a randomized clinical trial, researchers evaluated the safety and efficacy of oral curcumin combined with conventional chemotherapeutic agents as an adjuvant therapy in cancer patients. The results demonstrated that, compared with patients receiving standard chemotherapy alone, those who received curcumin as an adjunctive treatment achieved higher response rates and improved clinical outcomes, suggesting that curcumin may exert synergistic therapeutic effects when combined with chemotherapeutic agents [575]. However, the safety of combining curcumin with different therapeutic agents requires further validation. Therefore, broader and better‐designed clinical trials are needed to determine the optimal dosing regimens and assess the long‐term safety of curcumin‐based therapies.
